<h1 align="center">Project Maya</h1>

<p align="center"><b>GLM-5.3-Flash (321 B), DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 (748 B) and MiniMax-M3.1-Flash (428 B) on your own GPU</b><br>
private and fast · one NVIDIA GPU or up to 16 · AMD and Windows · chat, pictures, OpenAI- and
Anthropic-compatible API · several requests at once</p>

<p align="center">
<a href="https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/GLM-5.3-Flash-Maya-GGUF"><img src="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mw00/project-maya/badges/glm.svg" alt="GLM-5.3-Flash Maya downloads"></a>
<a href="https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/GLM-5.3-Flash-Maya-M-Derisked-IQ2_S-GGUF"><img src="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mw00/project-maya/badges/glm-m-derisked.svg" alt="GLM-5.3-Flash Maya-M-Derisked downloads"></a>
<a href="https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-Maya-GGUF"><img src="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mw00/project-maya/badges/deepseek.svg" alt="DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Maya downloads"></a>
<a href="https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-GGUF"><img src="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mw00/project-maya/badges/minimax.svg" alt="MiniMax-M3.1-Flash Maya downloads"></a>
</p>

<p align="center">Created and led by <b>Peasant Smith</b> (<a href="https://github.com/mw00">@mw00</a>), co-maintained
with <b><a href="https://github.com/needmorevram">@needmorevram</a></b></p>

<p align="center"><a href="https://github.com/sponsors/mw00"><img src="https://img.shields.io/badge/Sponsor_on_GitHub-ea4aaa?logo=githubsponsors&logoColor=white&style=for-the-badge" alt="Sponsor on GitHub" height="40"></a>
<a href="https://buymeacoffee.com/peasantsmith"><img src="https://img.shields.io/badge/Buy_me_a_coffee-FFDD00?logo=buymeacoffee&logoColor=black&style=for-the-badge" alt="Buy Me A Coffee" height="32"></a><br>
<sub>Project Maya is free. If it runs well on your PC, sponsoring it pays for the test hardware and the work on it.</sub></p>

Models this size usually need a datacenter. Maya runs three of them on the hardware you already have:
**GLM-5.3-Flash**, **DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017** and **MiniMax-M3.1-Flash**. Each model has an engine built for it. It
keeps the experts your conversation uses most on the GPU, the next ones in RAM and the rest on your NVMe SSD, and moves
them as you chat. It measures your GPUs, CPU, RAM and SSD and tunes itself to them. Nothing leaves your machine.

- **Fast:** up to 149 tokens/s on two RTX 5090s and an RTX 4090, 124 on two H100s, 118 on four RTX 4090s, 81 on one
  RTX PRO 6000, 30+ on a single RTX 5090. [Real numbers from real users](#how-fast-is-it).
- **Close to the original:** Maya's own quants of GLM-5.3-Flash keep 97.7-99.2% of the FP8 model's zero-shot
  accuracy; DeepSeek V4.1 · Maya-S picks the same next token as the original 92.6% of the time, MiniMax M3.1 · Maya-S
  87.6% (the average over its categories).
- **Plugs into your tools:** OpenAI- and Anthropic-compatible API with tool calls, ready for coding agents.
- **Several requests at once:** up to 8 answered together with GLM-5.3-Flash, DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash and
  MiniMax-M3.1-Flash - for agents that run several sessions, or several people on one PC.
- **Everything in the box:** a chat and a live monitor in the browser, pictures on every model, thinking levels, a
  Models page that downloads, loads and deletes models, a Turbo button, a one-command setup.

## Quick start

```sh
git clone https://github.com/mw00/project-maya.git && cd project-maya
./setup.sh
```

The setup asks which model you want, downloads it, builds everything once and **starts the model**. Then open
`http://127.0.0.1:8080`. Windows: `START-MAYA.bat` instead of `./setup.sh`. More in [Install](#install).

## <a name="the-models"></a><a name="deepseek-v41-flash-1017-new"></a><a name="minimax-m31-flash-new"></a>Pick your model

Every Maya quant is named after its model, then its tier - XS, S, M or L, by how many bits its experts keep. The setup
recommends one for your PC; to pick one yourself, give its setup id: `./setup.sh --model glm-maya-s24`.

| Model | Maya quant · setup id | Size | Made for |
| --- | --- | ---: | --- |
| **[GLM-5.3-Flash](https://huggingface.co/zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash)**<br><sub>zai-org · MIT · 321 B, 18 B active · up to 1M context</sub> | **Maya-S** (default) · `glm-maya-s` | 96.5 GB | PCs with a smaller memory pool (RAM and VRAM together) |
| | Maya-S24 · `glm-maya-s24` | 94.7 GB | cards of 24 GB or less: ~1.5 GB more of the card for experts, decode +14% |
| | Maya-XS · `glm-maya-xs` | 73.4 GB | PCs with 64 GB of RAM and a 24 GB card, at 128K context |
| | Maya-M · `glm-maya-m` | 116 GB | a bigger memory pool; calibrated toward tool calls and front-end code |
| | Maya-M-Derisked · `glm-maya-m-derisked` | 116 GB | Maya-M with a weight edit by [Blackfrost_AI](https://x.com/Blackfrost_AI) that reduces blanket refusals; experimental |
| | Maya-L · `glm-maya-l` | 156.3 GB | the biggest memory pools; the closest to the original |
| **[DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017](https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017)**<br><sub>DeepSeek · MIT · 748 B</sub> | **Maya-S** (default) · `deepseek-maya-s` | 340 GB | any amount of memory: what VRAM and RAM don't hold is read from the SSD |
| | Maya-XS (v2) · `deepseek-maya-xs` | 277 GB | the whole model in a 128 GB PC's memory (the Engram tables stay on the SSD); v2 is about 12% closer to the original and about 20% faster at writing than v1 - running the setup again offers the update |
| **[MiniMax-M3.1-Flash](https://huggingface.co/MiniMaxAI/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash)**<br><sub>MiniMax · [MiniMax Community License](LICENSE.minimax-m31) · 428 B, 23 B active · up to 1M context</sub> | Maya-XS · `minimax-maya-xs` | 93 GB | the whole model in a 128 GB PC's memory |
| | Maya-S · `minimax-maya-s` | 122 GB | closer to the original |

All of them run on NVIDIA from the V100 / RTX 20 on, and on AMD and Windows (see
[What you need](#what-you-need)). DeepSeek's sizes include its Engram tables. The setup also offers unsloth's GGUF and
LibertAIDAI's EXL3 quants of DeepSeek, orcarouter's abliterated DeepSeek, Mia-AiLab's EXL3 quant of MiniMax,
turboderp's EXL3 quants of GLM, Qwen-3.8-Flash-Next (CYBER-FROST) as its Q5_K_M GGUF and Qwen-3.8-Flash-Next
itself as unsloth's UD-IQ2_XS GGUF, both with pictures
([its settings](docs/SETTINGS.md#qwen-38-flash-next-cyber-frost)). **Each model's files, drafters, pictures and options:
[docs/MODELS.md](docs/MODELS.md).**

## Quality

Maya's quants are made from each model's released weights and measured against the original:

- **GLM-5.3 · Maya quants** keep 97.7-99.2% of the FP8 model's zero-shot accuracy and pick its next token 83-90% of
  the time (Maya-L: 99.2%, 90%). How they're made and measured: [MAYA-S](bench/results/MAYA-S.md),
  [MAYA-S24](bench/results/MAYA-S24.md), [MAYA-M](bench/results/MAYA-M.md), [MAYA-L](bench/results/MAYA-L.md).
- **DeepSeek V4.1 · Maya-S** picks the same next token as the original 92.6% of the time.
- **MiniMax M3.1 · Maya-S** picks it 87.6% of the time (the average over its categories).

More on each model's card on [Hugging Face](https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith).

## How fast is it?

Real machines measured by our users, most with `./maya.sh --bench` (decode: three greedy 256-token answers; prefill:
an 8K-token prompt unless the row says otherwise). Share yours: `--bench`, then `--report`, in a
[GitHub issue](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues).

</details>

A token is about ¾ of a word. The speed holds as a conversation grows, and the first answers after a start are the
slowest while the caches fill with what you use.

## Using it

- **In the browser:** chat (kept in your browser, with search, pins and folders, per-chat instructions and thinking
  level, edit and regenerate, HTML previews), a live **Monitor** of the model and your hardware (down to what fills
  each GPU's memory), a context size and port you can change in Settings, and **Models**: every model set up on this
  machine, switching to another (Maya starts again with it) and each one's engine settings (context, GPUs, drafting,
  images, memory), saved into its `maya-<model>.json`.
- **Models page:** the catalog lists every Maya model with its size. Download one with a progress bar, load it, unload
  the running one or delete one - no command line needed.
- **Turbo:** one button beside the model's parameters sets the fastest settings for the model (greedy sampling, no
  thinking, expert skipping, the fastest drafting). It trades some quality for speed; switching it off restores your
  settings exactly.
- **Saved:** the sidebar's Saved line adds up what the model's own API (DeepSeek's, Z.ai's or MiniMax's) would have
  charged for the same tokens - the prompt Maya reuses from its cache at the API's cached price, DeepSeek's peak hours
  at its peak price - kept per model in `token-savings.json`; its prices can be changed in **Settings › Savings**.
- **Your apps and coding agents:** an OpenAI-compatible API at `http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1`, and Anthropic's at
  `http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/messages` (Claude Code: `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL=http://127.0.0.1:8080`). Tool calls are
  streamed as the model writes them, and a long prompt's reading progress too when a request asks for it
  (`"return_progress": true`). A web page served from another address can call it too once you allow its address
  (`"cors_origins"`, [docs/SETUP.md](docs/SETUP.md#web-pages-on-another-address)).
- **Responses API:** `/v1/responses` supports text/image conversations, streaming, function tool calls,
  schema-validated output, background tasks with cancellation/replay, and optional shared in-memory history
  (`store:true`, `previous_response_id`). Responses are not stored by default. Options and limits:
  [docs/RESPONSES.md](docs/RESPONSES.md).
- **Model API:** `/api/tags`, `/api/ps`, `/api/show`, `/api/chat`, `/api/generate`, `/api/pull` and `/api/delete` list,
  load, download and delete models from any app or script. A request for a model that is not loaded swaps to it, and
  `keep_alive` unloads it when idle. `/models/load`, `/models/unload` and `/models/sse` are on by default too
  (`"model_management": false` turns them off).
- **Thinking:** Off, Low, High (the default) or Max, in Settings or per request, and a **thinking budget** field that
  sets how many tokens the model may think before it answers.
- **Loop guard:** on by default - an agent that repeats the same tool call with the same result is steered off it.
- **Pictures:** attach one in the chat, or send image parts through either API. Every model reads them (setup details per
  model: [docs/MODELS.md](docs/MODELS.md)).
- **From another device:** `./maya.sh --setup --host 0.0.0.0 --api-key <secret>`. Always set a key.
- **Tuning:** `./maya.sh --calibrate` tunes the engine to your hardware in 10-15 minutes. Every setting:
  [docs/SETTINGS.md](docs/SETTINGS.md).
- <a name="deepseek-engram-prompt-read-ahead"></a><a name="deepseek-conversations-kept"></a><a name="deepseek-loading-the-experts"></a>**Each model's own options** - GLM-5.3-Flash's several requests at once (`--parallel`), long contexts and DFlash2
  drafter; DeepSeek's kept conversations, expert loading and Engram read-ahead; MiniMax's thinking levels:
  [docs/MODELS.md](docs/MODELS.md).

## What you need

| | |
| --- | --- |
| **GPU** | NVIDIA, compute capability 7.0 or newer (V100, RTX 20 and newer): one, or up to 16 that share the model. More VRAM is faster. Pascal (Tesla P40 / P100, GTX 10) is experimental, not validated yet. **AMD:** RX 7900 XT / XTX, Radeon AI PRO R9700 / RX 9070, Strix Halo and Gorgon Halo (Radeon 8060S / 8065S); RX 6800 / 6900 and Radeon PRO V620 / W6800 not validated yet; GLM-5.3-Flash and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 read pictures there too - [docs/AMD_MAYA.md](docs/AMD_MAYA.md) |
| **RAM** | 32 GB runs it. More RAM keeps more experts close and is faster |
| **Disk** | **A fast NVMe SSD** with room for the model (~75 GB for GLM-5.3 · Maya-XS, ~100 GB for GLM-5.3 · Maya-S, ~160 GB for GLM-5.3 · Maya-L, ~350 GB for DeepSeek V4.1 · Maya-S, ~470 GB for unsloth's Q8 GGUF of it, ~95 GB for MiniMax M3.1 · Maya-XS, ~200 GB for MiniMax-M3.1-Flash's EXL3 quant) and its picture encoder (1.1 GB). Not a hard disk |
| **System** | Linux (x86-64; a CPU with AVX2 is best), the NVIDIA driver, CUDA 12.x (CUDA 13 works for RTX 20 and newer, not for the V100 or Pascal), g++, Python 3.10+. Windows 10/11: [docs/WINDOWS.md](docs/WINDOWS.md). Not WSL2. AMD: ROCm 7 |

The setup checks all of this, prints the exact command for anything missing, and installs nothing system-wide.

## Install

Run the two commands in [Quick start](#quick-start) and answer a few questions (Enter takes the recommended choice).
The setup shows every download before it starts it, builds everything once (20-40 minutes plus the download) and
**starts the model**. Then open `http://127.0.0.1:8080`. Ctrl+C stops it, and `./setup.sh` starts it again right away.

- **Windows:** `START-MAYA.bat` instead of `./setup.sh` - see [docs/WINDOWS.md](docs/WINDOWS.md).
- **AMD:** [docs/AMD_MAYA.md](docs/AMD_MAYA.md).
- **Mac with Apple Silicon (experimental, GLM-5.3-Flash only):** `./maya.sh --backend metal` (on a Mac, `./maya.sh`
  picks Metal by itself) - see [docs/METAL.md](docs/METAL.md). Built and unit-tested on GitHub's Macs, not yet tested
  by us with the real model (we have no Mac): if you run it, please tell us how it went in an issue.
- **Intel Arc:** not supported by Maya's engine; a community report of the Maya-XS DeepSeek GGUFs on llama.cpp's SYCL backend: [docs/INTEL_ARC.md](docs/INTEL_ARC.md).
- **Updating:** the page's **Settings › About and updates**, or `git pull` then `./setup.sh`. The model isn't downloaded again.
- **Model files downloaded another way** (a download manager, another PC, a Hugging Face cache): `./maya.sh --import-model FOLDER`
  finds Maya's files in FOLDER by size and checksum, whatever they are called, and moves them into place (`--copy` when
  FOLDER is on another drive; the originals stay). Files that do not match are left alone.
- **Every option** (GPUs, context, a model folder you already have, remote access, ...): [docs/SETUP.md](docs/SETUP.md).

> **The start takes a few minutes:** the engine pins most of the free RAM (all but about 6 GB) for its expert tier,
> so other programs get little RAM while Maya runs.

## Something went wrong?

Run **`./maya.sh --report`** (Windows: `START-MAYA.bat --report`) right after a slow answer or an error, and attach the
`maya-report.txt` it writes to a [GitHub issue](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues). It holds your hardware,
your setup and the engine's speed lines; nothing is sent anywhere by itself. The engine's own log is
`maya-<model>.log` in the Maya folder.

- **The build stops** ("nvcc ... cannot build for these GPUs", "unsupported GNU version", or `cuda_profiler_api.h` /
  `nvtx3/nvToolsExt.h` missing): [docs/SETUP.md](docs/SETUP.md#if-the-build-fails) has the fix for each.
- **Slow, with the SSD busy all the time:** not enough free RAM for the experts. Close programs or add RAM.
- **Windows: answers suddenly several times slower:** the card is too full and Windows moves GPU memory to RAM. Leave
  more of it free: `START-MAYA.bat --setup --vram-reserve-mib 2048`
  ([the expert pool's size](docs/SETTINGS.md#the-expert-pools-size)).
- **Windows on a Strix Halo / Gorgon Halo: other programs run out of memory, or the setup warns about the page
  file:** set the page file to the size the setup prints ([docs/AMD_MAYA.md](docs/AMD_MAYA.md)).
- **The download stopped:** run `./maya.sh` again; it continues where it stopped.
- **Port 8080 is in use:** Maya is already running, or `--port 8081` starts another one. Settings > Port moves Maya to
  another port for good (it starts again there).

## Support

Maya is free and open source, and it always will be. It exists so that anyone can run the biggest open models at home,
on hardware they already own. Sponsors make that possible:

- **More hardware runs well.** Every release is tested on our own NVIDIA and AMD machines before it ships. Macs are so
  far tested only on GitHub's virtual Macs. The current goal is 50 monthly sponsors, enough for a real Mac and newer
  GPUs.
- **New models sooner.** Every new model and quant takes many hours of GPU time to build and check.
- **Faster fixes** when something breaks on your setup.

If you enjoy running big models on your own machine, a small monthly sponsorship is the best way to keep Maya growing.
Thank you!

# A benchmark shaped like coding agents: `tools/agent_bench.py`

`./maya.sh --bench` answers "how fast is one request on this PC": three short questions and two cold prompt reads,
one at a time. Coding agents load a server differently, and settings that only matter under that load (`--parallel`,
the expert tiers, the batched decode, reading a prompt while others decode, keeping a system prompt for the next
conversation) show nothing there. `tools/agent_bench.py` runs a scripted agent workload against a running server, so
two settings, two builds or two machines can be compared on the same requests.

## What it sends

Everything is fixed by `--seed`, so every run sends the same requests:

- `--clients` conversations at once (default 4). Each starts with one of a few shared system prompts (30K, 8K and 4K
  tokens by default) and a first message of 2-6K tokens.
- Then turns: each appends new text, as a tool result or a message would, and asks for an answer of 32-1024 tokens.
  80% of the new texts are under 2K tokens, 5% are 8K-64K. The next turn comes 2-20 s after the answer (the agent
  running its tools).
- Checks: at `--checks-per-hour` (40), a 30K-token system prompt with a fresh 2-4K-token question and a 16-token answer,
  as an agent's permission or routing check does.
- Two cold bursts, at a third and two thirds of the run: half the conversations start over and are read again with
  nothing reusable past the system prompt, as after an agent restarts.
- A conversation that grows past `--max-context` starts a new one from its latest turn, as an agent compacts.

The model's answers are not fed back: the next turn sends the scripted history, so the prompts do not depend on what
the model wrote. Requests are greedy with thinking off and ban the model's end tokens (`--ban auto` knows
GLM-5.3-Flash by the served model's name; `--ban none` or token ids otherwise), so every answer writes its whole
max_tokens and every run writes the same number of tokens. The texts are slices of this repository's files
(`--corpus <folder>` for others).

## Running it

Start the server the way you use it (`./maya.sh` with your config, `--parallel` as you run it), then:

```
python tools/agent_bench.py run --url http://127.0.0.1:8080 --clients 4 --label mine --out runs/mine-1.json
python tools/agent_bench.py plan --clients 4          # what it would send, without a server
```

A run takes `--duration` (900 s) plus the answers in flight. The first `--warmup` seconds (180) are left out of the
summary: the server's caches fill with the conversations then. For an A/B, restart the server before each run (a run
leaves its conversations in the server's caches), alternate the settings (A B A B), use the same `--seed`,
`--clients`, `--duration` and `--warmup`, and run each setting at least twice:

```
python tools/agent_bench.py compare runs/base-*.json runs/new-*.json
```

`compare` prints each metric's mean and standard deviation over a label's runs and marks a difference larger than
twice its standard error with `*`. A difference without the mark is within the runs' own spread.

It works against any OpenAI-compatible server (the requests are streamed and timed by the client). A Maya server adds
its own figures from `GET /metrics`.

## What it reports

Over the window from `--warmup` to `--duration` (a request counts when it ends inside it):

| metric | what it is |
|---|---|
| `output_tok_s` | tokens written per second by all conversations together |
| `in_flight` | requests in flight on average (with think times, fewer than `--clients`) |
| `decode_median`, `decode_p10` | per request: tokens per second from its first token to its last, what an agent sees; slowed by the other conversations' steps and reads (answers of 50 tokens or more) |
| `latency_median`, `latency_p90` | a turn from its send to its last token |
| `ttft_median`, `ttft_p90` | a turn's time to its first token: the read of its new text and any wait behind other reads |
| `decode <n>K+` | the decode median by the request's context length |
| `read <n>+` | prompt reads by their number of new tokens (not reused from the server's cache): new tokens per second of read time - the server's own read time when it sends `timings` (Maya, llama.cpp's server), else the time to the first token |
| `vram_hit_per_request`, `vram_hit`, `ram_fetch`, `promo` | Maya with experts in RAM: the routed experts found in VRAM per request (median), and the tiers' readings over the run (VRAM hit, RAM-tier experts fetched over PCIe a token, promotions a token) |

The run's JSON keeps every request's times, sizes and the server's records, for anything the summary does not show.

## Reading the results

- `output_tok_s` follows the workload as much as the server: with think times, a server twice as fast does not write
  twice as many tokens, because the conversations wait for their tools. Compare `decode_median` and the latencies for
  speed; `output_tok_s` and `in_flight` show how the load itself moved.
- Reads by new tokens and decode by context length compare like with like when runs drift apart (a faster run gets
  further into its conversations, so its contexts are longer).
- With experts in RAM, runs of the same setting differ more than with every expert in VRAM (where experts end up
  depends on timing): expect a few percent between reps.


# RDNA decode expert kernels

For `n_embd=4096`, `n_ff=2048`, the HIP decode dispatcher selects kernels by
architecture:

| Architecture | IQ2_XXS gate/up (`gate_up<16>`) | IQ2_S down (`down<22>`) | IQ3_XXS down (`down<18>`) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| gfx1100 | Optimized | Optimized | Optimized |
| gfx1151 | Optimized | Optimized | Original (legacy) |
| gfx1201 | Original (legacy) | Original (legacy) | Original (legacy) |

gfx1201 is unchanged and untuned by this expert-kernel work. Other shapes,
other quant types, other architectures and CUDA keep the original dispatch. Set
`MAYA_HIP_EXPERTS_LEGACY=1` before starting a process to force original HIP
expert kernels for an end-to-end comparison.

The main change is packed sign expansion: a multiply spreads four sign bits
into the high bit of four bytes, then masks and shifts produce the XOR masks
and two's-complement corrections. Codebook bytes are positive and nonzero, so
ordinary word addition introduces no carries between bytes. This replaces
HIP's emulated packed compare/subtract while retaining the original integer
scale divisions, FP32 accumulation order, clamping, q8_1 quantization and
expert combine order.

Gate/up retains sixteen wave32 waves per workgroup, two gate and two up rows
per wave, its 2 KiB codebook in LDS, and the fused shared-down branch. Down
uses two output rows per workgroup instead of four on optimized paths, reducing
its register budget. IQ2_S reads its 8 KiB codebook through global memory;
IQ3_XXS keeps its 1 KiB codebook in LDS. The grid selection and sign operations
are HIP-only.

The signed-codebook technique was adapted from
[Strata's moe_fused_iq.cu](https://github.com/Niko1221/Strata/blob/fb58e0dbc8399662c0e47c76578c6e878b14f6cf/src/prefill/moe_fused_iq.cu)
(MIT). Its HIP sign lookup tables were measured first; direct sign arithmetic
performed better here. The benchmark retains alternatives for reproducible
comparison: sign LUTs, global/LDS codebooks, eight/sixteen gate waves,
two/four/eight down rows, alignment-safe eight-byte IQ2_XXS/IQ3_XXS quant loads, a separate
shared-down launch, and an eight-wave occupancy constraint. Wider loads,
splitting shared down, and the occupancy constraint did not beat the selected
paths. This work does not fuse gate/up and down into one kernel.

## Reproduce

```bash
cmake -S . -B build-multi -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release \
  -DCMAKE_PREFIX_PATH=/opt/rocm-7.2.1 \
  -DCMAKE_HIP_COMPILER=/opt/rocm-7.2.1/llvm/bin/clang++ \
  -DMAYA_ENABLE_HIP=ON -DMAYA_ENABLE_CUDA=OFF \
  -DMAYA_PREFILL_MMQ=ON -DMAYA_NATIVE_EXPERTS=ON \
  -DMAYA_BUILD_TESTS=OFF \
  -DMAYA_GGML_DIR=/ai/github/project-maya/third_party/llama.cpp \
  '-DCMAKE_HIP_ARCHITECTURES=gfx1100;gfx1201;gfx1151'
CCACHE_DIR=/tmp/maya-experts-ccache cmake --build build-multi -j 8 \
  --target maya hip_expert_kernel_bench glm_ffn_parity glm_layer_parity \
  glm_model_test iq1_s_parity hip_intrinsics
export HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0
export LD_LIBRARY_PATH=/opt/rocm/lib
build-multi/hip_expert_kernel_bench
build-multi/hip_expert_kernel_bench --shared
build-multi/hip_expert_kernel_bench --parity-only --sweep
build-multi/glm_ffn_parity --selftest
build-multi/glm_layer_parity --selftest
build-multi/glm_model_test --selftest
build-multi/iq1_s_parity
build-multi/hip_intrinsics
```

Run these commands from the repository root. `--sweep` also works with timing;
without it the benchmark compares original kernels against production
dispatch. Baseline launches bypass production dispatch, so both implementations
are measured in the same binary. `--shared` includes a Q6_K shared-down matrix
of shape 4096x2048 in the gate/up launch and a shared contribution in down.

Random finite quantized blocks use the actual GGML block layouts (66, 82 and
98 bytes). Eight expert blobs contain `[gate | up | down]`. Timing rotates
five independent pools: each kernel's weight working set exceeds the 7900 XT's
80 MiB cache, including down's smaller matrices. Requested weight and buffer allocations are below 300 MiB
for either timed fixture. Parity-only uses one pool and less than 80 MiB.
Each timing graph contains sixty launches, with seven alternating original/new
rounds after warmup. Reported latency is the median graph time per launch.
GB/s counts quantized weight bytes (plus shared-down weights with `--shared`),
not codebooks, activations or actual DRAM transaction bytes.

## RX 7900 XT results, 2026-10-08

ROCm 7.2.1, Release, all three requested architectures compiled. GPU 0 only;
the existing Maya server stayed running. No clocks or other processes were
changed. These are synthetic decode measurements, not Strix Halo results.

| Kernel | Original us/call | New us/call | Original GB/s | New GB/s | Latency reduction |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| IQ2_XXS gate/up, IQ2_S fixture | 92.572 | 65.607 | 373.80 | 527.43 | 29.1% |
| IQ2_S down | 57.806 | 37.570 | 371.86 | 572.15 | 35.0% |
| IQ2_XXS gate/up, IQ3_XXS fixture | 93.209 | 68.616 | 371.24 | 504.30 | 26.4% |
| IQ3_XXS down | 48.220 | 43.506 | 532.77 | 590.49 | 9.8% |
| IQ2_XXS gate/up including shared down, IQ2_S fixture | 101.049 | 77.944 | 410.54 | 532.23 | 22.9% |

The full parity sweep passed all 620 comparisons with byte-identical q8_1
outputs and bit-identical FP32 outputs: maximum absolute error and relative L2
were both zero. Cases cover 1/3/8 routed experts, null expert slots, CPU/shared
contributions, zero activations, unclamped SwiGLU, and all tuning variants for
types 16/18/22. Legacy dispatch parity also passed. All five requested tests
passed: `glm_ffn_parity --selftest`, `glm_layer_parity --selftest`,
`glm_model_test --selftest`, `iq1_s_parity`, and `hip_intrinsics`.
Runtime tests used gfx1100; gfx1151/gfx1201 code objects were built, and
gfx1201 continues to select the original kernels. No CUDA toolkit was available
for a CUDA build; CUDA retains its original kernel template signatures and
arithmetic.

Full-model measurements reported after the initial synthetic tests showed
decode increasing from 15.1 to 16.4 tok/s (+8.6%) on RX 7900 XT (gfx1100),
with bit-identical parity. The two-GPU full-model configuration improved decode
by 3.9%.

## Strix Halo 8060S results (gfx1151), 2026-10-08

The following hardware measurements were reported for the original kernels
versus the optimized dispatch in commit `6f0bdf6`, before restoring legacy
IQ3_XXS down on gfx1151:

| Kernel / fixture | Original us/call | Optimized us/call | Speedup |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| `gate_up<16>`, gu16/down22 fixture | 203.9 | 181.4 | 1.124x |
| `down<22>` (IQ2_S) | 131.4 | 126.6 | 1.038x |
| `gate_up<16>`, gu16/down18 fixture | 207.7 | 183.3 | 1.133x |
| `down<18>` (IQ3_XXS) | 126.5 | 130.2 | 0.972x (regression) |

Full-model decode on gfx1151 increased from 17.8 to 18.0 tok/s with that
dispatch. Because optimized `down<18>` was slower, production now retains the
original IQ3_XXS down kernel on gfx1151 while keeping optimized `gate_up<16>`
and `down<22>`. gfx1100 keeps optimized `down<18>`. The full-model result above
predates this selective fallback; decode after the fallback has not yet been
measured. gfx1201 continues to use the original kernels and remains untuned.

## Further measurements on Strix Halo

Run the benchmark, `--shared`, and `--parity-only --sweep` on gfx1151 using the
same ROCm build settings. Then compare the resident-pool Maya-S decode trace
with the same prompt, token count and routing settings, once with
`MAYA_HIP_EXPERTS_LEGACY=1` and once without it. Set that variable on the
separate profiling process; these changes do not require touching a running
server.

Record per-kernel call counts and mean/median durations, total expert
milliseconds per token, decode tokens/s, and dense q14 matvec time as a control.
For the supplied trace, a 20% latency reduction corresponds to gate/up <=183 us
and IQ2_S down <=96 us. Check whether expert effective bandwidth rises from
~165 GB/s toward 204–216 GB/s (85–90% of the supplied 240 GB/s peak). The
discrete-card speedups do not establish that APU bandwidth result; use the
resident decode trace to confirm it. `--sweep` allows checking whether Strix
prefers different codebook placement or row geometry before changing dispatch.


# Maya on RX 7900 XT / XTX, R9700 / RX 9070 and Strix Halo / Gorgon Halo

This branch adds a Linux HIP build and installer path for Maya's GLM-5.3-Flash
engine on `gfx1100`, `gfx1201` and `gfx1151` (Strix Halo, Gorgon Halo). It uses one GPU, or two supported GPUs that split the
layers (see [Two GPUs](#two-gpus)), and reads pictures too: setup builds the image encoder for the GPU
(ggml's HIP backend), started only while a picture is encoded, as on NVIDIA; `--no-vision` leaves it out. The
[CPU image encoder](#cpu-image-encoder-on-linux) stays an option on Linux.
Windows: see [Windows](#windows).

Use a system ROCm 7 installation with its HIP compiler and hipBLAS, Python
3.10+, CMake 3.24+, and a C++20 compiler. `ROCM_PATH` selects an installation
outside `/opt/rocm`. Versioned LLVM slots, as Gentoo's
`<root>/lib/llvm/22/bin/clang++`, are accepted too. Everything Maya downloads
goes into the project or its model-data folder; setup does not install system
packages.

A distribution's own ROCm packages under `/usr` work too, including Debian's and Ubuntu's multiarch layout
(`/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libhipblas.so`, the compiler in `/usr/lib/llvm-<N>/bin`, the device bitcode in
`/usr/lib/llvm-<N>/lib/clang/<N>/amdgcn/bitcode`). A ROCm older than 7 (Debian's 6.4, hipBLAS 2.x) is untested and
refused by default; `MAYA_HIP_ALLOW_ROCM6=1` lets setup go on with a warning. The build then turns on hipBLAS 2's
`HIPBLAS_V2` API and skips `hipblasSetWorkspace` (absent before hipBLAS 3). Measured that way on Debian sid
(ROCm 6.4.3, hipBLAS 2.4) with an MI50 + MI100 layer split and a CPU without AVX2 - see the pull request.

```sh
./maya.sh --backend hip --gpu 0 --check
./maya.sh --backend hip --gpu 0 --context 8192 --no-vision --yes \
  --download-model --no-start --port 8099 --env MAYA_GLM_RAM_GB=60
./maya.sh --backend hip --port 8099
```

The first setup downloads about 96.5 GB of weights and verifies the SHA-256
hash of each shard. `--gguf-dir DIR` uses existing files instead. GPU numbers
are the kernel KFD topology order shown by `--check`; on the test machine the
7900 XT is GPU 0, an R9700 is GPU 1, and the integrated GPU is GPU 2. This
installer accepts `gfx1100`, `gfx1201` and `gfx1151` and compiles the binary for the
architectures of the chosen cards only (`gfx1100;gfx1201` for that 7900 XT + R9700).

RDNA 2 (`gfx1030`: RX 6800 / 6800 XT / 6900 XT / 6950 XT, Radeon PRO V620 / W6800) is accepted too, as experimental:
it has been run on a Radeon PRO V620 with ROCm 7 - please report how it goes on other RDNA 2 cards in an issue. These
cards have no matrix cores (WMMA), so the engine uses its portable kernels there (the prompt attention on the F32
kernel), and a build for RDNA 2 cards alone needs no rocWMMA headers (ROCm 7 no longer ships them). The ROCm
installation needs its library kernels (hipBLAS) for `gfx1030`.

Configs are named `maya-<quant>-hip.json` and
select the AMD device through `HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES`.

`--bench` and `--report` work on HIP: `./maya.sh --backend hip --bench` uses
`build-hip/maya` and the config's GPU order and environment; run it with Maya
stopped. `./maya.sh --backend hip --report` includes AMD GPU details from
`rocm-smi` or `amd-smi` when available (KFD topology otherwise), the ROCm path
and version, and the engine's speed lines. Add `--config /path/to/maya-hip.json`
to either command to select a particular installed config.

The discrete-GPU HIP config starts with an 8K context when requested above, 3 GiB of GPU
headroom, and 16 GiB of system-RAM headroom. The engine sizes the prompt chunk
from its prompt-memory budget. The example additionally caps the pinned expert
cache at 60 GiB. Change those settings with `--env KEY=VALUE` during setup.
Available RAM, rather than installed RAM, determines how much can be cached.
The engine reduces its RAM-tier allocation if ROCm cannot pin the requested
amount.

## CPU image encoder on Linux

HIP setup builds a GPU image encoder (above); instead, the server can use a CPU-only
`maya-vision` helper alongside the HIP text engine. This is an explicit manual
setup. Stop Maya before building or changing the config.

After HIP setup has fetched its pinned `third_party/llama.cpp` checkout, run
these commands from the Maya project root. If you used `--llama-dir`, use that
same directory for both the patch and `LLAMA_DIR` below. Use a separate build
directory to keep any existing CUDA image encoder intact.

```sh
.venv/bin/python tools/vision/glm5next_patch.py third_party/llama.cpp
cmake -S tools/vision -B build-vision-cpu -G Ninja \
  -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release \
  -DLLAMA_DIR="$PWD/third_party/llama.cpp" -DMAYA_VISION_CUDA=OFF
cmake --build build-vision-cpu --target maya-vision -j 2
```

The idempotent patch above adds GLM-5.3-Flash support to the pinned llama.cpp
image library. It is the same patch used by Maya's CUDA vision installer.
Download the matching vision files into your model-data directory (about
1.14 GB). This example uses the artifact revision tested with Maya v1.1.5:

```sh
mkdir -p data/vision
vision_url=https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/GLM-5.3-Flash-Maya-GGUF/resolve/f16ee4ba50d8fd2bb726ea1862ca6ca14ba188da/vision
curl -fL --retry 5 -C - -o data/vision/mmproj-GLM-5.3-Flash-F16.gguf \
  "$vision_url/mmproj-GLM-5.3-Flash-F16.gguf"
curl -fL --retry 5 -C - -o data/vision/GLM-5.3-Flash-vocab.gguf \
  "$vision_url/GLM-5.3-Flash-vocab.gguf"
printf '%s\n' \
  '3627575df16bd152db0f3fd7e488d270b33f3a9e6c7fa3b1b8ac381faafde882  data/vision/mmproj-GLM-5.3-Flash-F16.gguf' \
  '8f53cb1bd2e631c14ef413e3284735d9e53f3c508d07a6f609e705b487105912  data/vision/GLM-5.3-Flash-vocab.gguf' \
  | sha256sum -c -
```

Add this `vision` member to the HIP config, replacing `/absolute/path/to/maya`
with your project path. If your model-data directory differs, use its paths:

```json
"vision": {
  "exe": "/absolute/path/to/maya/build-vision-cpu/bin/maya-vision",
  "mmproj": "/absolute/path/to/maya/data/vision/mmproj-GLM-5.3-Flash-F16.gguf",
  "model": "/absolute/path/to/maya/data/vision/GLM-5.3-Flash-vocab.gguf",
  "gpu": false
}
```

Start with `./maya.sh --config /path/to/your-hip-config.json --port 8099`.
Check that `/health` reports `images: true`, then send an actual image through
`/v1/chat/completions`. Keep the manually edited config if running setup again;
the HIP installer still writes a text-only config.

Verified on Linux, Ryzen AI Max+ 395 / Radeon 8060S (`gfx1151`), 128 GB unified
RAM, ROCm 7.14.1, stock Maya v1.1.5 (`5403c222`), Maya-S24, and pinned llama.cpp
`3cf03257`: a real image request correctly identified a green circle on a white
background. Two checks took 3.38 and 3.12 seconds, including generation.
These are small functional checks, not broad vision-quality or performance
claims. CPU image encoding uses system RAM and adds CPU work; other AMD devices,
Windows, large-image limits, and multiple-image workloads were not tested here.

## Two GPUs

`--gpus 0,1` splits the layers across two supported cards, as on NVIDIA: each card caches the experts of its own
half, and the second half also runs the model's MTP block, which drafts the next token so both cards stay busy. Setup
puts the card with more VRAM first (it takes the bigger first half), leaves the split point to the engine, and writes
one hipBLASLt table per architecture (`MAYA_HIPBLASLT_TUNING` takes a path list, `:`-separated on Linux and `;` on Windows; each card uses the table
for its own architecture). A discrete GPU paired with a unified-memory APU puts the discrete GPU first and loads the
parts sequentially (`MAYA_GLM_PARALLEL_LOAD=0`), so the APU sizes its pool after the first part pins its RAM tier.
The two cards may be different architectures. The split passes one small host-memory hop
per token, so no peer-to-peer access between the cards is needed.

Measured on an AI PRO R9700 (32 GB, first) + RX 7900 XT (20 GB), 192 GB RAM, Maya-S, 8K context, 90 GB RAM tier,
v1.0.11, greedy, 256-token answers (median of 10 requests):

| | one RX 7900 XT | R9700 + RX 7900 XT |
|---|---|---|
| answer speed | ~15 tok/s | ~33 tok/s (drafts accepted ~76%) |
| prompt speed, 4K-token prompt | ~414 tok/s | ~490 tok/s |

39 back-to-back requests (1.8-4K-token prompts) ran without an error. The default split (the midpoint + 2) measured
best: moving two or four more layers to the first card did not speed up answers.

### Experimental expert helper

`MAYA_GLM_EXPERT_HELPER=1` is an opt-in alternative to a layer split: visible GPU 0 keeps every trunk layer,
attention, state and MTP; visible GPU 1 owns a complementary expert cache. A unified-memory helper reserves slots
in the existing portable pinned RAM tier (no second weight copy); a discrete helper uses its own VRAM, automatically
sized by aligned expert bytes with 512 MiB left free. Primary admissions, prefetch and background promotions exclude
helper-owned experts. At idle boundaries, colder helper experts can be replaced by hotter unowned experts of the same
layer.
The helper quantizes activations and reduces weighted expert outputs on its GPU before returning one vector per token.
CPU quantization runs only for actual CPU fallback; uncovered experts retain the existing CPU/PCIe/disk paths.
Batched prefill retains its primary-GPU staging and CPU pool, and may read helper-owned UMA slots without evicting them.
All of this is part of the single helper switch; there is no separate optimization flag.
Native GGUF experts only; EXL3 is not supported.

For physical device 0 = 8060S and 1 = R9700, run the engine with `HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1,0`,
`MAYA_GLM_EXPERT_HELPER=1` and `MAYA_GLM_SPLIT=0`. The indices are process-local; the system order is unchanged.
Keep the normal pack, context, headroom and MTP settings. Unset `MAYA_GLM_EXPERT_HELPER` to restore the existing
behavior. This experimental path does not claim general multi-GPU support or a speedup; measure it on the target
hardware.

#### The helper beside a layer split (two cards and the APU)

With `MAYA_GLM_SPLIT=auto` (or pinned boundaries) the helper's device is left out of the split and every part of the
split opens its own helper there over its own layers: a part's misses go to the APU, which computes them inside that
part's pinned RAM tier at the APU's memory speed instead of the part's CPU lane. For physical device 0 = 8060S and
1, 2 = two discrete cards, run the engine with `HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1,2,0`, `MAYA_GLM_EXPERT_HELPER=2` and
`MAYA_GLM_SPLIT=auto` (the split's devices are then 0 and 1; `MAYA_GLM_DEVS=0,1` says so explicitly). The helper's
device may not be one of the split's. The engine logs one `glm helper:` line per part. Measured on this layout: see the
speed table; the CPU lane still takes the misses the helper does not own.

## Strix Halo

Ryzen AI Max+ 395 / Radeon 8060S (`gfx1151`) uses unified memory. Use the same
HIP setup command above, selecting its GPU number and omitting the
`--env MAYA_GLM_RAM_GB=60` override. Setup detects the APU even if sysfs
reports only a small VRAM carve-out. It writes a 1 GiB reserve, 16 GiB of
system headroom, `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_SUB=1024`, and a 6144 MiB prompt budget
when system RAM is at least 96 GiB (4096 MiB otherwise), plus the existing
`gfx1151` hipBLASLt table. Use `--gpu N` for the APU alone, or `--gpus D,N` to pair it with a supported discrete GPU
(see [Two GPUs](#two-gpus)). It leaves `POOL_GB` and `RAM_GB` unset.

For integrated HIP devices the engine sizes the pool from `/proc/meminfo`'s
`MemAvailable` **after** dense weights, KV and prompt staging are allocated:
`min(expert bytes, max(0, MemAvailable - RAM_HEADROOM_GB - RESERVE_MB))`.
It does not treat HIP's reported GTT capacity (112 GiB on the 128 GB box) as
free physical memory. For example, 110 GiB available leaves 93 GiB after the
default headroom/reserve, capped at Maya-S's ~80.17 GiB expert pool. When every
expert fits, warm-up fills every pool slot without reserving idle spares;
the RAM tier keeps only its small per-class staging floor, and
the prompt disk landing ring defaults to 12 slots. Prompt buffers borrow the
pool's tail; they do not require a second 6 GiB allocation. Explicit `--env`
settings still override defaults; pool/VRAM caps still limit the pool.
Discrete HIP GPUs and CUDA keep their existing sizing behavior.

Measured on a 128 GB Strix Halo box with ROCm 7.2.2 and manually tuned settings:
~210 tok/s for 3-4K-token prompts and ~17.8 tok/s answers, with every expert
in the GPU pool (288 slots/layer, 12,096 total, ~99.9% hits). These measurements
precede the automatic sizing change; verify its startup log and repeated
prompt/answer rounds on the real box. See [tracking issue #6](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/6).

**Answers and prompts (v1.1.10).** Where every expert sits in the GPU pool, the APUs answer faster with the same
text (`MAYA_GLM_MTP_DECODE`, on by default on `gfx115x` only). On the Gorgon Halo of [Windows](#windows) (Radeon
8065S, Maya-S, 32K context), 256-token answers run at 28.4-29.6 tokens/s to short prompts and at 23.2-23.5 after
3K- and 8K-token prompts, against 18.4-19.7 without it; 3K- and 8K-token prompts read at 271 and 278 tokens/s
(the fused prompt experts and the `wmma2` attention, both on by default on `gfx1151`).

## Instinct MI300X / MI325X

AMD Instinct MI300X / MI325X (`gfx942`, CDNA3) is accepted as experimental, on one GPU. Like the MI100 it is a wave64
card and runs through the hip_compat half-wavefront layer.

Install ROCm's development packages for the card first (ROCm 7.14: `amdrocm-core-dev7.14-gfx942` and
`amdrocm-blas-dev7.14`), then run the setup above without the `MAYA_GLM_RAM_GB` override. On an MI325X (256 GB) every
expert of Maya-L fits in HBM next to a 1M-token context; with that much room, `"--kv-streaming", "off"` in the config's
`"args"` keeps the whole latent cache on the card too.

```sh
./maya.sh --backend hip --gpu 0 --context 1048576 --no-vision --yes --model Maya-L --download-model
```

`./maya.sh --bench` on one MI325X (v1.1.27, Maya-L, 1M context, `--kv-streaming off`): answers at 84.0 tokens/s,
prompts at 1307 tokens/s (2K tokens) and 1663 tokens/s (8K tokens). Not tried yet: the MI300X itself, more than one GPU.

## Windows

`START-MAYA.bat --backend hip` sets Maya up natively on Windows 10/11 for the same GPUs, Strix Halo and Gorgon Halo
included (Ryzen AI Max 300 / 400, Radeon 8050S / 8060S / 8065S, all `gfx1151`). It compiles the engine with ROCm's
clang in Visual Studio's environment, with Ninja, as `tools\hip\build_maya_windows.bat` does (#54). The download, the
pack and the dashboard are the same as on an NVIDIA PC.

1. Install once: a current AMD driver (AMD Software: Adrenalin Edition),
   [Visual Studio Build Tools](https://visualstudio.microsoft.com/visual-cpp-build-tools/) 2022 or 2026 with
   "Desktop development with C++", 64-bit Python 3.12 (`winget install -e --id Python.Python.3.12 --scope user`),
   Git, and [AMD's HIP SDK for Windows](https://www.amd.com/en/developer/resources/rocm-hub/hip-sdk.html) (7.2 is
   measured below; without it the setup offers AMD's ROCm SDK wheels).
2. On an APU, give the GPU most of the memory: Variable Graphics Memory in AMD Software (Performance > Tuning), or
   the iGPU memory size in the BIOS - 160 GB of 192 GB on the PC below, 96 GB on a 128 GB one. Restart.
3. Then:

   ```bat
   START-MAYA.bat --backend hip --gpu 0 --check
   START-MAYA.bat --backend hip --gpu 0 --setup --model glm-maya-l
   ```

The setup takes the ROCm it finds first: `ROCM_PATH`, TheRock's `rocm-sdk` (`ROCM_VENV`, Maya's `.venv` or PATH), AMD's
HIP SDK (`HIP_PATH`, else the newest in `C:\Program Files\AMD\ROCm`). With none it offers AMD's ROCm SDK wheels in
Maya's `.venv` (pip, from `repo.amd.com/rocm/whl-multi-arch`: `rocm[libraries,devel,device-<arch>]==7.14.1`, the first
with Gorgon Halo); `--check` prints that command instead. GPU numbers are hipInfo's (HIP's own order); without
hipInfo, Windows' AMD display adapters in the registry's order.

The build copies the ROCm SDK's HIP runtime (`amdhip64_7.dll`, `amd_comgr*.dll`, `rocm_kpack.dll`) next to
`build-hip\maya.exe`: Windows would otherwise load the driver's own from System32 before the SDK's.

**Memory on a Windows APU.** Windows gives the APU's GPU a fixed carve-out and does not count it as RAM: the 192 GB PC
below, with 160 GB of Variable Graphics Memory, shows 32 GB of RAM, and HIP reports 171.9 GB (the carve-out and three
quarters of Windows' shared half of its RAM). The engine therefore sizes it like a discrete card - the expert pool from
the GPU memory HIP reports free, the RAM tier from the free RAM and commit, the rest read from the SSD - and not from
`MemAvailable` as on Linux above. Windows' HIP runtime allocates at most 64 GiB plus the RAM Windows sees in one
piece (95.7 GiB there), so a larger pool goes into several allocations, each holding whole layers. Setup writes a
3 GiB reserve (the desktop runs on the same GPU), `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_SUB=1024`, and a 6144 MiB prompt budget when RAM
and GPU memory together are at least 96 GiB (4096 MiB otherwise).

**The page file on a Windows APU.** Windows charges every allocation on the APU's GPU to its commit - RAM plus page
file - although it does not count the carve-out as RAM: on the PC below, the free commit fell by each of the engine's
GPU allocations, whatever their size. So the page file must be at least about the GPU's share. With less, either the
experts that do not fit the commit stay off the GPU (slower answers), or other programs run out of memory while the
model runs. Both engines (GLM-5.3-Flash's and DeepSeek's) keep their GPU experts within what Windows can still
commit, less max(8 GB, 10% of the RAM) for the other programs, and say how many GB of experts that left off the GPU,
and the page file that keeps them on it, in their log, the dashboard's Monitor and `/v1/status`. `--check` and the setup ask for the GPU's share plus max(8 GB, 10% of the RAM) - the RAM
holds Windows and the other programs - and warn when the page file is smaller: set a custom size (initial and maximum,
in MB) of at least that under System > About > Advanced system settings > Performance > Settings > Advanced > Virtual
memory > Change, then restart Windows. It is a reserve more than a file in use: the PC below ran with 160 GB of page
file for its 160 GB GPU share (the setup asks for 180 GB there: at 160 GB about 6 GB of commit was left while the
model ran), and at most 12 GB of it was ever written.

**Kernel submission.** Windows' HIP runtime keeps launches in a batch until the host waits on the GPU; Linux submits
each one. The engine's service thread waits for routes the GPU writes into host memory, so after 1 ms without one it
submits the batch (`cudaStreamQuery`, which does not wait). `GPU_FLUSH_ON_EXECUTION=1`, the runtime's own switch to
submit every launch, also works but costs ~31 us a launch.

Checked on a Ryzen AI Max+ PRO 495 / Radeon 8065S (Gorgon Halo, 192 GB LPDDR5X, 160 GB of it the GPU's), Windows 11,
HIP SDK 7.2 (HIP 7.2.60201), driver 32.0.31041, Maya-L, 32K context: all 15 HIP checks of [Validation](#validation)
pass, and every expert sits in the GPU pool (134.3 GB, 288 slots a layer, warmed from the NVMe in 44 s).

## Prompt speed

Prompts run in chunks, and inside a chunk the mixers and the dense FFN run in
sub-batches. Two settings matter on AMD:

- `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_SUB` sets the sub-batch size. It was a fixed 256; it is
  now a runtime setting with 256 as the default. Larger sub-batches give the
  matrix cores bigger GEMMs. Setup writes `1024` together with
  `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_MB=4096` for cards with 20 GB or more; the extra prompt
  memory is borrowed from the expert pool only while a prompt runs.
- `MAYA_HIPBLASLT_TUNING` points at a table of hipBLASLt solutions for the
  prompt's FP16 projections, measured per architecture with
  `tools/hip/tune_hipblaslt` (`tools/hip/<arch>-glm-hipblaslt-<version>.txt`).
  Setup writes it when a table for the card exists. The engine accepts a table
  only for its own architecture and hipBLASLt version; otherwise, and for shapes
  without a row, it keeps plain hipBLAS. Builds of one hipBLASLt version can
  number their solutions differently, so a version may have more than one table
  (`<version>.txt`, `<version>-b.txt`): the engine keeps every matching table
  and takes, per shape, the first whose solution its hipBLASLt accepts. The tuner's default shapes are
  DeepSeek's, so a table generated from them covers none of another model's
  projections: pass `--preset glm` for GLM-5.3-Flash (or `--shape T,N,K`) and
  check the engine's log, which now prints one line per shape that misses the
  table. On a 8060S, four GLM rows took a prompt from 128 to 168 tok/s. The tuner's absolute-error gate now
  scales with `sqrt(K / 4096)`, so long-K projections are no longer rejected.

Measured prompt speed, GLM-5.3-Flash Maya-S-v2 IQ2_XXS, ROCm 7.2:

| Setting | RX 7900 XT | Ryzen AI Max+ 395 (8060S) | R9700 |
|---|---|---|---|
| prompt chunk forced to 256 (the earlier config) | ~50 tok/s | ~70 | - |
| engine-sized chunk | ~198 | ~116 | - |
| + tuned hipBLASLt | ~253-259 | ~195 | - |
| + `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_SUB=1024`, `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_MB=4096` | ~413 | ~217 | ~490-560 |

The 8060S runs used the same prompt-path changes on a local build. The R9700
runs used a pinned RAM tier sized for every expert the VRAM pool does not hold. On
gfx1201, plain hipBLAS is already close to hipBLASLt for most shapes, so its
table keeps only the rows that were at least 2% faster.

## What changed

- Added HIP GPU detection, compilation, rebuild stamps, and serving configs to
  `maya.py`, with separate CUDA and HIP build directories.
- Completed the CUDA-shaped runtime/BLAS mappings needed by GLM, excluded
  NVIDIA's profiling ranges from HIP, and linked the GLM prompt path to HIP MMQ
  and hipBLAS.
- Replaced the GLM hyper-connection kernel's unsupported FP32 `__ldcg` with
  agent-scope acquire loads on HIP. CUDA keeps its existing implementation.
- Kept CUDA WMMA attention guarded to CUDA builds. HIP uses the F32 attention
  kernel; since v1.0.7 its 32-row tile keeps the latent rows FP16 in shared
  memory (34 KB), within RDNA's 64 KiB workgroup limit.
- Fixed the KDA parity fixture to initialize its recurrence state explicitly;
  its numerical tolerance is unchanged.
- The HIP post-store system fences in both shared doorbell kernels and Maya's
  fused routing signal (backported from Strata) arrived separately in v1.0.7
  (#2); the validation below includes them.

Upstream Strata was inspected at `d5ea713` (0.1.40.3). Its AMD backend documents
runtime selection, memory limits, desktop VRAM headroom, and newer packed-byte
optimizations. Maya's GLM engine is separate from Strata's current Qwen engine,
so Strata's model benchmarks do not establish Maya performance.

## Validation

Test host: RX 7900 XT 20 GiB, Ryzen 7 9800X3D, 192 GB installed DDR5, native Ubuntu Linux, system ROCm 7.2.1 / Clang 22, plus AI PRO R9700 32 GiB. Maya base v1.0.10 (`e4bd57e`).

The HIP engine and selected test targets build. All 14 selected GPU checks
pass: device allocation, expert upload staging, packed-byte/shuffle
intrinsics, asynchronous mapped-memory handoff, IQ1_S arithmetic, GLM
hyper-connections, KDA, FFN, DSA, layer arithmetic, synthetic-model logits
against the committed reference, and HIP MMQ. Six isolated installer tests
pass. The fused HC decode kernel also agrees with the independent CPU
reference across eight consecutive 4096-wide steps, including in-place gate
updates, in all three variants (default HC3, forced HC2, and forced HC1).
The full-model runs are below.

The real batched MLA attention kernel agrees with a double-precision CPU
softmax reference (absolute tolerance `5e-5`), including empty and masked cell
lists, partial and multiple tiles, and multiple head groups. It reads the
FP16 latent cache added in Maya v1.0.4.

The GLM handoff test replays the real routing graph for 100 disk requests
and 100 CPU-lane requests with changing IDs, weights, inputs and answers. The
CPU observes each request without a driver query or stream synchronization.
The same multi-architecture engine and test build pass all 14 checks on the
7900 XT and R9700. HC1 and HC2 also pass on the R9700. The HIP intrinsics check
covers all 65,536 pairs of byte values with varying neighboring lanes and
permutation selectors, against per-byte CPU references. The runtime rejects
the uncompiled `gfx1036` integrated GPU with the actual device and compiled
architecture list in its message. See [R9700 build details](AMD_RDNA4.md) and
[the shared AMD backport audit](AMD_UPSTREAM.md).

```sh
python3 tools/test_maya_hip.py
LD_LIBRARY_PATH=/opt/rocm/lib HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0 \
  ctest --test-dir build-hip --output-on-failure --timeout 60 \
  -R '^(glm_(hc|kda|ffn|dsa|layer)_parity|glm_model_test|hip_intrinsics|hip_(glm_handoff|glm_prefill_attention|handoff|device_selftest|prefill_mmq_parity|expert_cache_staging)|iq1_s_parity)$'
MAYA_GLM_HC2=1 LD_LIBRARY_PATH=/opt/rocm/lib HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0 \
  build-hip/glm_hc_parity --selftest
MAYA_GLM_HC1=1 LD_LIBRARY_PATH=/opt/rocm/lib HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0 \
  build-hip/glm_hc_parity --selftest
```

The relevant build targets must be built before running CTest. Build and test
logs are kept in `build-hip/`.

Full model (Maya-S, 8K context) on the RX 7900 XT with the config setup writes:
seven smoke requests (arithmetic, code, a two-turn conversation, three
400-token answers) and five back-to-back prompt+answer rounds complete, with
prompts at 307-385 tok/s on 1.8-2.5K-token prompts and the hipBLASLt table
loaded. The R9700 runs the full model with every expert in VRAM or a pinned
RAM tier; the prompt speeds above were measured there.


### Confirmed working 
-  Test host: RX 7900 XT 20 GiB, Ryzen 9 7900X, 96 GB installed DDR5, decokerized Ubuntu Linux, system ROCm 7.2.4, Maya base v1.1.46 (`3589d6f`). 
- Model : Maya-S24, 1M context


# R9700 / RX 9070: Maya HIP

The Linux HIP installer accepts RX 7900 XT/XTX (`gfx1100`) and RX 9070 /
Radeon AI PRO R9700 (`gfx1201`). It uses one GPU, text input, and system ROCm 7.
The shared backend changes come from Strata
[`d5ea713`](https://github.com/Niko1221/Strata/tree/d5ea7133741e67743c0e886bb426c0ce8d69cf6c).
The R9700 runs the full model (Maya-S, about 20 tok/s answers and 490-560 tok/s
prompts, every expert in VRAM or a pinned RAM tier).

```sh
./maya.sh --backend hip --gpu 1 --check
```

Use the KFD GPU number printed by setup; the example machine has a 7900 XT
at GPU 0 and an R9700 at GPU 1. Setup chooses the supported card with the most
VRAM when `--gpu` is omitted. See [AMD_MAYA.md](AMD_MAYA.md) for model setup.

## One binary, three code targets

Setup compiles the architectures of the chosen cards (a 7900 XT + R9700: `gfx1100;gfx1201`); a manual build can
name all three, `gfx1100;gfx1201;gfx1151`. CMake also accepts space-separated
lists and architecture feature suffixes. The runtime strips the feature suffix
and checks exact membership in the compiled list, then requires wave32.
Its diagnostics print the actual architecture and the compiled targets.

`gfx1151` (Strix Halo / Gorgon Halo) is supported too: the setup takes the APU,
with its unified-memory accounting, and it runs GLM-5.3-Flash, DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017
and MiniMax-M3.1-Flash, their EXL3 quants included ([AMD_MAYA.md](AMD_MAYA.md)).

A manual build, using Maya's existing pinned llama.cpp checkout:

```sh
maya_rocm_root="$(realpath "${ROCM_PATH:-/opt/rocm}")"
cmake -S . -B build-multi -G Ninja -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release \
  -DMAYA_ENABLE_HIP=ON -DMAYA_ENABLE_CUDA=OFF \
  -DMAYA_NATIVE_EXPERTS=ON -DMAYA_PREFILL_MMQ=ON \
  -DMAYA_BUILD_TESTS=OFF \
  '-DCMAKE_HIP_ARCHITECTURES=gfx1100;gfx1201;gfx1151' \
  -DCMAKE_HIP_COMPILER="$maya_rocm_root/llvm/bin/clang++" \
  -DCMAKE_HIP_COMPILER_ROCM_ROOT="$maya_rocm_root" \
  -DCMAKE_PREFIX_PATH="$maya_rocm_root" \
  -DMAYA_GGML_DIR="$PWD/third_party/llama.cpp"
cmake --build build-multi --target maya strata-device hip_expert_cache_staging \
  hip_intrinsics hip_handoff hip_glm_handoff hip_glm_prefill_attention iq1_s_parity \
  glm_hc_parity glm_kda_parity glm_ffn_parity glm_dsa_parity glm_layer_parity \
  glm_model_test hip_prefill_mmq_parity -j 3
```

The handoff target and the tested publication fences were merged in v1.0.7 (#2).

## Validation

Native Ubuntu, Ryzen 7 9800X3D, 192 GB DDR5, ROCm 7.2.1 / Clang 22;
RX 7900 XT 20 GiB and AI PRO R9700 32 GiB. The same engine binary contains
64 bundled code objects for each of gfx1100, gfx1201 and gfx1151.

```sh
python3 tools/test_maya_hip.py
python3 tools/test_hip_archs.py
env -u ROCR_VISIBLE_DEVICES LD_LIBRARY_PATH=/opt/rocm/lib HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1 \
  ctest --test-dir build-multi --output-on-failure --timeout 60 \
  -R '^(glm_(hc|kda|ffn|dsa|layer)_parity|glm_model_test|hip_intrinsics|hip_(glm_handoff|glm_prefill_attention|handoff|device_selftest|prefill_mmq_parity|expert_cache_staging)|iq1_s_parity)$'
env -u ROCR_VISIBLE_DEVICES LD_LIBRARY_PATH=/opt/rocm/lib HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1 \
  MAYA_GLM_HC1=1 build-multi/glm_hc_parity --selftest
env -u ROCR_VISIBLE_DEVICES LD_LIBRARY_PATH=/opt/rocm/lib HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1 \
  MAYA_GLM_HC2=1 build-multi/glm_hc_parity --selftest
```

Both cards pass 14/14 GPU checks; HC1/HC2 also pass on the R9700. The intrinsics
test compares signed dot4, overflow, shuffles, and 65,536 packed-byte pairs and
permutations to CPU references. Six setup tests and five architecture-parser
tests pass. Selecting the uncompiled gfx1036 iGPU is refused before a kernel
launch. CUDA was not compile-checked because the test host has no `nvcc`.

The copied gfx1201 hipBLASLt tables match versions `100202` and `100500`.
They serve the inherited tuning loader. Maya's GLM prompt projections can now
use the separate `gfx1201-glm-hipblaslt-100202.txt` table through that loader;
shapes without a tuned row continue through hipBLAS. Its quantized expert path
still uses GGML MMQ.

## v1.1.29 R9700 Validation (2026-10-26)

On upstream `v1.1.29` (`8b4b39f`) on AI PRO R9700 (`gfx1201`):

- **`ds4_gguf_test`**: **100% PASS (`ALL OK (0 failed)`)** across all KV pack, sparse attention, WMMA, router, `mv`, `IQ1_S`, `IQ1_M`, `IQ2_XXS`, `IQ2_XS`, `IQ2_S`, `IQ3_XXS`, `IQ3_S`, `IQ4_XS`, `Q2_K`, `Q3_K`, `Q4_K`, `Q5_K`, `MXFP4`, and CPU-lane tests. The 1-ULP `NE=4` vs `NE=1` discrepancy was resolved by wrapping `sub_dot`, `gate_up_kernel`, `down_kernel`, and `lanes_sum` reductions in explicit `#pragma clang fp contract(off)` helpers with `asm volatile` register barriers (`fadd_no_fma`, `fsub_no_fma`, `fmul_no_fma`, `fma_no_contract`).
- **`glm_attn_bench`**: **PASS** (100% bit-exact parity; `dsa_score` is **3.84x faster** on `gfx1201` at 130K context: `65.0 us` vs `249.4 us` via `v_permlanex16` + `row_xmask` intra-lane butterfly reduction).
- **`hip_expert_kernel_bench --parity-only`**: **PASS** (`bit_identical=yes` across all production quantization configurations including `gu=21/down=17` `IQ3_S/IQ2_XS` and `gu=22/down=22`).
- **`hip_intrinsics`**: **PASS** (100% parity).



# Shared AMD backport audit

Compared Maya v1.0.4 (`70e0746`) plus this branch's GLM HIP port with Strata
[`d5ea713`](https://github.com/Niko1221/Strata/tree/d5ea7133741e67743c0e886bb426c0ce8d69cf6c).
The publication fixes below were merged in v1.0.7 (#2). Broader backend
updates require their own GLM validation.

## Publication fixes applied

Strata [issue #697](https://github.com/Niko1221/Strata/issues/697) reports that
the mapped-memory doorbell store can remain invisible to the CPU until stream
synchronization on gfx1201. Upstream uses a volatile ring store and a HIP-only
`__threadfence_system()` after the store in both shared doorbell kernels.
Maya now has those changes. The earlier payload fences remain.

Maya's `glm_fast.cu` has a separate routing request ring. Its `rq->seq` is
already volatile, but also lacked a post-store fence. The same HIP-only fence
now follows that signal. This is an application of the same publication rule
to Maya's code, not a copied Qwen kernel.

Validation on native Linux / system ROCm 7.2.1:

| Check | RX 7900 XT, gfx1100 | AI PRO R9700, gfx1201 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Shared handoff: 100 CPU waits, 100 copy/rings, 100 fused publishes | Passed | Passed |
| Actual GLM routing graph: 100 disk requests and 100 CPU-lane requests | Passed | Passed |
| Same multi-architecture engine/GPU suite | 14/14 passed | 14/14 passed |
| 321B model request | Not verified | Not run |

Both cards now use the same `gfx1100;gfx1201;gfx1151` build. HC1/HC2 pass on
the R9700. `gfx1151` (Strix Halo / Gorgon Halo) is supported now too and runs GLM, DeepSeek and
MiniMax, EXL3 included ([AMD_MAYA.md](AMD_MAYA.md)). The installer enables all three architectures. These checks do not establish full-model correctness or speed.
See [AMD_RDNA4.md](AMD_RDNA4.md) for build and validation commands.

## Further integration requirements

| Shared component | Upstream change and integration requirement |
| --- | --- |
| `cmake/hip_backend.cmake` | Applied: upstream architecture lists, semicolon/space parsing, and compiled architecture metadata. Normalization also removes empty entries and duplicates before compiler use. |
| `src/core/device.cu` and `include/strata/core/device.hpp` | Applied: exact base-architecture checks against the compiled list, wave32 check, and actual/build architecture diagnostics. Broader upstream device-list helpers are separate. |
| `include/strata/hip_compat/` | Use the upstream headers as the base, retaining Maya's GLM mappings for occupancy, stream legacy, profiling, SGEMM/batched SGEMM, error codes and `__grid_constant__`. Replacing them without those additions breaks GLM compilation. |
| `intrinsics.hpp` | Applied: upstream RDNA4 dot4 guard, byte permutation, SWAR byte operations and older-HIP warp synchronization. Packed-byte and GLM reference checks pass on both discrete cards. |
| AMD setup detection | Applied: gfx1201 eligibility and fallback name; Maya uses the shared supported-architecture list. Unified-memory detection and wider installer helper consolidation are separate. |
| hipBLASLt table loader and tables | Added upstream gfx1201 tables for versions 100202/100500. The loader keeps its architecture/version checks. Maya's GLM prompt projections now reuse that loader with separate per-architecture GLM tables. |

The runtime query `hipblasLtGetVersion` on this host returns `100202`, or
**1.2.2**, and the installed header agrees. This is the value the loader
compares, regardless of a package's version label. Upstream includes both
`gfx1100-hipblaslt-100202.txt` and `gfx1201-hipblaslt-100202.txt`.

Maya's GLM dense prompt products in `src/core/glm_prefill.cu` still use SGEMM
and batched SGEMM through hipBLAS where appropriate. Its FP16 prompt
projections now reuse the inherited hipBLASLt loader in `src/prefill/gemm.cu`
when `MAYA_HIPBLASLT_TUNING` names a matching GLM table. Shapes absent from
the table fall back to GEMM Ex through hipBLAS, and the quantized expert path
continues to use GGML MMQ. The GLM tables were tuned and checked separately for
gfx1100, gfx1151 and gfx1201; the inherited Qwen tables are not interchangeable
with them.

## Compiler and model boundaries

Strata [issue #1180](https://github.com/Niko1221/Strata/issues/1180) concerns
its fused native expert prompt kernel and `waves_per_eu(8)`. That source is
absent from Maya, and no such setting was found in Maya's source tree. It is
not a reason to disable Maya's different GLM kernels without evidence.
The synthetic GPU checks do not establish full-model quantized-expert
or draft verification correctness. The first real request remains necessary.

Strata [issue #1389](https://github.com/Niko1221/Strata/issues/1389) measures
Qwen prompt performance. Its profiling and tuning method is useful once GLM
runs; its token rates are not Maya benchmarks.


# Maya on an NVIDIA DGX Spark (GB10) and Grace

A single DGX Spark runs Maya-S (GLM-5.3-Flash) at its full context, with every expert in the GPU's own pool. The
catch is that a DGX Spark has no VRAM: the GB10 draws on the system's RAM, so the expert pool, the prompt buffers and
the desktop all share the same 120 GB. This page is what a bring-up needs to know about that, what the aarch64 port
had to change, and what is still missing.

Measured on one DGX Spark (see [Measured](#measured)): **16.8 tokens/s** decoding, **730 tokens/s** reading an
8K-token prompt, every expert resident, a 99.97% cache hit. The config this setup installs also drafts with a
DFlash2 drafter, which takes the decode to **21.1 tokens/s** (greedy; see
[Drafting and several requests](#drafting-dflash2-and-several-requests-at-once)), and a second config answers four
requests at once for 1.80x the tokens in all. [Measured](#measured) has the numbers, and the
README's [speed tables](../README.md#how-fast-is-it) have the DGX Spark beside the other machines.

What the aarch64 port changed, where each component of the build comes from, and what other people's work it stands
on are in [DGX_SPARK_BUILD.md](DGX_SPARK_BUILD.md).

## The machine

| | |
| --- | --- |
| GPU | NVIDIA GB10, one device, compute capability 12.1 (sm_121) |
| Memory | 120 GB, shared with the system (no VRAM of its own) |
| CPU | 20 ARM cores (Cortex-X925, CPU part 0xd87) |
| Driver | 580 or newer (CUDA 13 needs 580) |
| Toolkit | CUDA 13.0 or newer, `nvcc` on the path |
| OS | Ubuntu 24.04, aarch64 |

Grace (NVIDIA's aarch64 CPU, as in a Grace Superchip or a GH200) has the same shape: an aarch64 host whose GPU draws
on memory the CPU shares with it. A discrete NVIDIA card on an aarch64 host (a card in a Grace server, for example)
takes the ordinary discrete path - the unified-memory rules below apply only where the GPU's memory is the system's.

## Bringing it up

The installer is the same one every platform uses; nothing about the setup is DGX-specific.

```sh
./maya.sh --check                 # the PC checks only
./maya.sh --setup --yes --gpu 0 --context 131072 --no-vision --download-model
```

`--check` prints what the engine will do with the hardware:

```
  NVIDIA GPUs:
    GPU 0 (NVIDIA GB10, 120 GB shared with the system) - can be used
  [ok] using GPU 0 (NVIDIA GB10, 120 GB shared with the system)
  the GPU's memory is the system's RAM: the engine fills it with the most-used experts and leaves the rest to the RAM
  tier and the SSD, so the free RAM at the start is what the pool can take
  [ok] CUDA toolkit 13.0: /usr/local/cuda/bin/nvcc
  [ok] NVIDIA driver 580.95.05
  [ok] RAM: 120 GB, 16 GB available now
  [ok] CPU: ARM (CPU part 0xd87) (ARM64, 20 threads; the expert kernels use ggml-cpu's NEON paths)
```

The setup downloads about 96 GB of GGUF shards (Maya-S-v2-IQ2_XXS) and writes the pack next to them, so the models
folder wants a fast NVMe SSD with ~100 GB free. `--no-vision` skips the image encoder (see
[Still missing](#still-missing)); without it the setup also fetches 1.1 GB of vision files and builds the encoder.

The context costs VRAM that the experts want, so 131072 leaves a pool of about 80 GB - enough for every expert of
Maya-S. A smaller context (32768, the default) leaves more room for the pool, but at 131072 every expert already
fits, so there is nothing to gain from it here: the context only costs state and KV room.

## Running it

The engine answers on an OpenAI-compatible API and serves a dashboard:

```sh
./serve-maya.sh            # start (waits until the API answers)
./serve-maya.sh status     # is it up, and where
./serve-maya.sh logs       # the server's and the engine's logs
./serve-maya.sh stop
```

- API: `http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1` (`/v1/chat/completions`, `/v1/responses`, `/v1/messages` for Anthropic's shape)
- Dashboard: `http://127.0.0.1:8080/`

`serve-maya.sh` finds a server it did not start (one a supervisor or a hand started), so `status` and `stop` work
whatever started it. The first start reads ~86 GB of experts: about 40 s to load them and warm the tiers, and the
first answers wait for that.

The default config drafts with DFlash2; the parallel-4 config (four requests at once, no drafting) is started with
`MAYA_CONFIG=$PWD/maya-maya-s-v2-iq2_xxs-parallel4.json ./serve-maya.sh` - stop the running one first, they are the
same model ([Drafting and several requests](#drafting-dflash2-and-several-requests-at-once)).

```sh
curl http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/chat/completions -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"model":"glm-5.3-flash","messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Hello"}],"max_tokens":200}'
```

## What the aarch64 port needed

The engine's fast expert kernels are x86 (AVX-512 and AVX2). On aarch64 the port leaves them out and routes the
work through ggml-cpu's own NEON paths:

| | x86-64 | aarch64 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The Q2_0 pack's expert kernel | AVX-512 VNNI/VBMI | not built: the native packs use ggml-cpu instead |
| The EXL3 expert kernels | AVX-512 / AVX2 / scalar | not built: the EXL3 quants do not run (the setup refuses them) |
| The native packs (IQ2, IQ3, Q4, ...) | this repository's kernels | ggml-cpu's NEON kernels |
| The spin wait | `_mm_pause` | `yield` |
| The store fence | `_mm_sfence` | `dmb oshst` |
| The CPU feature checks | AVX-512 present or not | always "no AVX-512", so no illegal instruction |

`src/kernels/cpu/portable.cpp` replaces the x86 kernels in the build. Its stubs do not silently compute something
else: they print what is missing and stop, because a wrong answer is worse than no answer. The canonical Q2_0 pack
therefore refuses to start on an ARM CPU with a clear message (`cpu_require_expert_support()`), and the IQ2_XXS pack
this setup uses is a native pack - it goes through ggml-cpu.

ggml-cpu is built with the CPU's own features, taken from `/proc/cpuinfo`:

```
-march=armv8.6-a+dotprod+i8mm+fp16
```

The GB10 has `asimddp` and `i8mm`, so the dot-product and the 8-bit integer matrix-multiply paths are on (its `bf16`
is not in the string: ggml's ARM backend does not use it). `setup.py` picks the string from the kernel's feature list,
so the same setup on an older ARM CPU picks a smaller one.

## The expert pool on a unified-memory device

A discrete card reports its own free VRAM, and the pool takes it. A unified-memory device cannot: the runtime's free
figure counts the model's file-backed page cache as used (on the test machine it read about 2 GB free with the 90 GB
pack cached), so the pool would come out far too small - the start fails with

```
maya generate: glm fast: CUDA0 has no room for the expert pool: 5 slots a layer, at least 8 needed (about 604 MiB
more). To make room: a smaller --max-context, --kv int8, or close other programs that use the GPU
```

The pool is therefore sized from `/proc/meminfo`'s `MemAvailable`, which leaves the reclaimable cache out - the kernel
evicts it as the experts are read back in:

```
glm fast: CUDA0 unified memory: MemAvailable 99.17 GB, headroom 16.00 GB + 1024 MB reserve, experts need 80.17 GB,
           pool budget 80.17 GB
glm fast: CUDA0 layers [0,45) expert pool 80.17 GB, 288-288 slots/layer (12096 total)
glm fast: CUDA0 all experts fit in the GPU pool; RAM tier is staging only
```

- `headroom 16.00 GB` is `MAYA_GLM_RAM_HEADROOM_GB` - the RAM left free for the system (the desktop, the file
  cache, everything else that is not Maya). Raise it on a busy machine; lower it when the pool is short.
- `1024 MB reserve` is the VRAM kept free beside the pool for the driver, the sampler and what is allocated later (a
  unified device uses 1024 MiB, a discrete card 700).
- Nothing is checked after the pool is written: the free figure moves with the page cache, so a second check would
  give the memory back and then take it away. The start fails only if the pool cannot hold one route's experts in
  every layer, and it then says how many MiB more it needs.

Because the pool is RAM, **other programs on the machine take from it**: the pool is sized from what is free at the
start, so a desktop with 40 GB of its own applications running leaves the engine that much less. The RAM tier (the
pinned tier below the pool) is staging only when every expert fits, as it does here.

The CPU lane - the threads that compute the experts that do not fit in the pool - measures itself against the copy
over the link at the start and takes fewer experts when the link is faster:

```
glm fast: CUDA0 CPU lane: 13 threads, an expert 0.171 ms on the CPU vs 0.117 ms over PCIe -> of 0..8 RAM-tier experts
          the CPU takes 0 0 1 1 1 2 2 3 3
```

On this machine the link is the faster way, so the CPU lane takes little, and when every expert fits in the pool it
takes none at all (`glm fast: CUDA0 CPU lane off: the RAM tier holds no expert`).

## Measured

`./maya.sh --bench` with Maya stopped (the standard measurement: three greedy answers of 256 tokens with the
thinking off, and a prompt read at 2048 and 8192 tokens). This is the baseline without drafting - the config this
setup installs drafts with DFlash2, which takes the decode to 21.1 tokens/s
([Drafting and several requests](#drafting-dflash2-and-several-requests-at-once)):

```
Project Maya v1.1.26 benchmark, 2026-10-24 11:36
GPUs: NVIDIA GB10 120 GB; RAM 120 GB; CPU ARM (CPU part 0xd87)
setup: maya-maya-s-v2-iq2_xxs.json, context 131072, GPUs [0]
decode (writing the answer): mean 16.8 tokens/s over 3 answers of 256 tokens (greedy, thinking off)
prefill (reading the prompt): 597 tokens/s, 2048 tokens
prefill (reading the prompt): 730 tokens/s, 8192 tokens
```

The engine's own lines for the same run:

```
glm stat: decode 59.34 ms/tok (16.85 tok/s) | vram hit 99.97% | ram fetches 0.00/tok | disk 0.09 reads/tok
```

Every expert sits in the pool (12 096 of 12 096), so a token needs no disk and no RAM: the experts are read into the
pool at 8.7 GB/s at the start (86 GB in 10 s), and the decode is bound by the kernels.

These numbers reproduce the earlier DGX Spark row in the README's [speed table](../README.md#how-fast-is-it) (16.5 tokens/s, ~730 tokens/s on a 6K
prompt, reported by @ericoh88 on v1.1.15); the row above it is this build, at 21.1 tokens/s with the drafter.

## Drafting (DFlash2) and several requests at once

The pack this setup uses (Maya-S-v2-IQ2_XXS) has no MTP draft block, so a plain install decodes token by token. Two
ways to get more out of the machine were measured here. **They are mutually exclusive**: with `--parallel` above 1 the
several-conversation scheduler decodes without the drafter (`src/program/generate.cpp`: `glm dflash: not used with
MAYA_GLM_SEQS=%d`), so each lives in its own config and only one runs at a time.

### DFlash2 - the default config

`./maya.sh --setup --draft dflash2 --no-vision` downloads the drafter (0.7 GB,
[on Hugging Face](https://huggingface.co/Anbeeld/GLM-5.3-Flash-DFlash2-GGUF); license CC BY-NC-ND 4.0, non-commercial
use only) and writes `--dflash <GGUF>` into the config's `"args"`. A GB10 (sm_121) is eligible - DFlash2 is offered on
NVIDIA from sm_80 (RTX 30 / A100) on. The setup then runs the speed calibration, which measured both sides here (the
model loaded twice, 1536 tokens a side after a warm-up, the config's own sampling):

| | sustained (tokens/s) | |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| no drafting | 16.1 | |
| DFlash2 | 17.4 | +8%, 92% of the drafts accepted |

`./maya.sh --bench` (greedy, the engine alone, thinking off): **decode mean 21.1 tokens/s** over three 256-token
answers against 16.8 without it (+26%); prefill is unchanged (614 / 732 tokens/s at 2048 / 8192 against 597 / 730) -
the drafter only changes decode. The greedy gain is the larger one because every draft is then accepted. The drafter
costs nothing here: the pool is sized from `MemAvailable`, and every expert still fits beside it (12 096 of 12 096,
the RAM tier staging only):

```
glm dflash: 5 layers, hidden 4096, 32 heads (8 kv), block 8, window 2048, selector top-16 rank 256,
            reads trunk layers 6,15,25,34,43; 717 MB on CUDA0
glm fast: CUDA0 all experts fit in the GPU pool; RAM tier is staging only
```

The result is kept in the config (`installer.speed_calibration`: the two speeds, the kept choice), so a start does not
measure again by itself; `./maya.sh --speed-calibration` measures again at any time (the GPUs to itself, 5-20
minutes).

### Several requests at once - the parallel-4 config

`--parallel 4` answers up to four requests at once: a token of each goes through the model together, so every dense
weight is read once for all of them. It is a second config, `maya-maya-s-v2-iq2_xxs-parallel4.json` (the same
arguments, `--parallel 4`, no `--dflash` - the drafter is not used with it - and its own engine log), started with:

```sh
MAYA_CONFIG=$PWD/maya-maya-s-v2-iq2_xxs-parallel4.json ./serve-maya.sh
```

Measured with the engine's own multi bench (`STRATA_GLM_MULTI_BENCH=4,512,256`: four synthetic 512-token prompts,
256 greedy tokens each, sequence 0 alone first as the reference):

| | tokens/s | |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| one request alone | 17.2 | |
| four at once, together | 30.9 | 1.80x in all, 7.7 each |
| the tokens | 256 of 256 the same | each request gets what it would get alone |

Each extra request keeps its own context state, taken from the expert pool (the start log's `glm multi:` line gives
the size): with four running, the VRAM hit was 99.8% and a token saw 0.67 disk reads, against 99.97% and 0.09 for one
- a few more experts live outside the pool, which is what the aggregate gain is paid from.

### Which one to run

- One request at a time (a chat, an agent, `opencode`): the default config - DFlash2 drafts, 21.1 tokens/s greedy.
- Several at once (agents with workers, several people): the parallel-4 config - 30.9 tokens/s in all for four, no
  drafting (a request alone still runs at 17.2).
