- `./serve-maya.sh stop` before switching: the two configs are the same model, so only one can hold the GPU.

## What was tried on this machine (the enhancement log)

| | | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DFlash2 drafter | **done** | +8% sampled / +26% greedy decode, the same answers; the default config drafts now |
| `--parallel 4` | **done** | 1.80x for four at once, identical tokens; the second config |
| Vision | not used | `--no-vision`: the encoder builds for sm_121, but its 1.1 GB of files and ~320 MB on the GPU buy nothing here |
| The 27-29 s load | skipped | agreed not worth the work: only the first start is slower, the steady state is not |
| Prefill at short prompts (0.96x at 256-512 tokens) | skipped | the advantage was uncertain, and the work is engine-level |
| The Q2_0 and EXL3 kernels on NEON | skipped | they unlock other packs on ARM, not this setup's |

## Still missing

- **The canonical Q2_0 pack** is x86-only. Maya-S-v2-IQ2_XXS (a native pack) works; a Q2_0 pack does not, and the
  engine says so at startup instead of failing later.
- **The EXL3 CPU lane** is x86-only, so the DeepSeek EXL3 quants have no CPU lane here: their expert kernels are
  AVX2/AVX-512. The setup refuses them on an aarch64 host and points at the GGUF quant instead:

  ```
  [X]  DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017's EXL3 quant needs the x86 expert kernels (AVX2/AVX-512), which this ARM CPU does not
       have
       set up its GGUF instead: ./maya.sh --setup --model DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 --ds4-quant gguf-q8
  ```
- **The image encoder** builds for aarch64 (`tools/vision`, CUDA sm_121) and its files exist, but this setup
  deliberately leaves it out (`--no-vision`): 1.1 GB of files and ~320 MB on the GPU for pictures this box does not
  need. Drop the flag and re-run the setup to have it.
- **Allocation failures no longer end the engine** (the CHANGELOG's Unreleased section has the details): a failure
  in the prompt path ends that request with an error (503) and the next one runs, and where the layout does not fit
  the prompt is read again with a smaller chunk (down to 256 tokens). On a unified device that matters most - an
  out-of-memory moment no longer costs the ~40 s reload and the expert cache rebuild.
- **`ple_parity`** (a C++ test) needs the maintainer's fixtures - the Qwen3.8-Flash-Next GGUF and
  `bench/micro/ple_*.bin` - and fails without them on any machine, not only here.

## Troubleshooting

**"has no room for the expert pool"** - the pool came out too small for one route's experts in every layer. On a
unified device that means the RAM was not free: close what holds it, or lower `MAYA_GLM_RAM_HEADROOM_GB` (16 GB by
default here) or `--max-context`.

**The pool is smaller than expected** - it is sized from the free RAM at the start, so anything else on the machine
is subtracted. The start log says what it took (`pool budget ... GB`); compare it with `MemAvailable` in
`/proc/meminfo`.

**The decode is slower than the table says** - check the engine's `glm stat:` line. `vram hit` below 99% means the
experts are not all resident (the pool was too small), and `cpu lane` above zero means the CPU is computing experts
it should not.

**The build fails in ggml** - check the `-march=` line in the configure output. It comes from `/proc/cpuinfo`'s
feature list; a CPU without `i8mm` gets a smaller one.


# The DGX Spark build: what we changed, where it comes from, what others did

*The aarch64 port for the NVIDIA DGX Spark (GB10) and Grace, by carlos patriawan
([@xcpatr9231975](https://github.com/xcpatr9231975)), 2026-10-24.*

This page documents that work: **what every component is** and **where it comes from**, **what other people did**
that it builds on, and **what the diff actually is** - file by file, with the reason for each change.

The base is Project Maya, and upstream's **v1.1.27** is merged in (commit `3ed58e6`), so this branch is a
fast-forward of upstream's `main` plus the additions below. **43 files changed, +1960 / -132** over the v1.1.26
base; the merge itself brought their 26 files and was re-tested (68/69 C++ tests, 369 server, 395 tools, and the
benchmark re-measured at 21.2 tokens/s against 21.1 before it).

This pull request carries the two code changes and their tests and docs:

| | |
| --- | --- |
| **The aarch64 port** | the engine builds and runs on aarch64 (GB10, Grace); x86-64 is unchanged |
| **Allocation-failure recovery** | an out-of-memory moment in the prompt path ends the request, not the process |

---

## 1. What the machine is, and what the model is

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Machine | one NVIDIA DGX Spark: GB10 GPU (compute capability 12.1, `sm_121`), 20 ARM cores, **120 GB of RAM shared with the GPU** |
| Software | Ubuntu 24.04 aarch64, driver 580.95.05, CUDA 13.0 (nvcc 13.0.88) |
| Model | `GLM-5.3-Flash-Maya-S-v2-IQ2_XXS`, 89.83 GiB, 320.76 B parameters (18 B active), 46 layers, 288 experts (8 used), 1M-token context, run at 131072 |
| Result | 21.1 tokens/s decoding with the DFlash2 drafter (16.8 without it), 769 tokens/s reading a 16K-token prompt, every expert resident, 99.97% cache hit |

The one thing that makes a DGX Spark different from every machine Maya ran on before: **the GPU has no VRAM of its
own**. Its memory is the system's RAM, so the expert pool, the prompt buffers, the pinned tiers and the desktop all
draw on the same 120 GB. That single fact drives most of the port.

---

## 2. Where every component comes from

Maya's engine is a C++/CUDA program built from several independent code bases. This is what each one is and what it
provides; the licence notices stay with every copy (`third_party/*/LICENSE`).

### What Maya's own code is (`src/`, `serve/`, `tools/`, `ref/`)

| Component | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| `src/core/`, `src/kernels/` | **Maya's own engine**: the GLM-5.3-Flash (llama.cpp's `glm5-next` architecture) implementation, the expert pool with its VRAM / RAM / SSD tiers, the layer split, MTP and speculative decoding, the verify windows, the prompt (prefill) path |
| `src/program/generate.cpp` | the engine's command line and its `--serve` protocol (`GEN` / `GENI` / `DONE` / `ERR` over stdin/stdout) |
| `serve/server.py` | **Maya's own HTTP server**: the OpenAI- and Anthropic-compatible API, the dashboard, the streaming, the request queue |
| `tools/` | the packer (`iq_pack.py`), the installer (`maya.py`, `setup.py`), the benchmark, the parity tests |
| `ref/glm.py`, `ref/load.py` | **Maya's own Python reference forward pass** for the architecture, one op at a time - the specification the C++ kernels are parity-tested against (it is written from the upstream snapshot below) |

Maya's engine started from **Strata** ([Niko1221/Strata](https://github.com/Niko1221/Strata), MIT) and was rewritten
for GLM-5.3-Flash; its server and dashboard grew from Strata's too.

### What is taken from other projects

| Component | Where from | What it provides here |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `third_party/llama.cpp` | [ggml-org/llama.cpp](https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp), MIT, **pinned at `3cf03257`** (setup.py's `LLAMA_CPP_COMMIT`) | the quantization formats (IQ2_XXS, IQ4_NL, Q2_0, ...), **ggml-cpu's dot products**, the **prompt path's MMQ kernels** (`src/prefill/` compiles a subset of ggml-cuda with them), `mtmd` for the vision encoder, and ggml's build |
| `third_party/ggml` | the same project, MIT | a **patched copy** of llama.cpp's ggml-cuda sources, with `mmq-fused-swiglu.patch` (the fused gate/up + SwiGLU product) applied at configure time |
| `third_party/exllamav3` | [turboderp-org/exllamav3](https://github.com/turboderp-org/exllamav3), MIT | the EXL3 format, its codebooks, the CPU expert kernel and the tensor-core decoding of a tile |
| `third_party/beellama.cpp` | [Anbeeld/beellama.cpp](https://github.com/Anbeeld/beellama.cpp), MIT | the DFlash2 drafter's forward pass |
| `ref/upstream-llama.cpp/` | **llama.cpp master @ `272aad8b9`** (fetched 2026-09-30), MIT | a read-only snapshot of upstream's `glm5-next.cpp` and friends - the specification `ref/glm.py` transcribes. Documentation only: it does not compile outside llama.cpp and must not drift into `third_party/ggml` |
| `third_party/wheels/` | PyPI packages | the setup screen's Python packages, installed offline |

### The model files

- **GLM-5.3-Flash** by zai-org (MIT, Hugging Face) - the released weights Maya's quant and the image encoder are made
  from.
- **Maya-S-v2-IQ2_XXS** - the quant used here: 89.83 GiB across three GGUF shards, packed by `tools/iq_pack.py` into
  `pack/` (the dense weights, the native expert blobs and the tokenizer).
- The optional **DFlash2 drafter** ([Anbeeld's GGUF](https://huggingface.co/Anbeeld/GLM-5.3-Flash-DFlash2-GGUF), CC
  BY-NC-ND 4.0, non-commercial) - downloaded only when chosen; it is what the server running on this machine uses
  (`--dflash`).

---

## 3. What other people did

This build stands on work by many people. The order below is "what this build needed, and whose it was".

**The project itself.** Project Maya is created and led by **Peasant Smith** ([@mw00](https://github.com/mw00)), who
owns the project, the name and the Maya models. **@needmorevram** is the co-maintainer and contributed the parts this
build leans on hardest: **the expert tiers across VRAM, RAM and SSD** (the pool our port sizes differently), **the
CPU expert kernels**, the expert profile, the layer split, KV streaming, conversation checkpoints, MTP decoding and
DFlash2 drafting.

**The AMD (HIP) port, and the unified-memory policy.** Before this build, Maya already ran on a machine whose GPU
memory is the system's RAM: **AMD's Ryzen AI Max / Strix Halo APUs on Linux** (the HIP path, measured and
documented in `docs/AMD_MAYA.md`). That port established the policy this build reuses - size the expert pool from
`/proc/meminfo`'s `MemAvailable` rather than the runtime's free figure, because on a unified device the runtime
counts the model's page cache as used. Our change lifts that policy from "AMD only" to "any device whose memory is
the system's, including a GB10". Contributors to the AMD path are credited across the CHANGELOG (@rkarp, @izabera,
@Gazelle221B, @pepuscz and others).

**The DGX Spark measurement.** The README's [benchmark table](../README.md#how-fast-is-it) already carried a DGX Spark row - **16.5 tokens/s,
~730 tokens/s on a 6K prompt, reported by @ericoh88 on v1.1.15 (#282)**. Our measurement (16.8 tokens/s, 730 tokens/s
on an 8K prompt) reproduces it on the same hardware, which is what makes the port's numbers trustworthy.

**llama.cpp's own implementation of this architecture.** Upstream llama.cpp master carries `glm5-next` - the same
GLM-5.3-Flash architecture - so it can run the same GGUF. We built it for `sm_121a` on this machine and measured it:
it was the baseline of this port's measurements (Maya reads a prompt up to 3.2x faster there; the decode is a tie at
a short context and 3-7% faster at a long one); the DGX Spark rows are in the README's [speed tables](../README.md#how-fast-is-it). The snapshot in `ref/upstream-llama.cpp` is that
implementation, and it is also the specification `ref/glm.py` follows.

**The community's model work.** The GGUF this runs is a community quant of zai-org's weights; the speed reports in
the README's table come from dozens of people on every kind of GPU.

---

## 4. What the diff is, and why

### 4.1 The aarch64 port (`fde5e98`, 28 files)

The engine's fast expert kernels are x86 (AVX-512 and AVX2). On an aarch64 CPU they cannot run, and code compiled for
them would execute an illegal instruction. The port leaves them out and routes the work through ggml-cpu's own NEON
paths instead.

| File | Change | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `include/strata/platform/cpu_relax.hpp` | **new**: `strata_cpu_pause()` and `strata_store_fence()` | the doorbell spin used `_mm_pause` and the store fence `_mm_sfence`, both x86-only. aarch64 takes `yield` and `dmb oshst` - the outer-shareable store barrier is what makes host stores visible to a coherent device before the doorbell it polls |
| `src/core/graph.cpp`, `session.cpp`, `verify.cpp`, `src/kernels/cpu/pool.cpp` | use the two helpers above | four spin loops and three store fences, all previously x86-only |
| `src/kernels/cpu/portable.cpp` | **new**: stubs for every x86 kernel (Q2_0, i-quant, k-quant, EXL3, the IQ4_NL multi-token kernel) | a build must not silently compute something else. Each stub prints what is missing and stops; the engine refuses a Q2_0 pack at startup (`cpu_require_expert_support()`) instead of crashing at token 4000 |
| `include/strata/kernels/cpu/expert_layout.hpp`, `src/kernels/cpu/expert_layout.cpp` | `q2_own_kernels()`; the AVX-512 checks answer "no" on non-x86 | the same refusal, and no CPUID on ARM |
| `src/kernels/cpu/native_expert.cpp`, `src/core/expert_source.cpp` | gate the Q2_0 and IQ4_NL kernels on x86 | the native packs go through ggml-cpu's NEON dot products instead |
| `src/ds4/ds4_iq_cpu.cpp` | `IQ_X86` guard around the timing/variant code | the AVX-512 variants are x86-only |
| `src/core/glm_fast_path.cu`, `glm_fast_state.hpp` | **the unified-memory policy for CUDA**: a device whose memory is the system's sizes its pool from `MemAvailable`, and the RAM-tier headroom is 16 GB there | the runtime's free figure counts the model's page cache as used - it read ~2 GB free with the 90 GB pack cached, so the pool came out at 5 slots a layer and the start failed. `cudaDevAttrIntegrated` is 1 on a GB10, so this is now a device property, not an AMD-only branch |
| `CMakeLists.txt` | `STRATA_CPU_X86`; the x86-only sources, flags, tests and benchmarks gated on it; `GGML_NATIVE` off on ARM with `GGML_CPU_ARM_ARCH` | the x86 kernels are compiled with `-mavx512f...`; on aarch64 ggml-cpu needs its own arch string |
| `setup.py` | `ARM`, `arm_cpu_arch()` (from `/proc/cpuinfo`'s features), `cpu_info()`, and `unified_memory_pci_ids()` (a GPU with no memory BAR of its own) | the installer must recognise the machine, pick ggml's `-march` (`armv8.6-a+dotprod+i8mm+fp16` on the GB10) and report "120 GB shared with the system" instead of "120 GB of VRAM" |
| `maya.py` | the ARM branch in `check_pc`, the GPU label, and `-DGGML_CPU_ARM_ARCH` passed to the engine and vision builds | the setup's checks and messages |
| `tools/vision/CMakeLists.txt` | the same ggml arch handling | the image encoder builds for aarch64 too (verified: `build-vision/bin/maya-vision`) |
| `src/kernels/cpu/expert_multi_test.cpp`, `src/platform/memory_test.cpp`, `tests/exl3_test.cu` | skip where the kernel or the lock limit is missing | a test that cannot run must say so instead of failing |
| `serve/server.py` | `STRATA_HTTP_MAX_CONNECTIONS` (64) and `STRATA_HTTP_READ_TIMEOUT_S` (30 s) | a stuck client held a handler for ever; a stalled body is now a 408 and an over-limit request a 503 with `Retry-After: 1` |
| `serve/test_server.py` | tests for both limits | |
| `serve-maya.sh` | **new**: the local entrypoint - start / stop / status / logs | and it *finds a server it did not start*, which the pid file alone did not |
| `docs/SETTINGS.md` | the unified-memory paragraph, the headroom row, the two new server settings | |

**Measured on the result:** 16.8 tokens/s, 730 tokens/s on an 8K prompt, all 12096 experts resident, 99.97% cache
hit, a 42-second restart - matching @ericoh88's reported numbers on the same hardware.

### 4.2 The bring-up documentation and the EXL3 refusal (`8545eed`)

- `docs/DGX_SPARK.md` (**new**, 214 lines): the machine, the setup, running it, what the port changed, how the pool
  is sized on a unified device, the measured numbers, what is still missing, troubleshooting.
- `maya.py`: the setup now **refuses the DeepSeek EXL3 quants on an ARM CPU** (their expert kernels are x86-only),
  offering the GGUF quant instead - so nobody downloads ~300 GB of weights that cannot run.
- `README.md`, `docs/SETTINGS.md`: links.

### 4.3 The benchmark (`7ca21cf`)

- `tools/bench_tokens.py` (**new**): a sweep of the sizes the standard `--bench` does not do - the prompt read at
  256-32768 tokens with the time to its first token, and the answer written at 32-1024 tokens after a prompt of a
  given size. It drives the engine alone, the way the dashboard starts it.
- `BENCHMARK.md` (**new**): the numbers on this machine, and **the same GGUF on the same GPU with upstream
  llama.cpp** (`272aad8b9`, built here for `sm_121a`). Prefill: 754 vs 239 tokens/s at 32K tokens (**3.16x**).
  Decode: a tie at a short context (17.4 vs 17.7) and 1.03-1.07x Maya's way as the context grows (upstream loses
  ~12% of its rate between an empty context and 2K tokens, Maya ~4%).

### 4.4 Allocation-failure recovery (`e6f317c`, 16 files)

This one is not aarch64-specific: it is the "known gap" `docs/SETTINGS.md` had documented.

**What was broken.** An out-of-memory moment in the prompt path ended the *process*: the prefill's kernel launches
(28 sites), the MMQ kernels (8) and the prefill GEMMs called `std::exit(1)`, and the MMQ scratch pool's `cudaMalloc`
went through `CUDA_CHECK` into `std::abort()`. The client got a 503, the next request waited for the engine to load
again, and the expert cache had to be rebuilt from disk. Separately, `prefill_carve` carved its layout **past the
lendable region and only printed a warning** - an out-of-bounds pointer.

| File | Change |
| --- | --- |
| `include/strata/prefill/failure.hpp` | **new**: `strata::prefill::Failure` (a `std::runtime_error`), `fail()`, and `is_allocation_failure()` |
| `src/prefill/kernels.cu`, `moe_mmq.cu`, `gemm.cu` | every `std::exit(1)` becomes a thrown `Failure` |
| `src/prefill/ggml_cuda_host.cu` | the scratch pool's `cudaMalloc` failure throws (with the bytes it wanted and the bytes free) instead of aborting |
| `src/core/glm_prefill.cu` | `prefill_carve` returns false when the layout does not fit; `Glm5Model::prefill` reads the prompt again with a **smaller chunk** (down to 256 tokens) when it does not; `prefill_run` is guarded so the expert pool's tail is given back either way |
| `src/program/generate.cpp` | `guarded()` catches a request's failure; `cuda_context_broken()` still ends the engine for a sticky CUDA error; the serve loops end the *request* (not the process) and lower the chunk for the requests that follow |
| `src/prefill/prefill.cpp` | the same chunk-halving retry for the borrowed layout, with the `STRATA_PREALLOC_FAIL` test hook |
| `serve/server.py`, `serve/test_server.py` | a memory failure is a **503** (the client can try again), a wrong request still a **400** |
| `src/prefill/failure_test.cpp`, `tools/test_alloc_recovery.py` | **new tests**: the classification (no GPU), and both injected failures against the real engine - the request fails, the engine answers the next one |

Three `std::exit(1)` sites are deliberately left, because none of them is a memory failure: an MMQ type this build
does not cover (`moe_mmq.cu`), a build without the fused gate/up product (same file), and ggml's own init-time CUDA
checks (`ggml_cuda_error`, reachable only from `ggml_cuda_info` / `ggml_cuda_get_device` at start-up). The AMD-only
invariant checks in `moe_fused_rdna4.cu` are untouched.

---

## 5. How to verify any of this

```sh
git log --oneline f26d8ba..HEAD      # the four commits
git diff --stat f26d8ba..HEAD        # 43 files, +1960 / -132

./maya.sh --check                    # the PC checks: the GB10, CUDA 13, the ARM CPU, 120 GB shared
./serve-maya.sh start                 # ~42 s to load and warm the tiers
curl http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/models

./serve-maya.sh stop
.venv/bin/python tools/bench_tokens.py --out BENCHMARK.md   # the sweep in BENCHMARK.md
.venv/bin/python -m unittest tools.test_alloc_recovery      # the recovery, on the real engine
```

The C++ tests (`build-tests`, `ctest`): 68 of 69 pass; `ple_parity` needs the maintainer's fixtures
(the Qwen3.8-Flash-Next GGUF and `bench/micro/ple_*.bin`), which no machine has outside the project. The Python
tests: 392 tools and 360 server tests pass.

## 6. What is still missing

- **Images**: the encoder compiles for aarch64, but its 1.1 GB of files were not downloaded on the machine this was
  brought up on - that install is text only.
- **`ple_parity`** needs the maintainer's fixtures (see above).
- The **canonical Q2_0 pack** and the **DeepSeek EXL3 quants** are x86-only (the setup refuses EXL3 on ARM now; a
  Q2_0 pack is refused at the engine's startup).



# A Docker image of Maya

There is no official Maya image yet. This page is a recipe to build one from a release:
[`docker/Dockerfile`](../docker/Dockerfile) and [`docker/build.sh`](../docker/build.sh), which builds the image, checks
it and, if you ask, pushes it. Back to the [README](../README.md).

```bash
git checkout v1.1.54
docker/build.sh                 # project-maya:1.1.54-cuda13: RTX 20 / 30 / 40 / 50, A100, the A-series
docker/build.sh --cuda 12       # project-maya:1.1.54-cuda12: the same, plus the V100
```

Almost all of a build's time is compiling the engines. `--archs "86"` builds for one kind of card only, so it compiles
less. A rebuild for the next release recompiles only what changed: the Dockerfile puts ccache in front of the
compilers and keeps its cache in Docker's build cache on the machine that builds, between builds
(`docker builder prune -a` clears it). `docker/build.sh --help` lists the options.

## What the image holds

- **The three engines**, compiled the way `maya.py`'s setup compiles them, where the setup puts them:
  `build/maya` (GLM-5.3-Flash and Qwen-3.8-Flash-Next), `build/maya-ds4` (DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017) and `build/maya-mm3`
  (MiniMax-M3.1-Flash).
- **The release's tree** in `/opt/maya`: the server (`serve/server.py`), the pack builders (`tools/iq_pack.py`,
  `tools/ds4/pack_gguf.py`, `tools/mm3/pack_gguf.py`) and the data files they use (the expert profiles, MiniMax's chat
  template). The tests, the benches, `ref/` and the bundled wheels are left out.
- **llama.cpp's `gguf-py`** in `third_party/llama.cpp/`, where `setup.py` puts it: the pack builders read the tokenizer
  with it.
- **A `.venv`** with `setup.py`'s `PY_PACKAGES` less `cmake` and `ninja`. The list is read from the release's own
  `setup.py` when the image is built, so a package a release adds comes with it. The list and the versions pip took are
  in `/opt/maya/image/`.
- **`MAYA_UPDATE_CHECK=0`**: the dashboard's self-update is a `git` fast-forward, which an image can't do. Build the
  next release instead.

**Text only:** the vision encoder (`maya-vision`) and the DFlash2 drafter are not built or fetched.

## How it's built

### A portable CPU build

The Dockerfile passes `-DSTRATA_PORTABLE=ON`, which `maya.py` passes only for its HIP build. Without it the CMake sets
`GGML_NATIVE=ON`, which compiles ggml's CPU code for the build machine's own CPU. That's right for a PC that compiles
its own engine and wrong for an image: an image built on an AVX-512 machine stops with an illegal instruction on a CPU
without AVX-512, a Ryzen 5000 for example. With it, ggml's CPU code targets an AVX2 baseline. Maya's own AVX-512
kernels are compiled per file and picked at run time either way.

So the image needs an **x86-64 CPU with AVX2**.

### The CUDA architectures

The image is a fat binary: each engine carries compiled code for each architecture in the list, plus its PTX.

| `--cuda` | Base | Architectures (`CMAKE_CUDA_ARCHITECTURES`) | Cards | NVIDIA driver |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `13` (the default) | CUDA 13.0 | `75;80;86;89;120` | RTX 20 / 30 / 40 / 50, A100, the RTX A-series, RTX PRO 6000 | 580 or newer |
| `12` | CUDA 12.9 | `70;75;80;86;89;120` | the same, plus the **V100** (CUDA 13 can't compile for Volta) | 575 or newer |

- **A card newer than the list**, for example an H100 (`90`), has no compiled code of its own. The driver compiles the
  PTX of the newest listed architecture below it when the engine starts. That works in principle, but nobody has run
  it. `--archs` adds an architecture: `--archs "75;80;86;89;90;120"`.
- **`--cuda 12` is the toolkit `maya.py` itself picks** for a PC with a V100 and a Blackwell card: 12.8 or newer for
  Blackwell, older than 13 for Volta.
- **Not covered:**
  - Pascal (`60`, `61`), experimental in Maya. CUDA 12 can compile for it (`--cuda 12 --archs "61;70;…"`), but no
    such image was built here.
  - AMD and Apple: the HIP and Metal builds aren't part of this recipe.
  - ARM64, such as a DGX Spark or Grace: the image is x86-64.

### The checks before a push

`docker/build.sh` builds from `git archive <ref>`, so local changes and untracked files never reach the image. It stops
at the first failed check, before anything is pushed. None of the checks needs a GPU.

| Check | What it catches |
|---|---|
| `setup.py`'s llama.cpp pin (`LLAMA_CPP_COMMIT`) is the one `CMakeLists.txt` names | the two pins disagreeing (the image builds from `setup.py`'s, as `maya.py` does) |
| `VERSION` is the release, when `--ref` is a `vX.Y.Z` tag | a tag on a tree whose `VERSION` wasn't bumped (v1.1.21 shipped an empty one) |
| `CMakeCache.txt`: `STRATA_PORTABLE` on, `GGML_NATIVE` off, `GGML_AVX2` on, no AVX-512, VNNI or AMX | a native build slipping into the image |
| the architectures in `CMakeCache.txt` are the ones asked for | a build for the wrong cards |
| `ldd` on each engine: everything resolves except the host driver's `libcuda` and `libnvidia-*` | a library missing from the runtime image |
| `serve/server.py --help` and each pack builder's `--help` run | an import or a package the server needs missing |
| the expert profiles (`data/expert-profile-*.bin`) and `tools/mm3/chat_template.jinja` are there | a data file the setups pass to the engines left out |
| `gguf-py` imports the way the tools find it, and `tools/iq_pack.py` indexes `data/glm5-synth.gguf` | a pack builder that can't run in the image |
| every `PY_PACKAGES` entry in the image's own `setup.py` is installed at a version it accepts | a package a release adds, missing from the image (v1.1.18's `jsonschema`, which the Responses API's structured output needs) |

`docker/build.sh --check-only <image>` runs the same checks on an image built before. Give it the same `--cuda` and
`--archs`.

## Running it

The image has no entrypoint. Its default command prints `serve/server.py --help`.

**The setup doesn't run in it.** `./maya.sh` checks for the CUDA toolkit (`nvcc`) and `g++` before anything else. The
image is built on NVIDIA's runtime image, which has neither, so the setup stops there, even though the engines are
already compiled. The setup also writes its configs next to `maya.py`, inside the container.

What the image runs is the server, with a pack and a config made for it. A config names absolute paths: `exe`, `cwd`,
the pack in `args`, `tokenizer` and `log`. In the container they have to be the container's: `/opt/maya/build/<engine>`,
`/opt/maya`, and wherever the packs are mounted. A config written by a setup on the host points at the host's paths.
Mount the files of one of the models the setup offers (`./maya.sh --setup` asks which model and which quant;
[MODELS.md](MODELS.md) has each one's files) and a folder for the packs and configs, then start the server with
`--host 0.0.0.0`, so the port reaches the host. `--ulimit memlock=-1` is needed because the engines pin (lock) the
experts they copy into RAM for the GPUs:

```bash
docker run --rm --gpus all --ulimit memlock=-1 -p 8080:8080 \
  -v /path/to/models:/models:ro -v maya-data:/data \
  project-maya:1.1.54-cuda13 \
  /opt/maya/.venv/bin/python /opt/maya/serve/server.py --engine maya --config /data/<model>/<config>.json \
    --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8080
```

For `./maya.sh` to set up inside the container, an image would need the CUDA toolkit and `g++` (NVIDIA's devel image
instead of the runtime one) and a place outside `/opt/maya` for the configs. It would also have to write
`build/MAYA-BUILD.json`, so the setup finds the compiled engines and doesn't compile them again. That would be the next
step towards an official image.


# Refreshing DS4 startup expert placement

On a three-GPU AMD machine, refreshing the startup expert profile raised held-out
128-token code generation from **11.3 to 18.0 tokens/s** on the first response and
**14.6 to 23.9 tokens/s** on the repeated response. First-response expert VRAM hit
rate rose from **42.3% to 75.5%**. This changes which experts start in the existing
VRAM slots; it adds no VRAM and changes no model weights, kernels, or routing decisions.

The persisted profile accumulates counts. In this experiment, 919,740,960 historical
selections overwhelmed 975,360 recent calibration selections. The tool replaces that
large historical influence with a per-layer blend: recent counts plus a historical
prior normalized to one quarter of the recent total (approximately 80% recent,
20% historical after rounding). It preserves the recent count scale so future saved
observations can influence the profile. Live adaptive cache replacement remains on;
this is a startup/persisted-profile improvement, not a replacement for live learning.

## Relationship to existing profile tools

Upstream already records and saves DS4 expert counts. `tools/ds4/profile.py make`
normalizes and combines saved profiles with equal input weights and a configurable
output count scale; `compare` checks overlap in their hottest experts. Those tools
remain useful when each input already represents the workload you want to retain.

This utility handles calibration runs that **start from an accumulated profile**:
it subtracts each run's starting counts before combining recent observations, then
adds a separately normalized historical prior. Passing cumulative snapshots directly
to `profile.py make` would retain the historical selections inside every snapshot.
Starting calibration from empty profiles and using the existing tool is another
option. This utility adds no automatic aging or change to runtime defaults.

## Safe calibration recipe

This tool handles the DS4 engine's **raw little-endian uint32 count array**, not the
STRP ranking files handled by `make_profile.py`. Supply the actual saved geometry:
trunk-only or trunk plus MTP rows, with the model's experts per row. Files must all
have exactly that geometry. The experiment used 40 rows of 384 experts and no drafter.

1. Back up the original profile while the engine is stopped. Work on copies.
2. Copy the same starting profile to `before.bin` and `calibration.bin`. Start the
   engine with `--expert-profile calibration.bin`; run representative requests.
   Shut down normally so counts are saved, then copy it to `after.bin`.
3. For an independent second run, reset `calibration.bin` to `before.bin`, repeat
   calibration, and save `after2.bin`. Do not combine overlapping cumulative deltas.
4. Write a new profile and its SHA-256 provenance receipt:

```sh
python3 tools/refresh_ds4_profile.py --layers 40 --experts 384 \
  --historical historical.bin --pair before.bin after.bin \
  --pair before.bin after2.bin --recent-weight 0.8 --out refreshed.bin
```

5. Compare fresh engine starts using original versus refreshed copies on held-out
   prompts. Reverse test order to check warming effects. Select the new profile
   explicitly with `--expert-profile` only after validation; retain the old profile
   for rollback. Engine saves will mutate the selected profile, so keep the seed copy.

The tool refuses existing output/receipt paths, decreasing counters (reset/wrap),
missing observations in any layer, incorrect file sizes, and uint32 overflow.
An unobserved historical layer contributes no prior. Calibration includes prefill
and decode counts; use short prompts and longer outputs for a decode-heavy profile.
No automatic refresh or runtime default changes are included.

## Measurement scope

Calibration used three synthetic tasks (LRU code, database tutorial, observatory
story), twice, each with a 128-output-token primer and two 256-token responses.
About 94% of calibration tokens were decode. Confirmation used different code,
technical, and prose prompts, 128 output tokens per response, greedy decoding,
with normal adaptive caching and unchanged GPU/host budgets. Initial tests ran old
then recent; confirmation reversed the order. The reversed confirmation was:

| Held-out prompt | First response old → recent (tokens/s) | Repeat old → recent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Asyncio rate limiter | 11.27 → 18.00 | 14.59 → 23.87 |
| Virtual-memory tutorial | 15.29 → 20.69 | 20.11 → 24.51 |
| Colony trade dispute | 14.43 → 17.23 | 19.81 → 21.53 |

Hardware was two 32 GB R9700s plus a 16 GB 9070 XT, DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017
EXL3 3.5 bpw, TP=2 on the main pair with third-card expert caching, drafting off.
A subsequent real API smoke test gave 17.8/22.3 tokens/s on first/repeated code.
The historical/recent blended profile SHA-256 values were respectively
`ae12f5039f192289595267d8d87502ac4f010d14a80f4a173239890c3076bbf0` and
`419ff834edd0952e6ebc2dc260d436590e31b17ecede0b9d5bc8941aa2f743ab`.

These are small-sample, workload-specific results, not universal speed or quality
claims. Ranking and initial cache heat changed together. Free-running text can differ
as CPU/GPU expert assignments change; there is no cross-profile logit-parity claim
or broad quality evaluation. A single 7,935-token prompt also prefilled faster
(21.0 → 17.1 seconds), but traffic did not decrease, so no prefill mechanism or
repeatable prefill gain is claimed.


# Experimental EXL3 overflow expert execution

The helper keeps additional target-model experts on a GPU outside the target TP
group. When that group misses an expert, the helper executes its resident expert
while the CPU processes the remaining misses. Only activations and results cross
the helper link during decode; the helper weights remain resident.

This extends the existing GGUF helper path to uniform EXL3 layers, including
target layers sharded across two GPUs. The helper stores a complete expert;
the normal TP cache stores shards. Router selections and weights remain
authoritative. Moving a contribution from CPU to GPU changes floating-point
arithmetic, so bitwise output equivalence is not guaranteed.

## Selecting a helper and its budget

The native executable accepts the `DS4_*` names below; Maya's public settings
use the corresponding `MAYA_DS4_*` names. GPU IDs are visible IDs printed at
startup, after any visibility filtering. Budgets are explicit decimal GB;
there is no default GPU or pool size.

For a dedicated helper, choose a visible GPU outside the target's `--gpus`
selection. For example, with target GPUs 0 and 1 and helper GPU 2:

```sh
DS4_HELPER=2 DS4_HELPER_GB="$helper_gb" DS4_HELPER_EXL3=1 \
DS4_WARM_CAP_GB="$host_warm_gb" \
maya-ds4 --pack /path/to/pack --gpus 0,1 --tp 2
```

Set `helper_gb` and `host_warm_gb` to budgets suitable for the machine before
running this example. The helper executes existing router selections;
it does not predict experts or alter their routing weights.

## Sharing a helper GPU with DSpark

For three visible GPUs, target TP2 on GPUs 0 and 1, and DSpark on GPU 2,
also select GPU 2 and give its target group zero layers. Set `target_layers`
to the model's number of target layers (40 for the measured Flash model):

```sh
DS4_SPLIT="$target_layers,0" DS4_DRAFT_GPU=2 \
DS4_HELPER=2 DS4_HELPER_GB="$helper_gb" DS4_HELPER_EXL3=1 DS4_HELPER_SHARED=1 \
DS4_WARM_CAP_GB="$host_warm_gb" \
maya-ds4 --pack /path/to/pack --gpus 0,1,2 --tp 2 --dspark on
```

Shared mode requires loaded DSpark blocks, TP2, and zero target layers on the
helper. The loader reserves the requested helper pool before placing the
drafter, so subsequent memory estimates include that allocation. The example
uses decimal GB and requires enough remaining VRAM for drafter weights, scratch
space, context buffers, and the requested reserve. It is an experimental budget,
not an automatic recommendation for every model or GPU.

The measured three-R9700 configuration used `target_layers=40`, `helper_gb=20`
and `host_warm_gb=200`. These are benchmark settings, not new defaults or
universal recommendations. CPU worker count is independently configurable.
That configuration explicitly disables the recent-expert
cache with `DS4_RECENT=0`. The engine otherwise defaults to 32 recent slots on
eligible layers. That changes the primary/helper partition, RAM use, and copy
traffic; treat it as a separate tuning choice. Do not omit the setting when
reproducing the reported helper measurements.

Leave all helper settings unset to disable this feature. `DS4_HELPER_EXL3=1` is
required for EXL3. Mixed-format EXL3 layers are not selected for the helper.
The existing exclusive GGUF helper configuration remains available.
Upstream's dynamic helper slots (`DS4_HELPER_RECENT`) currently read GGUF
weights. EXL3 helpers use the static pool; a nonzero recent-slot count is
rejected at load rather than passed to the GGUF fill path.

CPU backing copies of helper experts are retained for fallback. This can
increase host RAM compared with the original placement. `DS4_WARM_CAP_GB`
controls warm copies in mmap mode; it does not cap all host allocations when
`--mmap-experts off` is used.

The helper uses the existing async CPU service path and its profile-based
selection of experts left outside the TP cache. This is resident overflow
execution, not predictive prefetching. Inspect `helper`, `helper_fb`,
`helper_ms`, `helper_held`, and `helper_dead` in STAT to confirm actual use.

## Validation

`DS4_HELPER_CHECK=1` reads back each uploaded helper expert and compares all
bytes. Before decode, it checks each represented EXL3 format against CPU
execution, compares grouped versus per-token GPU execution bitwise, and forces
the CPU fallback path with and without an existing CPU contribution. It uses
the existing helper tolerance of 1e-3 relative to each token's largest CPU
output. A failed execution check disables the helper; a failed upload byte
check fails loading. Do not interpret successful operator checks as broad
model-quality validation.

CPU fallback covers recoverable helper errors. A fatal error on a GPU also
hosting DSpark can affect the drafter, and is not guaranteed recoverable.

The optional HIP primitive test accepts a real-weight fixture and visible GPU:

```sh
cmake --build build-hip --target hip_ds4_exl3_helper
build-hip/hip_ds4_exl3_helper /path/to/full-layer.bin 2
```

Fixture header: 16 native-endian uint64 values: three K2 values, then
trellis/suh/svh byte offsets for each of three projections, full expert byte
size, expert count, embedding dimension (5120), and FFN dimension (2304).
The header is followed by full packed expert blobs; at least three are needed.
The test checks weight upload bytes, windows of 1–6 tokens, repeated experts,
unowned picks, and empty output rows. It needs external model weights, so it
is not registered as an unattended CTest.


# EXL3 prefill on HIP

The portable HIP implementations of `exl3::set_gate_up`, `set_down`, and `gemm_rows` reuse the existing eight-activation `block_gemv` decoder. They replace the previous no-op HIP branches. They use FP32 FMA arithmetic, not AMD matrix instructions; there is room for further optimization. The FP16 input adapter is separate from the original decode input structure. CUDA keeps its existing tensor-core implementation.

## In the setup

The setup offers DeepSeek's EXL3 quants on AMD (since v1.1.30), as it offers GLM's and MiniMax's EXL3 quants there: `--backend hip --model DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 --ds4-quant exl3` (or `exl3-3.5`) downloads, packs and configures them. The kernels pass `exl3_test` on gfx1201 and gfx1151. The manual steps below still work for a direct engine launch.

The EXL3 3.5bpw model has integer per-matrix K (3/4 for routed experts, 6 for most dense matrices). Its average bitrate does not require half-integer matrix encodings. Keep the original 1017 Engram shards beside the EXL3 shards; packing indexes the weights without copying them.

```sh
python tools/ds4/pack.py --model /path/to/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-EXL3-3.5bpw
# After building the HIP engine; this starts its raw token-ID serving protocol.
DS4_SPLIT=36,4 build-hip/maya-ds4 \
  --pack /path/to/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-EXL3-3.5bpw/maya-ds4 \
  --gpus 0,1,2 --tp 2 --max-context 262144 --reserve-mib 2048 \
  --mmap-experts off --prefill-chunk 2048 --vision off --dspark none --serve
```

The measured trial also enabled `DS4_SKIP_UNPICKED=1` from separate PR #297; this kernel change does not include or require that optimization to compile.

## Validation

On gfx1201 / ROCm 7.14.1, `exl3_test` passes with zero failures and zero launch errors. HIP now runs the previously skipped CPU-reference `gemm_rows` and grouped-expert tests, including empty experts, multiple eight-row groups and partial tails, instead of silently skipping those cases. Existing decode/window exactness tests also pass. In an appropriately configured HIP build:

```sh
cmake --build build-hip --target exl3_test maya-ds4 -j
ctest --test-dir build-hip -R '^exl3_test$' --output-on-failure
python tools/check_hip_compat.py
```

Full-model smoke tests produced coherent answers with short and fresh 1K-token prompts. This is not a full-model parity or quality evaluation.

## Practical setup measurements

Hardware: Ryzen 9950X, 256 GB RAM, two Radeon AI PRO R9700 32 GB cards plus a 16 GB RX 9070-family card, ROCm 7.14.1. TP2 pair plus singleton, `DS4_SPLIT=36,4`, context 262144, explicit RAM experts, reserve 2048 MiB. DSpark/profiling/flow-TP off.

| Fresh prompt | Previous Q8 setup | EXL3 setup |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1043 tokens A | 17.988 s | 8.063 s |
| 1043 tokens B | 16.698 s | 6.975 s |
| 2067 tokens C | 19.558 s | 10.654 s |

Same fixed synthetic prompts, one output token, temperature zero, reasoning none; every response reports `cache_n=0`. Repeated 256-token generation: 18.6 tok/s EXL3 versus 18.2 Q8 (first runs: 12.1 versus 13.4). These are individual measurements, not confidence intervals.

The Q8 setup used `abc09c7` plus PR #297's optimization; EXL3 used `8d9a9b5` plus that optimization and these kernels. Quant, expert profiles and default chunk sizes differ (Q8 8192, EXL3 2048). These compare usable setups, not an isolated quant-only or kernel-only A/B. Unmodified HIP has no valid grouped EXL3 prefill result to time.

EXL3 allocation: each R9700 has 3.02 GB dense weights and 26.85 GB experts; the third GPU has 1.04 GB dense weights and 12.40 GB experts. Host experts occupy 173.1 GB. Engram tables remain disk-backed.


# Configurable scalar EXL3 prompt tiles on RDNA4

A prompt often routes several tokens to the same expert. Larger scalar tiles unpack each compressed weight fragment once for more of those tokens, trading register/shared-memory occupancy for less repeated unpacking. The implementation keeps the current DOT2 sum order, four-lane reduction, rotations and FP16 rounding. It allocates no expanded-weight cache or extra persistent expert VRAM.

`MAYA_EXL3_HIP_SCALAR_ROWS=0` (default, or `8`) retains the original implementation. `12`, `16` and `24` select a fixed tile. `auto` selects gate/up's original 8 rows below `MAYA_EXL3_HIP_SCALAR_GU_MIN_ROWS` (48), otherwise 12; down uses 12 below `MAYA_EXL3_HIP_SCALAR_DOWN_MIN_ROWS` (128), otherwise 24. Each choice applies to one dispatched expert batch, using its existing `SetArgs.max_rows`; it is not an extra per-expert readback or learned predictor.

The cutoffs are configurable because occupancy and routed-row distributions differ between cards, model shapes and workloads. Capability is checked per visible GPU. Only gfx12, uniform integer 3/4-bit layouts with codebook 2 (mul1), on the scalar set path are selected. Other layouts, mixed expert sets and architectures retain the existing implementation. Existing matrix-kernel selection still wins. Decode and MTP-verification kernels are not changed. Native launches accept `STRATA_EXL3_HIP_SCALAR_*` and `EXL3_HIP_SCALAR_*` aliases too.

Example serving-config environment (explicit opt-in):

```json
{
  "MAYA_EXL3_HIP_SCALAR_ROWS": "auto",
  "MAYA_EXL3_HIP_SCALAR_GU_MIN_ROWS": "48",
  "MAYA_EXL3_HIP_SCALAR_DOWN_MIN_ROWS": "128"
}
```

## Measurements, 2026-10-28

On three R9700s, TP2 on two cards and a shared 20 GB helper/four-bit DSpark on the third, a matched **warm 4096-token prefill** screen measured:

| Mode | Two warm observations (ms) | Mean (ms) | Latency reduction |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Original 8 | 9331.26, 9339.47 | 9335.365 | baseline |
| Fixed 12 | 9118.02, 9148.37 | 9133.195 | 2.17% |
| Auto, cutoffs 48/128 | 9135.97, 9061.22 | 9098.595 | 2.54% |

One loaded model, same binary, fixed expert residency/profile, warm order 8/12/auto/auto/12/8 after parity passes 8/12/auto/8. Controls drifted 0.088%. All 48 captured full-vocabulary states across three prompts were exact, including repeat controls; only the 4K prompt exercised the new set kernels. Routing/payload statistics matched across all ten 4K passes, with no KFD eviction-counter increase during the warm bracket and no process swapping. DSpark weights were loaded but drafting was not timed. This is a small prefill result, not a decode, broad-workload or default-setting claim; two samples do not establish auto as significantly better than fixed 12.

That model integration used the immutable serving v1.1.29 model objects with only its EXL3 kernel object replaced. Its kernel baseline was the same current DOT2 eight-row arithmetic, **not** the earlier scalar-FMA16 experiment. The public implementation is ported onto v1.1.34 (`249f9a8`); capability/configuration handling is new in this port. Do not describe the model timing as a fresh full-model v1.1.34 benchmark.

A prior screen on the v1.1.34 kernel base compared 8/12/16/24/32 rows using archived real routed-count shapes and generated weights/gather indices. A simple policy was fitted on seven training batches and frozen before sixteen held-out batches were replayed with two gather patterns on each main GPU. Held-out sums of expert-stage times fell 16.8–17.2% for fixed 12 and 17.6–18.1% for auto. Those sums are not workload-frequency-weighted model throughput. All 504 checked operator cases matched exactly. Fixed 32 was about 18% slower on the held-out sets and required 80 bytes/thread of private memory in gate/up, so it is not exposed here. Fixed 24 sometimes improved down while regressing gate/up; a global bigger-is-better policy was rejected.

## Reproducing the public port

```sh
cmake --build build-hip --target maya-ds4 exl3_test exl3_hip_scalar_policy_test -j 4
./build-hip/exl3_hip_scalar_policy_test
HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0 ./build-hip/exl3_test --hip-scalar
HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0 ./build-hip/exl3_test --hip-scalar-bench
```

The policy test needs no GPU. The GPU test checks every integer bitrate/codebook, half-integer fallbacks, distinct gate/up/down formats, empty experts, nonzero row bases and tails around tile/threshold boundaries. Each public mode is compared with the original path byte for byte, including output guards; the original small cases retain the CPU-reference tolerance checks. The optional benchmark times combined gate/up and down at model-sized D5120/F1152, with synthetic weights and three expert slots (one empty), in balanced mode order. Its purpose is a small repeatable tuning screen, not a replacement for representative full-model measurements. Measure one GPU at a time on an idle engine.


# RDNA4 EXL3 matrix prefill

On HIP gfx12 devices, grouped EXL3 prefill can use matrix instructions for
`set_gate_up` and `set_down`: opt-in for now with `EXL3_WMMA_GFX12=1` (unset or `0`
keeps the portable path on gfx12); `EXL3_WMMA=0` disables matrix prefill on both gfx11 and gfx12.
`EXL3_WMMA_ROWS=16` (default) or `32` selects routed rows per block.

Earlier DeepSeek tests on three R9700s measured about 8–9% higher warm 4K
prefill throughput and 12% lower warm 16K prefill latency with this path.
Those whole-model measurements used an earlier integration;
they are not a speed guarantee for every model or machine.

## Guided setup

Use this with an EXL3 model and a HIP build containing this implementation,
compiled for the selected RDNA4 GPU (`gfx1201` for the R9700 / RX 9070 family).
It does not accelerate GGUF expert weights. A practical starting point is
16 routed rows per block; no particular GPU count or tensor-parallel layout
is required.

Turn it on with `EXL3_WMMA_GFX12=1`; 16 rows is the default tile. The explicit
settings below do that.

First check the GPUs with `./maya.sh --backend hip --check`. Stop any running
server before setting up again or editing its configuration. To save the
settings through setup, for example with DeepSeek's 3.5-bit EXL3 quant:

```sh
./maya.sh --backend hip --setup \
  --model DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 --ds4-quant exl3-3.5 \
  --env EXL3_WMMA_GFX12=1 --env EXL3_WMMA_ROWS=16 --env EXL3_WMMA=1 \
  --no-start
```

Keep your existing model-directory, GPU and context options when repeating
setup; choose your installed EXL3 quant if it differs from this example.
The example uses the normal setup workflow, which may build or download files;
it saves the configuration without starting the server. For an existing
installation, merging these entries into its configuration's `env` is enough:

```json
{
  "EXL3_WMMA_GFX12": "1",
  "EXL3_WMMA_ROWS": "16",
  "EXL3_WMMA": "1"
}
```

Preserve the other `env` entries. Use these exact `EXL3_*` names: the kernel
library reads them directly. The same names can be exported for a native
`maya-ds4` launch. They must be set before starting the engine.

### Verify and compare

1. Confirm setup selected a HIP build for your RDNA4 GPU. An older binary
   without this implementation cannot run the RDNA4 matrix path.
2. Check that the saved configuration contains the entries above, then start
   that configuration: `./maya.sh --config /path/to/maya-ds4-hip.json`.
   Use the actual config path printed by setup. A shell variable alone does
   not override a conflicting value already saved in the configuration.
3. To check kernel execution independently, build `exl3_test` and run the
   focused matrix tests below on an idle GPU. A saved flag alone is not proof
   that a request exercised the grouped EXL3 prefill path; short prompts may
   use the existing small-window kernels.
4. Compare representative long prompts with the same model, context, cache
   conditions and other settings. Record prefill and decode separately.
   For a portable-path control, set `EXL3_WMMA=0` in the same configuration
   and restart; keep the other settings fixed. Restore it to `1` and restart
   to re-enable matrix prefill. An environment change does not reconfigure
   an engine that is already loaded.

See [Setup](SETUP.md) for the installation workflow and
[Settings](SETTINGS.md#rdna4-exl3-matrix-prefill) for the switches.

## Implementation

The implementation decodes compressed weight tiles directly into matrix operand
registers, reusing each tile across the routed rows. It does not reconstruct the
whole expert or require a predecoded-weight cache. Activation rows use eight
bytes of shared-memory padding and two aligned 64-bit loads per lane operand.
The existing grouped-expert wrappers handle routing, channel scales, Hadamard
transforms, and clamped SwiGLU. Integer and supported half-integer bitrates use
upstream's K2 representation (twice the bitrate).

RDNA4 uses eight FP16 operand elements per lane and a different accumulator
layout from RDNA3. The existing gfx11 path, CUDA dispatch, and decode/verify paths
are retained. This is a focused port of the compressed matrix implementation
from fork experiment #28, not a port of the entire experimental engine stack.
The separate FMA16 and predecoded-fragment-cache experiments are excluded.

## Numerical and performance limits

Both RDNA4 paths use FP16 operands and FP32 accumulation. Matrix instructions
group products and additions differently from the portable DOT2 path.
The weights and operand formats stay the same. Different accumulation grouping
and rounding produce numerical differences that can affect later expert routing
and generated answers; bit-identical full-model logits are not required.
CPU-reference operator tests check numerical accuracy, while the reported
full-model logit differences do not by themselves measure answer-quality loss.

The previous experimental stack had full-model numerical differences. Its older
speed measurements do not establish an end-to-end gain for this current-upstream
port. Broader full-model quality, stability and matched performance evaluation
remain useful follow-ups. One earlier normal-cache/MTP comparison also
observed 3.24% slower decode despite faster prefill; generated continuations
and cache behavior differed. Measure both stages when choosing settings.

## Tests

Build `maya-ds4` and `exl3_test` with HIP enabled and gfx1201 in
`CMAKE_HIP_ARCHITECTURES`. Select a device with `HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES`.
If normal setup built the engine with tests disabled, enable the test targets
in that existing HIP build directory first:

```sh
cmake -S . -B build-hip -DSTRATA_BUILD_TESTS=ON
cmake --build build-hip --target exl3_test -j 2
```

```sh
# Full suite, RDNA4 matrix path:
EXL3_WMMA_GFX12=1 ./build-hip/exl3_test
# Portable control (the default):
./build-hip/exl3_test
# Focused grouped-expert cases, both matrix tile sizes:
EXL3_WMMA_GFX12=1 EXL3_WMMA_ROWS=16 ./build-hip/exl3_test --prefill-only
EXL3_WMMA_GFX12=1 EXL3_WMMA_ROWS=32 ./build-hip/exl3_test --prefill-only
# Explicit global opt-out overrides forced RDNA4 enable:
EXL3_WMMA_GFX12=1 EXL3_WMMA=0 ./build-hip/exl3_test --prefill-only
```

The test prints `grouped matrix prefill: enabled` or `disabled` to verify
the selected dispatch in that test process.

The focused suite compares against the scalar CPU reference with the existing
`3e-3` relative-RMS tolerance and worst-element/finite checks. It covers integer
K=1..8 with all three codebooks, supported half-integer codes, different down
bitrates, mixed expert layouts, empty experts, nonzero row offsets, row counts
around 16/32 boundaries, and output guards. Tolerances are not widened for WMMA.


# Experimental opt-in KDA layer graphs

`MAYA_GLM_KDA_GRAPH=1` captures recurrent layers of single-device GLM decode.
Default is the ordinary direct path. DSA layers remain direct. Split/MTP,
profiling, dumps, prefetch and lookahead bypass capture. Expert selection, weights,
quantization and kernel arithmetic are unchanged; graph replay explicitly accounts
for MoE requests and residual-buffer swaps. CPU-plan/buffer-key changes invalidate
the corresponding captured graph. Graphs are destroyed before their buffers.

## Measured thinking-Max screens (v1.0.21)

Windows, RTX 4090D 48 GB, Ryzen 5 3600, 128 GB DDR4-3200; unchanged
Maya-S-v2-IQ2_XXS weights, native FP16 cache, 262144 context capacity and six
CPU threads. Fixed 32755-token prompt, 128-token same-prompt warm-up, 2048
measured output tokens, temperature 1, top-p .95, seed 734621, MTP off.
Fresh engines and isolated copies of the same pre-test usage history; no
concurrent telemetry polling. Rates use native engine decode times.

| Order | Stock tok/s | KDA tok/s | Change |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Stock -> KDA | 30.7184 | 31.4429 | +2.36% |
| KDA -> stock | 30.7334 | 31.4516 | +2.34% |
| Arithmetic mean | 30.7259 | 31.4472 | +2.35% |

Both pairs have identical warm-up and measured streamed-output/reasoning hashes,
5292 VRAM / 8684 RAM slots, 32768 auto-prefill chunks and zero decode disk reads.
Mean decode time saved: 1.529 seconds per 2048 outputs. These are two repeats of
one seeded prompt, not a diverse quality benchmark or statistical confidence interval.

The control was v1.0.21 (`89a1336`), with only the common Windows environment-setter
build fix. The measured candidate was a sealed composition with **only KDA graphs
enabled**; its quota and growing-state switches were off. This PR contains neither
quota nor growing-state changes. Candidate/control executable SHA256:
`ce54343b9293456612d072863f80b49a6a7998e6167dd6d769f4ad08800ef647` /
`c059472e76f5876096f033645e945b95ad8d6f60c6b18963a87befaf6bdfdd69`.

This branch is rebased onto **v1.0.24 / e3660c2**. No v1.0.24 GPU, speed or
full native/quality result is claimed. The full v1.0.21 stock-oracle matrix was
deliberately interrupted after the synthetic gates to prioritize quick user-requested
screens; that interruption is not a numerical-parity pass or failure. Synthetic
graph checks passed on the rig, but current-main actual-pack and quality gates remain
pending and must be completed before merge or adoption. Historical v1.0.16 gains and the chart's progress
from a user-reported ~11 tok/s starting point are not this PR's isolated gain.

## Native validation

Build with `-DMAYA_BUILD_TESTS=ON`, then build `glm_layer_graph_test` and
`glm_pack_graph_test`. The first is a small
CUDA synthetic test; the second requires the real pack and GPU memory. Use an
isolated `MAYA_GLM_USAGE` and slot directory, MTP off, sufficient RAM tier and
the intended CPU lane. Do not run against live pack sidecars or alongside a
serving engine, and do not mistake compile-only checks for native execution.
The pack test compares the full vocabulary, not only argmax. Preserve any failed
gate; never turn a control's reset/re-prefill difference into a graph parity pass.
The actual-pack test first qualifies ordinary snapshot/reset repeats and refuses
loaded MTP; it does not attribute an unstable ordinary control to graph replay.
Check the final `glm KDA graphs:` line for nonzero captures and replays. Disable
profiling, dumps, prefetch and lookahead, which intentionally bypass capture.
No AMD GPU runtime validation is claimed by the CUDA-name mapping check.


# Running GLM-5.3-Flash - the study behind Maya's engine

What it would take for [zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash](https://huggingface.co/zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash) to run on this
engine, component by component, with every number the decision needs. Written 2026-09-29 against engine
0.1.27 (`main`); all `file:line` references are to that tree.

The engine Maya started from is not a general inference engine: it is a native implementation of one architecture -
Qwen3.8-Flash-Next ("qwen4exp") - with its memory tiers (VRAM expert cache / pinned RAM / mapped SSD) and
its speculation stack built around that one shape. GLM-5.3-Flash is the same *kind* of model (a 3:1
linear-attention/sparse-attention hybrid MoE with hyper-connections and an MTP layer), which is why a port
is worth studying rather than dismissing. About two thirds of the engine's machinery applies unchanged; the
remaining third is the mixer (KDA vs GDN), the full-attention mixer (MLA vs plain QKV), the router scoring,
and the packaging.

---

## 1. The model, exactly

From the released `config.json` (`zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash`, `Glm5NextForConditionalGeneration`,
`model_type: glm5-next`, MIT, released 2026-08-25):

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Total / active params | 320 B (321,323,031,390) / 18 B per token |
| Native weights | FP8 E4M3, block 128×128, dynamic activation scale; norms, gates, router, embeddings, `lm_head`, vision tower and indexer stay high-precision (BF16/F32) |
| Layers | 45 (`hidden_size` 4096); layer pattern 3 `linear_attention` then 1 `deepseek_sparse_attention`, so the full-attention layers are 3, 7, ..., 43 - **11 of them**, the same 4-interval as Strata's `qsa_interval` |
| Full attention (11 layers) | MLA, NoPE: 64 heads, `q_lora_rank` 1536, `kv_lora_rank` 512, `qk_nope_head_dim` 256, `qk_rope_head_dim` 0, `v_head_dim` 256, `mla_use_nope` true. Plus a DeepSeek-style sparse-attention indexer: `index_topk` 2048, `index_n_heads` 32, `index_head_dim` 128, `index_kpool` 4 (compressed index-key pool) |
| Linear attention (34 layers) | Kimi Delta Attention: 64 heads × head_dim 128, `short_conv_kernel_size` 4, `gate_lower_bound` -5.0 |
| Hyper-connections | `mhc: true`, `hc_mult` 4, `hc_sinkhorn_iters` 20 - the manifold-constrained variant (mHC) |
| MoE | 288 routed experts + 1 shared, top-8 per token, `moe_intermediate_size` 2048, sigmoid scoring, `noaux_tc` routing, `routed_scaling_factor` 2.5, router in float32; **layers 0-2 dense, 3-44 sparse (42 MoE layers)** |
| MTP | `num_nextn_predict_layers` 1 |
| Vision | 24-block ViT, hidden 1024, 16 heads, 448 px, patch 14, spatial merge 2, out 4096, projector intermediate 10240 |
| Vocab / context | 154,880 / 1,048,576 |
| Routed-expert parameter share | 288 × 42 × 3 × 4096 × 2048 ≈ **304.6 B params = 95 % of the model** |

GGUFs already exist: [unsloth/GLM-5.3-Flash-GGUF](https://huggingface.co/unsloth/GLM-5.3-Flash-GGUF)
(dynamic quants; ~120 GB at their 3-bit, ~180 GB at 4-bit). llama.cpp has the building blocks this
architecture needs (KDA chunked + recurrent kernels, NoPE-MLA, DSA lightning indexer from the DeepSeek
V3.2 work, nextn/MTP), which matters twice: it proves the kernels are portable, and `third_party/ggml`
is the pinned dependency Maya builds its native path and `maya-vision` against.

## 2. What the engine already is

For reference, the implemented geometry (`include/strata/core/layout.hpp:27-59`): n_embd 2560, 48 layers,
full attention every 4th layer (12 QSA), GDN linear layers (16 k-heads / 48 v-heads, state 128, conv 4),
QSA full attention (24 heads, 2 KV heads, head_dim 256, indexer 4 q-heads over one shared 128-dim key),
gated residual with hc = 4 streams and a 320-wide bottleneck, MoE 512 experts × top-10 × intermediate 640,
a layer-1 PLE module backed by a 28.8 GB n-gram table, and an MTP draft layer. Per layer:

    gr_read (attn) -> GDN | QSA -> gr_write (attn) -> gr_read (ffn) -> MoE -> gr_write (ffn)

The engine's unit is a captured per-layer CUDA graph replayed by a host loop that overlaps a CPU expert
pool and a VRAM expert cache behind a doorbell (`include/strata/core/session.hpp`). Experts live in
`experts.bin` (fixed-size blobs, `layer * n_expert + expert`), served from a pinned arena by default or
from an mmap in low-RAM mode (`--mmap-experts`, `include/strata/core/expert_source.hpp:303-360`). Dense
weights load from the pack into VRAM. A "native pack" path runs directly from GGUF shards with per-layer
runtime geometry (`include/strata/kernels/cpu/native_expert.hpp`) - this is how the Coder (mixed i-quant
types, 256 experts/layer) works, and it is the path a GLM pack would ride.

## 3. Component-by-component gap analysis

### 3.1 Applies unchanged

| Piece | Why |
| --- | --- |
| 3:1 layer pattern | GLM's full-attention layers are `layer % 4 == 3`, identical to `is_qsa_layer` (`layout.hpp:63`). 45 layers → 11 QSA + 34 linear, and `n_qsa_layers() = 45/4 = 11` is already correct arithmetic. |
| Hyper-connection residual plumbing | `hc_mult` 4 = the implemented `hc = 4`. The stream stack, the per-half read/write structure, the BF16 weight contract and the fused-GR fast path all carry over. (What changes is *which weights* and one normalization - §3.4.) |
| Expert memory-tier machinery | VRAM expert cache, expert profile, pinning, PCIe overlap, multi-GPU expert split, the mmap source - all parameterized by `(n_layers, n_expert, blob_bytes)` at runtime. |
| KV cache formats and streaming | INT8 / Q4_0 / K8V4, KV streaming past 64K, checkpoints - all sized from `ModelGeometry`. |
| MTP speculation protocol | Verify windows, draft policy, prompt-lookup drafter. The drafter's *weights* are the model's own MTP layer (§3.6). |
| Prefill pipeline | Chunked MMQ prefill, `--prefill auto` - shapes come from the pack. |
| Serving | OpenAI/Anthropic APIs, MCP, monitor, tokenizer-free prompt caching - model-agnostic already. |
| PLE | GLM has no PLE module. `PleRun` is already optional (`layer.hpp:545`, "null when the PLE is not wired"), and a pack without `blk.1.ple_*` skips it - which is what GLM must do. No shard 2, no 29 GB n-gram table. |

### 3.2 The linear-attention mixer: GDN → KDA (moderate kernel work)

The implemented GDN (`ref/gdn.py::gdn_decode`, `layer.hpp:8-17`) has per-head scalars: `alpha`, `beta`,
`gate` are one float per v-head (`layer.hpp:73-75`), the gate is `softplus(walpha@x + dt) * ssm_a`.

KDA differs in two ways that matter:

1. **Fine-grained (per-channel) decay gates.** The decay is a *vector* of head_dim per head (64 × 128),
   applied elementwise to the state, not a scalar per head. In llama.cpp this is the
   `linear_attn_config`'s two decay-gate forms, selected by `gate_lower_bound`; GLM sets -5.0, so KDA's
   lower-bounded fine-grained gate form is the one to implement. The recurrent state shape itself
   (64 heads × 128 × 128 ≈ 1 M floats/layer, close to the implemented 128 × 128 × 48 ≈ 786 K) and the
   short conv (kernel 4 = `d_conv` 4) are the same shape family the `gdn.cu` / `native_gdn.cu` /
   `fused_gdn.cu` kernels already handle.
2. **Gate lower bound.** The -5.0 clamp on the gate logit is a one-line change *once* the gate is a
   vector, but it changes the numeric contract, so both the CUDA and the `ref/gdn.py` oracle must change
   together and re-pass `gdn_parity`.

Work: extend the gate buffers and the decay application in the chunked (prefill) and recurrent (decode)
GDN kernels to per-channel gates; add the lower-bound clamp; new BF16 `ssm_alpha`-class weights (shape
changes from (48) to (64×128) per token); update `check_layer` assertions. The activation-contract logic
(which weights take Q8_K vs BF16 activations) is per-tensor and already data-driven.

### 3.3 The full-attention mixer: QSA → NoPE-MLA + DSA (the largest piece)

The implemented QSA layer is plain QKV attention with RoPE: 24 heads, 2 KV heads × 256, cos/sin tables
(`layer.hpp:239-241`), KV pools paged per head (`k_pool` `[page][kv_head][page][dim]`), a block-pooled
indexer (4 q-heads, one shared 128-dim key) driving a top-k cell selection, and a per-head output gate.

GLM's 11 full-attention layers need:

1. **MLA with absorption, NoPE.** `qk_rope_head_dim = 0` means *no RoPE at all* in these layers - the
   rope tables, `pos_dev` rotation and M-RoPE position ids for attention disappear from the path (M-RoPE
   remains only as the vision position encoding if the vision path needs it). The KV cache becomes the
   compressed latent `c_kv` (512 floats/token/layer) - which is *cheaper* than the implemented KV: 11
   layers × 512 × 2 B ≈ **11.3 KB per context token in FP16**, about half of the implemented 2 × 256 × 2
   heads × 2 B = 24.6 KB, and 1.4 GB at 128 K context. Two implementation options:
   * *Absorbed:* precompute `q_hat = W_uk^T q` (a 512-vector per head) and score directly against the
     latent. The attention kernel then reads ONE 512-wide pool instead of per-head pools - a simpler and
     faster memory pattern than today's, at the cost of a per-token 64 × 512 GEMV to build `q_hat`.
   * *Expanded:* materialize per-head K (256) and V (256) into the existing pool layout at write time
     (3.2× the latent bytes, 36 KB/token) and reuse the existing decode/verify kernels unchanged.
     Absorbed is the right target (that is why MLA exists), but expanded is the honest first milestone -
     it isolates correctness from kernel novelty.
2. **The indexer.** GLM's indexer is 32 q-heads × 128 (vs implemented 4 × 128 shared-key), top-k 2048
   cells (the implemented selection width is already ~2 K - `kTopkMaxCells`, "8 queries x 2,051 cells",
   `layer.hpp:275`), plus `index_kpool` 4 - a compressed index-key pool whose exact form needs the
   converted GGUF (or the llama.cpp glm5 conversion) as the reference. The QSA indexer machinery
   (`native_qsa_indexer`, block scores, pooled keys) is the right skeleton; the head counts and the pool
   compression are the deltas.
3. **Per-head output gate** (`v_head_dim` 256, gated attention output) - the implemented `q_full`
   q|gate split (`layer.hpp:367-371`) already has this shape; dims change.

Work: new absorbed-MLA attention kernels (decode + prefill + verify-window) or the expanded interim path;
indexer re-parameterization; `QsaState` without rope tables; `check_layer` per-type rewrites. This is the
single largest kernel workstream, and it is also where numerical parity effort concentrates.

### 3.4 Hyper-connections: HC → mHC (small, but resolve one question)

The implemented GR (`ref/gr.py`, transcribed from llama.cpp's `qwen4exp` `build_hc_mix/combine`,
`gr.hpp:110-120`) uses per-half weights `w_norm` (hc·n_embd), a low-rank bottleneck `w_down`/`w_up`
(hc_lr 320), and `w_inject`; the write gate is `2*sigmoid(inject/hc)`.

GLM's mHC adds the manifold constraint: the mixing matrix is Sinkhorn-normalized
(`hc_sinkhorn_iters` 20, `hc_mult` 4). The question to resolve from the first converted GGUF is *where*
the Sinkhorn iteration lives:

* If the released weights are already normalized (the constraint enforced during training), mHC is a
  **weight-preprocessing step at pack time** and the inference kernels are bit-identical to today's GR.
* If the conversion expects the engine to normalize per forward (the config exposes the iteration count
  because inference needs it), the engine needs the 20-iteration Sinkhorn on the 4×4 matrix per layer per
  token - trivially cheap (16 numbers), but it must be inside the captured graph, and the oracle (`ref/gr.py`)
  must grow the same step.

Either way this is the *cheapest* architecture delta; the risk is only parity discipline.

### 3.5 MoE: shapes + scoring (small engine work, one hard wall)

* **Shapes.** 288 experts × top-8 × intermediate 2048 vs 512 × 10 × 640. All of `n_expert`, `k` and
  `n_ff` flow through `ModelGeometry` at runtime, the router top-k is already a parameter
  (`router_top10.hpp:17`, "k must be <= 64"), and the doorbell/expert dispatch carry `k` explicitly.
* **Dense layers 0-2.** The implemented stack assumes every layer is MoE. GLM's first three layers are
  dense MLPs. `core/native_dense.hpp` exists (dense GEMV machinery) - a dense-FFN layer type is needed in
  `block_layer` for layers 0-2, which is a small structural change plus weights plumbing.
* **Scoring.** The implemented router is softmax over all experts + renormalise (`router_top10.hpp:3-5`,
  `ref/moe.py::router`). GLM is **sigmoid scoring, `noaux_tc`, renormalise over the selected 8, × 2.5
  routed scaling, float32 router weights**. A scoring-function variant of the router kernel + the scaling
  factor - small, but it is a *selection semantics* change: parity must be checked at the router output
  (the BF16-GEMV → logits → top-8 boundary), because a flipped selection is a different expert, not a
  rounding error (the repo's own note, `layer.hpp:131-139`).
* **The hard wall: the canonical Q2_0 CPU expert kernel.** `include/strata/kernels/cpu/expert.hpp:34-45`
  bakes H = 2560, FF = 640, NE = 512 and BLOB = 1,382,400 as compile-time constants, and its contract
  REFUSES other shapes ("a different n_embd or a different expert width is REFUSED rather than
  mis-indexed", `expert_source.hpp:18-21`). GLM does not go through it: the **native path**
  (`native_expert.hpp`) is runtime-geometry (per-layer `NativeFmt` with `n_embd`, `n_ff`, ggml types from
  the GGUF), and that is the path GLM must ride - exactly as the Coder and the Orca compatibility pack did.
  Consequence for the study: GLM packs are native packs; the canonical Q2_0 pack path
  is out of scope for the port.
* **Expert blob size.** 3 × 4096 × 2048 = 25.2 M params/expert ≈ 3.2× the implemented expert. Blob sizes
  become per-layer-native (already supported: `layer_offsets_`/`layer_blob_bytes_`,
  `expert_source.hpp:345`). Per-token routed traffic at Q2_0-class rates ≈ 336 reads × ~7 MB ≈ 2.4 GB -
  ~3.6× the implemented 664 MB/token (`layer.hpp:526-527`), which is the real performance story, §5.

### 3.6 MTP, PLE-class extras, vision, tokenizer

* **MTP.** One nextn layer, same protocol. Two fixes: the drafter's geometry is hardcoded to canonical
  defaults (`static const strata::core::ModelGeometry draft_geometry{}`, `generate.cpp:1726`) and must
  follow the target model; the Qwen draft-vocab embed size is hardcoded at one call site
  (`generate.cpp:1374`, 248320) and must come from the pack. GLM's MTP tensors must be in the GGUF
  (llama.cpp's glm5 conversion carries nextn tensors) and `tools/mtp_pack.py` taught the tensor names.
  Draft acceptance is an empirical question per model.
* **Vision.** `maya-vision` is built from the pinned llama.cpp `mtmd` (`tools/vision/`). GLM's 24-block
  ViT at 448 px is a standard layout; support reduces to what the pinned llama.cpp supports for
  `glm5-next` vision + a converted `mmproj` GGUF, plus the family wiring in `setup.py`. If the pinned
  llama.cpp predates glm5 vision support, this is a submodule pin bump with a re-run of the parity tests.
* **Tokenizer.** The packer exports the model's own tokenizer from the GGUF (the rule: never share
  another model's tokenizer). GLM's BPE (154,880 vocab) flows through `gguf-py` like Qwen's; verify the
  pre-tokenizer regex is one `gguf-py` knows. A GLM chat template (`serve/chat_template.jinja` is
  Qwen's) is needed for the server, plus its image/video token ids (154854/154855).
* **Metadata.** The engine reads nine `qwen4exp.*` keys (§6). A `glm5-next.*` set must be recognized -
  the minimal change is in `generate.cpp:1466-1480`, which today reads only `expert_count` and
  `expert_used_count` from the model file.

### 3.7 Quantization and the pack

* Source weights are FP8 E4M3; the packer consumes standard GGUF quants through ggml-cpu
  (`ref/load.py`'s twelve-format table + the native path). The unsloth GGUFs (Q2_0/IQ2/IQ3/IQ4 dynamic)
  are the input; the canonical Q2_0 pack pipeline is not needed (§3.5).
* The `--compat-bf16` route (`tools/iq_pack.py`, built for ORCA) is exactly what a GLM pack needs for its
  high-precision projections: GLM's BF16 list differs from Qwen's (config `modules_to_not_convert`:
  attention projections on sparse layers, norms, gates, router, embeddings, `lm_head`, vision tower,
  indexer), so the compat list must be derived from the architecture's tensor names rather than copied.
* Per-tensor activation contracts (`docs/activation-contract.md`, Q8_0 vs Q8_K by source type) are
  data-driven already - new quant mixes ride along.

## 4. The mmap study (the memory-pool question)

**The headline number: the routed experts alone exceed 64 GB of RAM at every useful quantization.**

| Format | bpw (approx) | Per-expert | 288 × 42 experts | + dense ≈ | File |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Q2_0-class | 2.25 | 7.1 MB | **~86 GB** | ~93 GB | ~100 GB |
| IQ2_XS-class | 2.06 | 6.5 MB | **~79 GB** | ~85 GB | ~95 GB |
| IQ3_XXS-class | 2.66 | 8.4 MB | **~101 GB** | ~108 GB | ~120 GB |
| 4-bit class | 4.25 | 13.4 MB | **~162 GB** | ~170 GB | ~180 GB |

(The original engine's Qwen numbers for comparison: 23-50 GB of experts *pinned in RAM* - the design assumption
"all experts resident in RAM" is dead for GLM on consumer hardware. This is the thing the user flagged.)

Decode traffic per token: 8 experts × 42 layers = 336 expert reads ≈ **2.4 GB/token at Q2_0 rates**
(3.6× Qwen's 664 MB/token). Where those bytes come from decides everything:

* VRAM cache (hot experts, profile-ranked): unchanged machinery, same hit-rate economics, but each hit
  now removes 3.2× more bytes from the RAM/SSD path.
* RAM (page cache or pinned): on 128 GB machines, ~86 GB of Q2_0-class experts fit alongside the OS -
  the mmap source with a warm page cache is then DRAM-speed and the current low-RAM mode *is* the main
  mode. On 64 GB machines roughly half the expert set fits; the rest pages from SSD, and a cache-miss
  heavy token costs ~1.2 GB of NVMe reads ≈ 100-150 ms - workable at ~5-8 tok/s cold, degraded but usable,
  and the expert profile is what keeps the miss rate down over time.
* SSD: NVMe random reads at blob granularity (~7 MB) are exactly the access pattern the existing
  mmap source and the PLE reader's unbuffered async path (`direct_file.hpp`) were built for.

What the engine already has for this: `FileExpertSource` (MapViewOfFile/mmap, blob = base + multiply,
`expert_source.hpp:303-360`), `pin_cache_complement` (pin the non-cached complement when RAM allows),
the VRAM tier with decayed-usage swapping, PCIe streaming of next-layer experts during prefill attention,
KV streaming, and the unbuffered async file layer.

One lesson the code already holds, and the study adopts: **retention is the whole game, and
random-access hints are the enemy.** `FILE_FLAG_RANDOM_ACCESS` was tried on the expert mapping and
measured at **1.93 GB/s - disk speed, 14x off** - because it tells Windows' cache manager to drop the
pages again quickly (`expert_source.cpp:332-343`). The expert access pattern is random per layer but
*repeated every token*, so the mapping deliberately carries no hint at all. The same reasoning rules out
`MADV_RANDOM` on Linux. What a 100 GB+ mapping does need (implemented on this branch, §7):

1. **Prefetch the routed blobs at `begin_layer`.** The source's own contract says it: "a source that
   reads from disk wants to start the read here so it overlaps the quantisation"
   (`expert_source.hpp:98`). When the router's ids for a layer are known, issuing
   `PrefetchVirtualMemory` (Windows) / `posix_fadvise(WILLNEED)` (Linux) for exactly those k blobs
   page-ins them while the pool quantizes the activation and the GPU finishes the previous layer -
   the mmap-path analogue of the PCIe expert streaming the prefill path already does. Unlike the
   random-access flag, prefetch is *retention-friendly*: it pulls the pages the router will actually
   touch into the standby list and says nothing about evicting the rest.
2. **Keep the mapping hint-free.** At GLM's scale (~86-162 GB of experts) the temptation to "fix"
   random access with hints is stronger and the failure mode is the same measured one. The mapping
   stays undecorated; the page cache decides retention by recency, which is what a hot working set
   inside a much larger mapped file wants.
3. **Never map-and-pin by accident.** `pin_cache_complement` remains opt-in and is now also the tool for
   the 128 GB-RAM case (pin the complement the VRAM cache does not hold, when it fits); on 64 GB it must
   stay off and the page cache must be allowed to reclaim.

## 5. Expected performance envelope (estimates, ±30 %)

The method: GPU part scaled by bandwidth and VRAM-held expert share,
CPU part by remaining expert bytes. Assume an RTX 3090-class card (24 GB) + DDR5, Q2_0-class pack:

* ~3,000 VRAM-cached experts cover ~70 % of reads (profile economics transfer; per-expert bytes are
  3.2× bigger, so a 24 GB card caches ~2.5-3 K of them). Remaining ~30 % = ~720 MB/token from
  DRAM at ~40 GB/s ≈ 18 ms → **decode ceiling ≈ 50-65 tok/s** if the CPU pool and the overlap hold.
* On 12 GB + 64 GB RAM: ~1.5 K experts cached (~55 % of reads); the misses split between page cache and
  SSD → **~25-40 tok/s** warm, lower cold.
* Prefill: batched expert MMQ over 8 K-token chunks is compute/weight-bound; expert bytes/token are
  amortized ~800× across a chunk, so **~1,000-1,800 tok/s** prefill on the same card class is plausible.
* Long context is *better* than Qwen's case: MLA-halved KV (11.3 KB/token fp16) means 128 K ≈ 1.4 GB,
  1 M ≈ 11 GB - 1 M context is feasible on 24 GB + 128 GB with KV streaming, where the implemented model
  saturates earlier.

These are study estimates, not measurements; the parity harness (§6) comes before any tuning.

## 6. Execution plan

**Phase 0 - reference and metadata (this branch).**
`generate.cpp` recognizes `general.architecture = glm5-next` and fails *loudly and specifically* until the
kernel work lands, instead of dying inside `check_all` with forty shape mismatches; the mmap improvements
from §4 (they serve the existing models' low-RAM mode too); this document.

**Phase 1 - the oracle (DONE, this branch).** `ref/glm.py`: GGUF → FP32 on demand (the
