`ref/load.py` machinery), one full teacher-forced forward pass of glm5-next with the released
config as metadata defaults; every intermediate the engine will need as a parity seam is
recorded under llama.cpp's own `cb()` names (175 tensors on the synthetic model), and
`tools/test_glm_oracle.py` asserts the architectural invariants (MoE weight renormalization,
Sinkhorn dst-stochasticity, gate ranges, finite seams). The spec was pinned against
`ref/upstream-llama.cpp/glm5-next.cpp` (vendored, llama.cpp @ `272aad8b`), which also answered
the two open questions: **mHC Sinkhorn normalization runs per forward, inside the graph**
(softmax over dst, +eps, then alternating src/dst normalizations - a 4×4 matrix per layer per
token, but data-dependent, so the engine must compute it per token, not at pack time), and the
**k-pool indexer** averages each pool of 4 consecutive indexer keys with a per-key-dim softmax
over (gate + learned per-position additive embedding), scores pools with a ReLU'd multi-head
dot product against prescaled weights, selects top_k/kpool pools per query, and appends the
incomplete tail (`kpool_select_tail` = true in the released config). Also pinned: the KDA
gates are LOW-RANK decomposed (`f_a` rank head_dim → `f_b`, same for the output gate `g_a/g_b`)
with the lower-bound form `lb·sigmoid(−g·A)`, and the fused delta-net kernel applies the gate
on the KEY axis and bakes the `1/√S` output scale. Remaining known gap: llama.cpp has not
implemented the GLM5-Next MTP graph yet ("not implemented yet" in glm5-next.cpp L190), so the
draft layer's exact form stays a Phase 3 question.

**Phase 2 - the mixers (STARTED).** Two kernel families landed, each with a parity harness in the
repo's negative-test discipline (every wrong reading must differ materially before the right one is
judged):

* **mHC** (`include/strata/kernels/glm_hc.hpp`, `src/kernels/cuda/glm_hc.cu`, `glm_hc_parity`): the
  read half is three kernels - the joint-stack inverse RMS (ONE norm over all four streams, not
  gr's per-stream), the 24-row mixes GEMV, and a serial lane that forms the three gates, runs the
  Sinkhorn on the 4×4 comb (softmax over dst, +eps onto the values, dst-norm, then
  (iters-1)×[src-norm, dst-norm]), and forms `mixed = Σ_s pre_s·R_s`. The write half is one
  elementwise kernel: `out_dst = block_out·post_dst + Σ_src comb[dst,src]·residual_src`. The
  negative tests pin the joint-vs-per-stream norm, the src-major comb rows, the post-softmax +eps,
  sum-vs-mean, and the pre-gate floor (with hc_eps exaggerated to 3e-2 in the structural fixture,
  since the real 1e-6 sits below f32 noise). Weights are F32 for now - the BF16-input variant is a
  Phase 3 wiring decision, pinned against this F32 reference.
* **Router** (`include/strata/kernels/router_sigmoid.hpp`, `src/kernels/cuda/router_sigmoid.cu`,
  `router_sigmoid_parity`): one block per token, no reductions (sigmoid is bounded), the rank
  formula from router_top10 reproduced for the tie rule, bias applied to the SELECTION only, weights
  gathered from the unbiased sigmoid, renormalised with the 2**-14 clamp, scaled. The negative tests
  pin biased-weights (the noaux_tc trap), softmax-vs-sigmoid, missing renorm/scale, and tie order
  via a quantised fixture.

* **KDA** (`include/strata/kernels/glm_kda.hpp`, `src/kernels/cuda/glm_kda.cu`, `glm_kda_parity`): the
  linear-attention layer's four pieces, in ggml layouts end to end so Phase 3 wiring needs no
  transposes - the causal conv + SiLU with the history slide as a separate kernel (an in-place slide
  while earlier tokens' threads still read the old columns is a race), the low-rank gate's lower-bound
  squash, the RECURRENCE (one block per head, tokens looped inside the block: key-axis row scale, the
  `u = S^T k` reduction, the delta correction, the outer update, `o = S^T q` with the 1/sqrt(dv)
  baked in like ggml's kernel), and the normed-and-gated output. The negative tests pin the GDN
  readings: scalar per-head gate, value-axis gate, NO delta correction (plain linear attention - the
  shapes all agree), the softplus form, the missing output scale, and reversed conv taps.
* **Clamped SwiGLU** (`include/strata/kernels/glm_ffn.hpp`, `src/kernels/cuda/glm_ffn.cu`,
  `glm_ffn_parity`): the dense-lead, routed-expert and shared-expert activation -
  `silu(min(gate, limit)) * clamp(up, ±limit)`. The negative tests pin the one-sided gate clamp and
  the clamp-before-silu order, whose differences live in silu's damped tail and are bounded
  (~4.5e-3 with the fixture), so their thresholds are set from those bounds rather than guessed.
  This kernel also settled an accuracy contract: `__expf`'s argument-scaled ulp error is visible at
  |x| ~ 10, so these kernels use precise `expf`.

* **DSA indexer + absorbed-MLA attention** (`include/strata/kernels/glm_dsa.hpp`,
  `src/kernels/cuda/glm_dsa.cu`, `glm_dsa_parity`): the pool (per-key-dim softmax over
  gate+ape weighted member average), the score (relu'd multi-head dots against prescaled weights),
  the selection (the router rank formula over VISIBLE pools - pool p visible to query t only when
  its last member is <= t - with the incomplete tail appended), and the attention itself: one block
  per (query, head) against the 512-wide latent cache, q absorbed through wk_b, v absorbed through
  wv_b after the softmax, the 1/sqrt(qk_nope) scale, padding excluded from the softmax. Negative
  tests: a pool-wide softmax, unweighted pooling, no relu, no tail, and the wrong scale denominator.
* **The layer composition test** (`glm_layer_parity`): two complete synthetic-model layers (0: KDA +
  dense FFN; 3: DSA + MoE) through the kernel family, every seam held to `ref/glm.py`'s raw dumps
  (`data/glm5-synth-dumps/`). It caught three real bugs no single-kernel parity could see: the
  oracle's conv einsum labeled the two channel axes differently and computed garbage
  (fixed: `"tc,tck->ck"` - the kernels were right); the router bias read past its per-layer
  (n_expert,) allocation for tokens >= 1 (fixed: broadcast); and the test's own array-KV read
  (`expert_feed_forward_length` is an ARRAY - `MetaValue::u` is 0 for arrays). It also fixed
  glm.py's hc flat to ggml stream-major.

Phase 2 status: **COMPLETE** - all seven suites green on Mercury (glm_hc, router_sigmoid, glm_kda,
glm_ffn, glm_dsa, file_expert_source, glm_layer_parity). The last composition bug was the layer
test's own down-exps read: ffn_down_exps is {n_ff, n_embd, n_expert}, so ne0 is the INPUT dim and
down_e[o, v] lives at v + n_ff*o + n_ff*n_embd*e (the transpose of gate/up, whose ne0 is the
embedding axis).

Phase 3 status: the **Glm5Model streaming runner** (`src/core/glm_model.cu`) is complete and
verified - `glm_model_test` holds it to the oracle at every position of a 12-token sequence
(batched-vs-oracle 2e-3, all 8 prefix argmaxes, streaming-vs-batched 1e-5), derives the greedy
chain from the dump rather than a constant (a hardcoded Phase-1 chain went stale when the oracle's
conv einsum was repaired - four independent reviews converged on this diagnosis), and checks the
streaming continuation at positions 8-11. **8/8 suites green on Mercury in 2.1 s of GPU.** The
packer (`iq_pack.py`) recognizes `glm5-next` and carries the per-architecture BF16-preserve list
(the mHC fn/base/scale names, the glm5 indexer set, the router + noaux_tc bias, the KDA gates)
with 3-D tensors packing byte-exactly as (ne0*ne1, ne2) views; `strata_tokenizer` implements the
glm4/glm5 pre-tokenizer scheme (a verbatim CHATGLM4 transcription from llama-vocab.cpp: digit runs
in groups of at most 3, no `\p{M}` in the letter/punct classes, `ignore_merges` whole-vocab-hit
emission) and still refuses schemes it has not transcribed; the pack export ships the per-scheme
pattern and the ignore_merges flag. The driver gate now points at the verified runner.
Re-verified 2026-10-01 on a fresh Mercury extract: 8/8 suites in 2.0 s of GPU. Remaining for
Phase 4: quantized-kernel wiring for the production driver path, the installer FAMILIES entry
(deliberately deferred - it would advertise a model the driver still refuses to serve), and
serve/measure - the owner has ordered the **IQ1_M quant first**, end-to-end before optimizing
upward; §3.5's hard wall applies (GLM packs are native packs; the canonical Q2_0 expert kernel's
compile-time geometry does not fit GLM). llama.cpp has no GLM5-Next MTP graph; the draft layer
stays deferred.

**Phase 3 - pack and install.** `glm5-next` GGUF → native pack via `tools/iq_pack.py` with the GLM
compat-BF16 list; MTP pack; tokenizer export; `FAMILIES["glm53"]` in `setup.py` (the installer's family
table, `setup.py:80-104`, is one dict entry); GLM chat template; engine metadata keys
(`glm5-next.embedding_length`, `.block_count`, `.expert_count`, `.expert_used_count`, attention head
counts) read into `ModelGeometry`.

**Phase 4 - serve and measure.** Vision helper against the glm5 mmproj; end-to-end parity vs the oracle
per residual ladder (`dump_layers`); needle tests across 1 K-1 M; the benchmark matrix; then the
benchmark tables gain a GLM section.

Rough effort weighting: Phase 2 ≈ 60 %, Phase 1 ≈ 15 %, Phase 3 ≈ 15 %, Phase 4 ≈ 10 %. The critical
path runs through the MLA attention kernels and their parity harness.

## 7. What this branch contains

* `docs/GLM5-FLASH.md` - this study.
* The §4 mmap work in `FileExpertSource`: per-layer prefetch of the routed blobs from the existing
  `begin_layer` hook (Windows `PrefetchVirtualMemory`; Linux `posix_fadvise(WILLNEED)`), best-effort and
  retention-friendly - the mapping itself stays hint-free for the measured reason above. With a test.
* The §6 Phase-0 architecture gate in the driver.
* A build fix found by the clean-clone verification: `native_mmvq_multi` and `hit_cpu_order_parity` are
  now EXISTS-guarded like their siblings - their bench sources are not in the repository, and with
  `MAYA_BUILD_TESTS=ON` the generate step refused to run at all.
* Phase 1: `ref/glm.py` (the glm5-next oracle), `tools/glm_synth_gguf.py` (the synthetic model it and the
  kernel parity tests run against), `tools/test_glm_oracle.py` (7 invariant tests, no GPU), and
  `ref/upstream-llama.cpp/` (the vendored llama.cpp spec, @ `272aad8b`).
* Phase 2 complete: the five kernel families - mHC (`glm_hc_*`), the sigmoid router
  (`router_sigmoid_*`), KDA (`glm_kda_*`), the clamped SwiGLU + MoE tails (`glm_ffn_*`), and the
  DSA indexer + absorbed-MLA attention (`glm_dsa_*`) - each with its parity harness, plus
  `glm_layer_parity` (the full layer composition) and the binary fixtures under `data/`.
* Phase 3: the `Glm5Model` streaming runner (`src/core/glm_model.{hpp,cu}`, held to the oracle by
  `glm_model_test`); `iq_pack.py`'s glm5-next support (`tools/test_iq_pack_glm.py`) and the
  glm4/glm5 pre-tokenizer in `strata_tokenizer` (`tools/test_tokenizer_glm.py`).

A note on running the kernel parity tests on a 2x V100 machine (Mercury): its GPUs are V100s (sm_70), BELOW
the engine's sm_75 minimum, so the prebuilt archs produce binaries the V100 cannot launch ("no kernel
image"). The kernel sources themselves are arch-portable (the sm_80 features have the fp32 fallback),
so the local test tree relaxes the configure guard one line (`sed -i 's/_base LESS 75/_base LESS 70/'`)
and configures with `-DCMAKE_CUDA_ARCHITECTURES=70` - an uncommitted, build-tree-local change that
exists only to let the V100s execute the parity suite.

### 8.1 Offset-width audit (second-opinion item 7, 2026-10-03)

Every index product the glm5 kernels and the runner compute was checked against the REAL
dimensions (n_embd 4096, n_ff 12288/2048x288, 1M context).  Findings, none of them bugs:

* Host arena addressing is int64 end to end: the region vectors (`kda_S_`, `dsa_lat_`, ...),
  every scratch offset, and the expert slab base (`ffn_*_exps + slab*e`, 2.4e9 elements over
  INT32_MAX) all use int64/size_t.
* Device-side indexing is `(size_t)` wherever a product grows (latent cache row
  `kv_lora*cell` = 5.4e8 at 1M ctx; q_absorb; gemv rows).  The largest product any kernel
  forms in 32-bit `int` locals is bounded by one call's activation size - the runner is
  single-token and the parity harness 8 tokens - at most ~2e7, two orders below 2**31.
* The 2.2B-element-per-layer expert tensors never appear as a kernel-internal offset: the
  kernel sees one expert's slab (8.4M elements), the host adds the int64 layer base.

The audit's boundary: the engine's own ggml-side kernels (native_expert, the dense GEMV path)
are out of scope here - they run qwen4exp production models already.

### 8.2 The big synthetic and the llama.cpp cross-check (second-opinion item 7, 2026-10-03)

`tools/glm_synth_gguf.py --big` (seed 777) builds the second fixture: 45 layers in the released
cadence (DSA at il%4==3, layers 3..43), n_embd/q_lora/kv_lora/n_ff_exp multiples of 256 (the IQ
row width), top-8 of 32 experts, kpool 4 with top_k 256 (a 512-token sequence crosses 128 pools
and discards half), sinkhorn iterations 5, hot FFN scales (SwiGLU clamps fire) and +8 dt spikes
(KDA gates reach the lower bound).  tools/cover_audit.py PASSES on it - every branch the first
fixture left at zero fires here.

The cross-check: llama.cpp (master, CPU) streams the same 512 tokens; the f64 oracle teacher-forces
them.  Result, all 512 columns:

  * per-column max |logit diff|: p50 9.0e-05, p99 2.5e-03 (logit std 0.8);
  * KL(oracle || llama) on the softmax: p50 2.0e-09, p99 1.7e-07;
  * argmax agreement 510/512; BOTH flips are explained by MoE selection near-ties - the rank-8 vs
    rank-9 biased-score gap is 1.2e-5 (col 108) and 1.7e-6 (col 250), inside f32-vs-f64 noise, so
    the two implementations legitimately pick different 8th experts.  This is the second opinion's
    item 2 mechanism verbatim, and it is why the Phase-4 acceptance gates are top-1 agreement +
    mean/p99 KL, not a per-logit 2e-3 tolerance.

Phase-4 acceptance thresholds calibrated on this pair: KL p99 < 1e-5, top-1 agreement > 99%,
with every disagreement traceable to a selection-boundary gap below ~1e-4.  The 272aad8b pin is
unusable for the cross-check (its graph_reserve aborts on the scatter dead-slot bug master
fixed); /tmp/llama-ref on Mercury holds the master build + tools/llm_logits_dump.cpp.

### 8.2.1 The REAL-model cross-check (UD-IQ1_S vs llama.cpp, 2026-10-04) and the quantized gates

The §8.2 gates were calibrated F32-vs-F32 (llama on the F32 fixture).  The REAL-model cross-check
runs llama.cpp (upstream master, CPU) against the PACK (device MMVQ dense + CPU IQ experts) on the
same quantized GGUF — both sides quantized, different kernels.  Result on tokens 1..8 (seam-level
bisect via GLM_CB_DIR vs llama's LLM_CB_DIR, the full recipe in NEXT-STEPS.md):

  * layer 0: every seam within 3e-3 (fp noise); router chain (logits → biased probs → top-8 →
    renormalized weights) EQUAL at the probed layer; MoE selection never flips;
  * first divergence: L3 ffn_out (the first routed-expert layer), growing smoothly; the L18 ×68
    state explosion (real model behavior, both sides) amplifies it;
  * per-expert h/down vs an exact dequant+fp32 reference: ours 3.0–4.3e-2, llama 1.5–4.5e-2 —
    EQUALLY FAITHFUL; the gap is each side's activation-quantization error (ours q8_1 on the
    device dense path, llama Q8_K on CPU), re-rolled at every re-quantization of an already
    slightly-different stream;
  * aggregate (8 tokens): top-1 5/8, p50|Δlogit| 0.31–1.29, cos-sim 0.70–0.97 — the shape tracks
    the target distribution's peakedness (positions 5–7 recover to 0.95), NOT a cumulative bug.

CONCLUSION (owner-accepted 2026-10-04): no structural divergence; the difference is accumulated
quantized-kernel noise.  **Quantized-mode acceptance gates** (for pack-vs-llama comparisons on
the same quantized GGUF — the F32 gates above DO NOT apply):

  * every argmax/logit finite, same vocab, no NaN through all trunk layers;
  * p50|Δlogit| < 1.5 AND cosine(logits) > 0.65 per teacher-forced position;
  * MoE selection (top-8 ids) equal at every probed layer; expert compute within 2× of llama's
    distance to the exact dequant+fp32 reference;
  * no positional drift: the divergence must NOT grow monotonically with position (a state/cache
    bug does; noise recovers when the distribution sharpens).

Improving the seed is M2-adjacent (owner decision: fold into M2): switching the device dense path
to Q8_K-style activation quant would cut the divergence seed vs llama, at the cost of reworking
every MMVQ call site — to be decided where the path is rebuilt anyway (IQ1_S kernel, expert
tiering).

### 8.3 The tokenizer diff (second-opinion item 5, closed 2026-10-03)

The real GLM-5.3-Flash tokenizer.json (154,856 BPE tokens + 36 specials, 321,649 merges; the
CHATGLM4 pre-regex appears verbatim, `(?i:'s|'t|...)` included) is now an accepted fixture:
`tools/vocab_gguf.py` writes it as a vocab-only GGUF (written with llama.cpp's own gguf-py
GGUFWriter - my hand-rolled writer desyncs llama's reader on large string arrays), and
`tools/tokenizer_diff.py` compares `llama-tokenize` against `strata_tokenizer` id for id.

One real bug: the ignore_merges whole-vocab lookup ran on the RAW pre-token piece instead of the
byte-MAPPED one - identity for ASCII (every earlier test passed), wrong for any non-ASCII
exact-vocab piece.  Fixed to match llama-vocab.cpp L634 (map, then look up).

Result: exact agreement on the boundary corpus (504 ids) and its 60x repetition (30,360 ids) -
digits, combining marks, CRLF (the harness must read with newline=""), CJK, emoji, contractions,
specials, byte edges.  The transcription is verified at the id level, not just structurally.

### 8.4 The runner takes the big fixture (2026-10-03)

`glm_model_big_test` (a ctest fixture: tools/glm_big_fixture.py generates the 1.2 GB seed-777
GGUF into the build tree; the f64 expectation is committed under data/glm5-big-dumps/) streams
the same 512 tokens the llama.cpp cross-check used through the CUDA runner:

  * streaming vs batched at prefixes 100/250/511: bitwise 0;
  * state determinism (A, reset, B, reset, A): bitwise;
  * vs the f64 oracle over all 512 columns: **KL p50 2.0e-9, p99 5.1e-11-bounded, max 6.8e-9,
    argmax 512/512, median column |diff| 1.6e-5, zero columns beyond KL 1e-4** - i.e. on the
    real-cadence geometry with discards, clamps and gate saturation firing inside the runner,
    the runner matches the oracle BETTER than llama.cpp does (llama's 2 flips are its f32
    rounding, the runner's f32 order happens to match the f64 oracle's every argmax);
  * glm_model_test gains the advisor's two missing gates: state determinism and mutation
    sensitivity (a 4 KB mid-file weight corruption must move the logits > 1e-2, proving gate 1
    can catch a wrong model).

Suite: 10/10 (the 8 previous + the fixture generator + the big test).  One process note: the
commit-time expectation file was briefly TRANSPOSED (double transpose) - the gate caught it
immediately, which is the system working as designed.

## 9. Phase 4 - the source file and the wiring plan (opened 2026-10-03)

Source: unsloth/GLM-5.3-Flash-GGUF **UD-IQ1_S** (3 shards, 93.0 GB; sha256s recorded), downloaded
to the OS NVMe per the owner's instruction (the HDD and the second NVMe stream too slowly for
mmap serving; the second NVMe only stages).  The owner moved the Qwen IQ3_S quants (79 GB) to the
second NVMe to make room.

### 9.1 The file's actual recipe (parsed from shard 2's tensor directory)

* token_embd / output: **Q4_K**; dense-lead ffn_gate/up **Q5_K**, ffn_down **Q6_K**;
  attn_output **Q5_K** (Q6_K at blk.11), attn_q_a Q5_K/Q6_K;
* the absorbed MLA projections + the whole indexer attention set: **Q8_0**;
* ffn_gate_inp, indexer.proj, indexer_compressor_ape: **F32** (exactly the packer's F32 set);
* routed experts, DYNAMIC PER LAYER: gate and up ALWAYS share one type per layer (IQ1_S or
  IQ2_XXS), down is IQ3_XXS (IQ4_XS at blk.11/12) - so the pack's single gu_type per layer
  holds as designed; the types are **IQ1_S, IQ2_XXS, IQ3_XXS, IQ4_XS** - NOT the types the
  study's §3.5 assumed (IQ1_M/IQ2_XS/IQ2_S/IQ3_S gate-up, Q2_0/IQ4_NL down).  The advisor's
  "extend the native path to the source's types" is therefore resolved by the best possible
  answer: `native_fmt` is ggml-cpu trait-driven, so the four types work as-is (ggml-cpu has
  vec_dot for every i-quant); no new dequantizers.

### 9.2 The IQ1_S-first milestone: pack-backed Glm5Model

Keep the verified runner math (mHC/KDA/DSA/hc kernels) and teach it to serve the pack:

1. load index.txt + native_experts.txt; dequant the DENSE tensors to the existing F32 device
   arena at load (token_embd+output alone are 2x2.5 GB in F32 - fits alongside ~24 GB of
   untouched V100);
2. route the MoE loop through the engine's CPU native expert path (FileExpertSource mmap +
   prefetch, native_gu_rows/native_down_rows) instead of the device-gemv F32 slabs;
3. fix kNativeActBytes/kNativeHBytes: they are sized for qwen4exp's 2560/640-wide activations;
   GLM needs 4096/2048 (Q8_K ~4.2 KB / ~2.1 KB) - a hard overflow if left as-is;
4. un-refuse glm5-next in the driver gate once the above serves; FAMILIES entry last.

The full production-driver graph path (captured graphs, the server, the glm5-next WeightTable
in layout.cpp) stays a later milestone; the layout tables are qwen4exp-specific down to the
tensor names.


# GLM MMQ correctness on Windows CUDA

On Windows with CUDA 13.2, batched GLM prefill could produce incoherent replies,
repeat previous turns or invent a translation task. The same model weights and
prompt worked through token-at-a-time prefill. Disabling the CPU expert lane or
fused SwiGLU did not resolve the problem.

## Cause and fix

The IQ1_S, IQ2_S and IQ3_S MMQ tile loaders read bytes by aliasing local packed
`int` / `int2` values through `uint8_t *`. In the affected NVCC build this produced
incorrect codebook indices, including out-of-bounds global reads. Extracting
unsigned bytes with shifts and masks preserves the packed format without these
local pointer aliases. The correction applies to both fused and plain products.

Compute Sanitizer also found destination-ID reads beyond the last partial expert
tile. Padded shared-memory entries now receive zero instead of reading an unused
ID. The output stores already exclude these padded columns.

The changes live in `third_party/ggml/mmq-fused-swiglu.patch`, which CMake applies
to its build-tree copy of the pinned GGML sources. Rebuild Maya after updating;
editing the installed Python server alone cannot correct the native kernels.

## Measurements

Measured against main commit `4615f346d9311dd1ce9f5738ab04446f023a6e42`, on an NVIDIA
RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell (sm_120), Windows, driver 596.86, CUDA 13.2, with Maya-M.

The regression test previously compared fused and plain products only. Both
could be wrong in the same way. It now also compares sampled outputs against
GGML CPU dequantization and independent scalar double-precision dot products.
The 0.04 relative-L2 threshold accommodates the MMQ path's Q8 activation rounding.

| Format | Independent dot relative L2 before | After |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| IQ1_S | 1.18493 | 0.0107327 |
| IQ2_S | 0.778627 | 0.00366127 |
| IQ3_S | 0.966161 | 0.00720439 |

The full-model diagnostic's KL divergence from token-at-a-time prefill decreased
from 3.48662072 to 0.0674770672; the next-token argmax changed from a mismatch to a
match. This measurement followed the byte-extraction fix, before the additional
partial-ID guards. The final guarded kernels passed targeted Compute Sanitizer
with zero errors.

Twenty-six real-server cases passed with the final kernels, including latest-turn
corrections, conversation switches and prefix reuse, streaming, cancellation and
recovery, literal role markers, thinking off/low/high, and prompts up to 31851
tokens. A subsequent 229041-token conversation with a 32768-token output allowance
retrieved a code from its first message. These runs also included the separately
submitted small-output reasoning-budget fix.

The numerical output changes intentionally: the original affected MMQ products
were incorrect. These measurements establish the failure and correction on this
machine; they do not establish identical rounding or performance on every GPU.
Other backends and CUDA toolkits were not exercised in these hardware runs.

## Reproduce

Configure a CUDA build with `STRATA_BUILD_TESTS=ON`. Stop the resident model before
running the standalone GPU tests so they have enough free VRAM.

```sh
cmake --build build --target glm_mmq_glu_test glm_prefill_probe
build/glm_mmq_glu_test
compute-sanitizer --tool memcheck --error-exitcode 88 build/glm_mmq_glu_test --iq-s-only
build/glm_prefill_probe /path/to/pack prompt-ids.csv /path/to/new-output-directory
```

The probe needs a real GLM pack and at least nine comma-separated token IDs; it
writes per-layer diagnostics and both logit vectors. The model-free MMQ test is
the targeted regression. For real conversation checks, start the server and run:

```sh
python tools/check_glm_conversations.py --url http://127.0.0.1:8080 \
  --tokenizer /path/to/pack/tokenizer --output /path/to/results
```

The conversation harness preserves the Italian prompts used during diagnosis.
It checks the output cap's rejection against the server's reported context size.


# Automatic HIP prompt transport selection

For an eligible tensor-parallel pair, startup compares the ordinary host-mapped reduction, exact-F32 AR2, and direct peer-VRAM reduction. It selects the lowest median GPU time at the configured full prefill-chunk size. The peer path preserves member-0-plus-member-1 F32 addition order; it changes transport, not routing or weight precision. A faster collective is not a guarantee of the same percentage improvement in whole-model prefill.

`MAYA_DS4_HIP_PEER_AR` is off by default (opt-in until it is measured on a two-GPU AMD PC). Set it in a serving config's `env`; native launches use `DS4_HIP_PEER_AR`. The choices are:

- `auto`: compare compatible transports once during model loading.
- `0` or unset: retain the existing transport, with no peer allocation or timing probe.
- `1`: force compatible peer transport without timing it against alternatives.

Both ranks must support HIP peer access, fine-grained device allocations and a queryable steady wall clock. IDs come from the tensor-parallel group; no card models, bus addresses or GPU numbers are hard-coded. Unsupported pairs retain host transport. Explicit `MAYA_DS4_AR2=1/2` and flow groups retain their chosen transport without calibration; explicit AR2 `0` excludes AR2 from the comparison. When AR2 is unset, only its exact-F32 mode (`1`), not its FP16 mode (`2`), is a candidate.

Only two-member groups using the batch prompt loader are eligible. Single-GPU execution performs no peer queries, allocation or timing. Groups of three or more, decode/verification windows, logits gathering and pipeline handoffs keep their existing paths. `MAYA_DS4_AR2_DECODE` remains independent.

Automatic mode logs `PEER_AR_AUTO` with GPU IDs, payload size, sample count, candidate median times, selected transport, calibration duration and completion status. An active peer pair also logs `exact peer prefill all-reduce enabled`; do not infer activation from the environment setting alone.

## Startup calibration

The probe uses private synthetic inputs, one warmup per candidate and up to six rotated timing rounds, requiring at least three samples per candidate. Every result is checked exactly before it can be selected. Flags and sequence state are reset before inference; model activations, weights and expert placement are untouched. Peer buffers are released if host or AR2 wins. The chosen transport stays fixed for that model instance.

The target is roughly 0.5–2 seconds of startup overhead, with a three-second measurement budget per eligible group (`DS4_PEER_AR_PROBE_MS`), started after that group's setup (peer enable, allocations, kernel preload). A probe that runs out of budget but drains keeps the host transport with a log line; only a probe that does not drain (10 s) stops the start. A current R9700-pair primitive test completed calibration in 237 ms at 4096 x 5120 floats (host 18.16 ms, AR2 7.14 ms, peer 3.46 ms per reduction). These are collective timings, not end-to-end model speeds. An incomplete, safely drained probe keeps the existing transport. Incorrect results or undrained work refuse model loading. GPU driver calls cannot be forcibly interrupted by this userspace budget; this is not a hard recovery deadline for driver hangs.

## Memory and failure handling

Scratch is allocated after expert placement, without removing experts to make room. Each rank needs two receive slots of `prefill_chunk * model_dimension * sizeof(float)`, plus flags and error records: approximately 80 MiB at chunk 2048/dimension 5120, or 160 MiB at chunk 4096. Automatic calibration additionally needs one private input buffer (80 MiB per rank at chunk 4096/dimension 5120), and initialization leaves at least 64 MiB free. If either rank's initialization fails before a probe launches, both temporary allocations are released and the group keeps its previous transport.

Calls on each rank's compute stream have matching shapes and sequence numbers. Two receive slots prevent a producer from overwriting data the other rank still needs. Per-block completion flags publish payload stores after a system fence. Every output uses the same rank-order `__fadd_rn` as the exact host transport. Host-sequence-based graph replay is not supported.

A missing peer has a two-second device deadline. It uses `wall_clock64()` calibrated by `hipDeviceAttributeWallClockRate`, rather than a GPU core-cycle counter: a previous R9700 run recorded a negative core-counter interval that became a spurious immediate timeout after unsigned conversion. An unsupported clock query leaves host transport selected.

Timeouts set a sticky error and retain the first diagnostic (rank, sequence, observed sequence, block, payload size and elapsed/budget ticks). Later queued peer calls drain. The model checks transport status after prompt workers finish, before accepting the request. A launch error, timeout or other prompt-worker failure with an active peer group invalidates the model; reset does not clear this state, and reload is required. The port uses upstream's coordinated chunk-boundary cancellation; cancellation followed by model reuse still needs current-base validation. This does not establish recovery from arbitrary GPU/driver hangs.

## Tests and historical measurements

Build a HIP/gfx1201 tree, then:

```sh
cmake --build build-hip --target maya-ds4 ds4_peer_allreduce_test -j 4
HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0,1 ./build-hip/ds4_peer_allreduce_test
```

Choose the two visible IDs appropriate to the machine. The standalone test needs about 800 MiB spare VRAM per rank and 640 MiB pinned host memory. It returns 77 when the selected pair lacks peer access, a queryable clock or sufficient spare VRAM. It compares 256 chained variable-size collectives against the existing host transport, with guarded outputs, signed zero, subnormals, extreme finite values, sequence wraparound and payloads through 8192 x 5120 floats. It also checks automatic selection against all three transports, a zero-budget fallback, explicit exclusion of AR2, first-sequence exact reuse after calibration, invalid argument rejection, an absent peer with a 50 ms physical deadline, and 32 subsequent poisoned calls preserving the first error record. The test's reset helper is only for isolated cases; the runtime never resets a failed transport.

Current-base full-model startup checks passed in auto, disabled, explicit AR2=1, and single-GPU modes. Automatic selection chose peer in 222 ms; auto/off/AR2 completed the same 1,213-token prompt with identical short greedy text. Disabled, explicit-AR2 and single-GPU runs emitted no calibration or peer-enable line. This smoke check does not establish full-logit parity or broad model quality.

Earlier full-model tests on our experimental EXL3 stack measured **9.2332% lower warm 2048-token prefill latency**, with a 95% interval of 8.9991–9.4673% across six balanced blocks/24 trials. Median latency was 7456.793 ms with host transport versus 6758.643 ms with peer transport. Cold results were inconclusive and no decode speedup was established. Scratch stayed allocated in both arms, keeping expert placement fixed; cold/warm/repeated logits and 80 greedy tokens matched independent references exactly.

Those tests used an older experimental engine with additional scalar EXL3, host-packing, predecode, copy-wait and transfer-pruning changes. They are evidence for this technique, **not a current-upstream end-to-end measurement**. Source and raw results are recorded in [fork PR #15](https://github.com/LostVector/project-maya/pull/15), with timeout diagnostics and the steady-clock fix in [#35](https://github.com/LostVector/project-maya/pull/35) and [#38](https://github.com/LostVector/project-maya/pull/38). Current-base end-to-end performance, cancellation/reuse and model-level failure-injection checks remain separate validation work.

## NVIDIA (CUDA)

The same transport builds on CUDA (`DS4_CUDA_PEER_AR`, a cmake option, on): `src/ds4/ds4_peer_cuda_compat.cuh` maps the
HIP runtime names it uses (fine-grained allocations are plain `cudaMalloc`; `wall_clock64()` reads `%globaltimer`, a
wall-clock rate of 1,000,000 kHz). `MAYA_DS4_HIP_PEER_AR` turns it on as on HIP; the log lines are the same
(`PEER_AR_AUTO`, `exact peer prefill all-reduce enabled on GPU0/GPU1`).

- **CUDA's lazy module loading:** a kernel's first launch on a device loads it there, and with peer access on that can
  wait for running work - beside a rank already spinning for its partner, for good (the probe submits both ranks from
  one thread). `peer_ar_preload()` loads the transports' kernels on each device before anything spins.
- **The partner wait** is `DS4_PEER_AR_TIMEOUT_S` (60 s; it was two). A 469K-token prompt had one rank more than two
  seconds ahead of its partner at one all-reduce, a skew the host transport waits out.
- `ds4_peer_allreduce_test` builds on CUDA too (`cmake --build build --target ds4_peer_allreduce_test`,
  `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0,1 ./build/ds4_peer_allreduce_test`).

Measured on 8x RTX 3090 (NVLink pairs 0-1, 2-3, 4-5, 6-7), DeepSeek EXL3 2.9 bpw, `--tp 2`: per chunk all-reduce
(20.97M floats) host 12.7-13.3 ms, AR2 11.5-12.0 ms, peer 1.98 ms; fresh prompts 9K / 37K / 150K / 469K tokens
+14 / +33 / +30 / +14-17 % (2,253-2,352 against 1,945-2,008 tok/s at 469K); decode and greedy output unchanged;
the self-test exact on every NVLink pair and on a PCIe P2P pair.


# Maya on the IBM Power System AC922 (POWER9, ppc64le)

The AC922 is IBM's POWER9 machine from the V100 era: two POWER9 sockets, six Tesla V100-SXM2-16GB cards on
NVLink 2.0, and a coherent link (ATS) that lets the GPUs read host memory directly. Maya builds and runs on it
from source, and GLM-5.3-Flash holds its full **1,048,576-token** context on all six cards.

Measured on one AC922 (see [Measured](#measured)): **72 tokens/s** decoding short answers, **1,297** and
**2,102 tokens/s** reading 70K- and 183K-token prompts, 14.7 of each card's 16 GB in use. One setting is not
optional here: [`MAYA_GLM_NUMA=0`](#the-one-setting-that-is-not-optional) - without it the machine panics.

## The machine

| | |
| --- | --- |
| GPU | 6x Tesla V100-SXM2-16GB, compute capability 7.0 (sm_70), 96 GB in all; NVLink 2.0 between the cards of a socket |
| CPU | 2x IBM POWER9, 176 threads (SMT4), ppc64le, **no AVX2** |
| Memory | 183 GB |
| OS | RHEL 8.10, kernel 4.18.0-553.el8_10.ppc64le |
| Compiler | gcc-toolset-13; RHEL 8's system GCC 8.5 cannot compile C++20 |
| Driver / CUDA | 535.216.03 / CUDA 12.4 (`-DCMAKE_CUDA_ARCHITECTURES=70`) |
| Power | 250 W per card (`gpu-power-limit.service`); the cards draw ~70 W decoding, up to 226 W in prefill |

## Bringing it up

RHEL 8 ships GCC 8.5, which rejects `-std=c++20`; Maya's CMake requires C++20, so put the newer compiler first
on `PATH` before anything that builds the engine:

```sh
source /opt/rh/gcc-toolset-13/enable
./maya.sh --check
./maya.sh --setup --yes --model glm-maya-s24 --gpus 0,1,2,3,4,5 --context 1048576 --download-model
```

`--check` lists the six cards, CUDA 12.4, the driver, RAM and this line, which is expected on POWER (the CPU
expert lane falls back to ggml-cpu's VSX kernels):

```
  [!]  the CPU (POWER9, altivec supported) has no AVX2: the engine runs, its CPU expert lane on ggml's own
       kernels (slower; ...). MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE=0 turns it off
```

The numbers on this page come from a from-source build:

```sh
cmake -S . -B build -G Ninja -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release -DSTRATA_ENABLE_CUDA=ON \
  -DCMAKE_CUDA_ARCHITECTURES=70 -DSTRATA_NATIVE_EXPERTS=ON -DSTRATA_BUILD_TESTS=OFF \
  -DCMAKE_CUDA_COMPILER=/usr/local/cuda-12.4/bin/nvcc \
  -DCMAKE_CUDA_HOST_COMPILER=/opt/rh/gcc-toolset-13/root/usr/bin/g++ \
  -DSTRATA_GGML_DIR=$HOME/builds/env/llama.cpp-3cf0325
cmake --build build --target maya -j 16
```

That is about five minutes on 16 jobs. The other engines build the same way with their own targets
(`maya-ds4`, `maya-mm3`).

### What the ppc64le port changes

| file | why |
| --- | --- |
| `setup.py` | `PPC` detection, and `cpu_info()` returns POWER's `cpu` line: POWER9's `/proc/cpuinfo` has no `flags:` line, so the x86 probe raised `AttributeError` before anything ran |
| `CMakeLists.txt` | `STRATA_CPU_PPC64` / `STRATA_CPU_ARM64`; the ARM `-march=armv8.6-a` flags are gated on aarch64 (GCC on POWER rejects them). ppc64le then takes the `GGML_NATIVE` path, where ggml-cpu reads `/proc/cpuinfo` and builds with `-mcpu=power9` itself |
| `CMakeLists.txt` | `CXX_EXTENSIONS ON` for `ggml-cpu`: its PowerPC quants use the `vector` keyword the file-scope C++20 extensions-off would remove |
| `maya.py` | EXL3 quants are refused on POWER as on ARM: their expert kernels are x86 AVX2/AVX-512 |
| `include/strata/platform/cpu_relax.hpp`, `src/ds4/ds4_pool.hpp` | POWER branches: `or 27,27,27` as the spin hint and `lwsync` as the store fence before a GPU polls a doorbell in pinned memory |

## The one setting that is not optional

```json
"env": { "MAYA_GLM_NUMA": "0" }
```

Maya pins the expert tier that does not fit in VRAM with `mmap` + `mbind` + **`cudaHostRegister`**
(`src/core/glm_fast_path.cu`, `numa_pinned()`), which it chooses automatically on any host with two or more NUMA
nodes. On POWER9 the V100s reach host memory through ATS, and the driver's UVM migration also moves *registered*
pages: that path is fatal here.

```
kernel BUG at mm/migrate.c:701        -> migrate_vma_pages
  nv_migrate_vma                      [nvidia_uvm]
  uvm_migrate_pageable                [nvidia_uvm]
  service_ats_requests                [nvidia_uvm]     <- a V100 faulting on a host page
```

It panicked the machine mid-benchmark (kdump caught it; the box rebooted on its own), and it was also taking half
the speed:

| | `cudaHostRegister` (default) | `cudaHostAlloc` (`MAYA_GLM_NUMA=0`) |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| decode, mean | 40-41.5 tokens/s | **74-76** |
| prefill, 2K / 8K | 407 / 557 | **498 / 722** |

Both columns were measured in one session; the point is the ratio, which does not depend on the cap - the default
path halved decode and cost a quarter of prefill.

`MAYA_GLM_NUMA=0` makes `numa_pinned()` return null, and the engine falls back to `cudaHostAlloc` - the path the
AC922 needs. Check that it reached the engine, since `maya.py` passes the config's `env` to the engine under the
name it reads:

```sh
tr '\0' '\n' < /proc/$(pgrep -x maya)/environ | grep GLM_NUMA       # STRATA_GLM_NUMA=0
```

## Six GPUs, not four

The quant is **Maya-S24** (`glm-maya-s24`, 94.7 GB): 4-bit attention and shared experts, made for cards of 24 GB
or less. On six cards ~74 GB of experts fit in VRAM (98.4 % of the routes hit) and ~25 GB in the pinned RAM tier.
Four cards measured **3.6 tokens/s**: too little room for both tiers, so every token streams its experts.

## Context

GLM-5.3-Flash is 34 linear-attention layers and 11 MLA (full-attention) layers; only the 11 grow with tokens, and
`--kv int8` halves them. With `--kv-streaming on` only the resident window stays in VRAM and the rest lives in
pinned RAM, which makes the full context cheap:

| `--max-context` | decode | prefill 2K / 8K | KV in VRAM, all cards | RAM tier | pool per card |
| ---: | ---: | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 65,536 | 73.5 | 495 / 717 | 0.55 GB | 21.1 GB | 12.30 GB |
| 131,072 | 75.8 | 495 / 715 | 0.62 GB | 21.3 GB | 12.28 GB |
| 262,144 | 75.0 | 484 / 711 | 0.81 GB | 21.9 GB | 12.25 GB |
| 524,288 | 71.6 | 484 / 696 | 1.17 GB | 23.2 GB | 12.18 GB |
| **1,048,576** | **72.6** | 468 / 694 | **1.93 GB** | 25.4 GB | 12.05 GB |

Going from 65,536 to 1,048,576 costs about 1.4 GB of VRAM, 4 GB of RAM and 1 % of decode. All five sizes loaded
and ran.

## Measured

Bench as the README's tables describe it: three 256-token greedy answers, thinking off, prefill at 2,048 and
8,192 tokens.

| | decode | prefill 2K / 8K |
| --- | --- | --- |
| three runs | 72.6 / 72.2 / 71.2 (mean **72.0**) | 464 / 692 |

Long context, one request at a time, prompts with a phrase planted mid-document:

| prompt | read at | the phrase |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| 69,646 tokens | 1,297 tokens/s | found |
| 182,746 tokens | 2,102 tokens/s | found |

Decode at depth: **66.8 tokens/s** over a 48-token answer at 70K, **32.5** at 183K - the KV streaming reads grow
with the context, so the deep answer is the slow one.

### Which quant

Two of Maya's quants were measured here, Maya-S and Maya-S24. This machine serves **Maya-S24** and it is the
faster one by a wide margin. Both at 1,048,576 tokens, one after the other in the same hour, same arguments:

| | Maya-S | Maya-S24 |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| decode, mean of three answers | 62.1 / 62.9 | **72.8** |
| prefill 2K / 8K | 454 / 682 | 467 / 694 |
| expert slots in VRAM | 10,984 | 11,229 |
| RAM tier, slots | 4,030 | 3,785 |
| RAM fetches per token | 6.2-7.3 | 5.1-6.0 |

Maya-S keeps its attention and shared experts in 6 bits, about 0.25 GB more on each card, and at 1M context the
smaller pool sends ~20 % more of each token's experts to the RAM tier: **-13.6 % decode** for a quality change
the quant's notes put at KL 0.444 -> 0.421 against the FP8 model. On 16 GB cards that is the wrong trade: ten
tokens a second for 0.02 of KL. Maya-M (116 GB) was not measured: it can only have a smaller pool than Maya-S,
so it cannot be faster here.

## What did not help

Each knob below was measured once against stock, in the same hour and at the same cap:

| setting | decode | verdict |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| stock | 72.0 | - |
| `MAYA_GLM_COMPLETE_POOL=0` | 72.9 | neutral |
| `MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE=0` | 71.6 | neutral (the CPU lane takes 0.1 experts a token here) |
| `MAYA_GLM_ROUTE_RANK=0 MAYA_GLM_ROUTE_FAST=0` | 68.6 | **worse** - the new route kernels are worth ~3.4 tokens/s on a V100 |

Two measurement rules this machine taught:

- **Do not sample `nvidia-smi` in a loop during a run.** A 2-second sampler cost 14 % (41.2 -> 35.5 tokens/s).
  Read the cards before and after, never during.
- **One cold run is not a number.** The first bench after a rebuild read 62.8 tokens/s with `cpu lane 5.03/tok`
  and a 97.3 % VRAM hit rate; every run since reads 0.11/tok and 98.4 %. If a run is 10 % off and its CPU-lane
  figure is off with it, run it again.

## Known limits

- **Pictures do not work**: the vision tower's BF16 GEMMs need sm_80, and the V100 is sm_70. The server still
  advertises image input when the pack carries a vision tower, so send text only.
- **EXL3 quants are x86-only** (their expert kernels are AVX2/AVX-512); the port refuses them on POWER with the
  same message as on ARM. Use the GGUF/Maya quants.
- **CUDA 13 cannot build sm_70**; stay on the 12.x toolkit.
- **Only the GLM engine was built and run here** (`maya`). `maya-ds4` and `maya-mm3` compile from the same
  CMake tree but have not been measured on this machine.
- **Prefill is the phase that reaches the power limit**: one card reports `SW Power Cap` at 226 W during
  prefill, while decode draws ~70 W a card.


# Intel Arc: the Maya-XS DeepSeek GGUFs on llama.cpp's SYCL backend

**This is outside Maya.** Maya's engine has no Intel backend ([#37](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/37)),
and we have not tested anything on this page: it is what @Jumbomuffin777 reported in
[#401](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/401), written down so others with an Intel Arc card can find it.
The setup, the dashboard, DSpark drafting and the other Maya features do not run on this path.

## What was reported

The Maya-XS DeepSeek-V4.1 GGUFs run on Intel Arc through llama.cpp's SYCL backend and the `deepseek41` pull request
[ggml-org/llama.cpp#30817](https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/pull/30817) (closed unmerged on October 27, 2026), after
a small patch to that pull request's model loader:

- Maya's GGUFs store the Engram metadata as `deepseek41.engram.*` with u32 offsets and primes, one Engram table per
  layer (`blk.1/14.engram_embd`, with local offsets) and no `hash_layer_count`.
- That pull request's loader expects `ple.*` keys and one global `per_layer_token_embd`.
- The patch accepts both layouts; files from the pull request's own converter load as before.
- llama.cpp's own DeepSeek-V4.1 support, [ggml-org/llama.cpp#31086](https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/pull/31086)
  (in releases since b11870), expects its converter's layout (`deepseek41.ple.*`, one `per_layer_token_embd`, an
  `engram.token_map`), so the Maya-XS GGUFs do not load on a llama.cpp release; the #30817 head with the patch is
  still needed. Re-checked on October 29, 2026.

The reporter's branch with the patch and a longer write-up (build steps, flags, known limits) is here:
[Jumbomuffin777:docs/intel-arc-llamacpp-sycl](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/compare/main...Jumbomuffin777:project-maya:docs/intel-arc-llamacpp-sycl).
We have not reviewed or built it; read the patch before you run it.

## What was measured

Their PC: Intel Arc Pro B70 32 GB + B65 32 GB + B60 24 GB, Ryzen 9 9950X, 64 GB DDR5, Linux. Model: Maya-XS IQ1_S,
64K context.

| Run | Prefill | Decode |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1,934-token prompt | 45.6 tok/s | 10.9 tok/s |
| 2,470 tokens through an OpenAI-compatible gateway | 41.0 tok/s | 10.8 tok/s |

Tool calls, streaming and an 11K-token recall check passed.

## GLM-5.3-Flash Maya-S v2

Reported later in [#401](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/401): the Maya-S v2 IQ2_XXS GLM GGUFs
(architecture `glm5-next`) load on stock llama.cpp [v0.8.0](https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/releases/tag/v0.8.0)
with the SYCL backend and no patch. On the same PC, with the routed experts of six layers (about 11.4 GiB) in host RAM
and the rest on the three cards, a 2,377-token prompt with 128 generated tokens read at 68.3 tok/s and generated at
13.6 tok/s. Other GLM quants were not measured. The command is in the reporter's write-up linked above.

## Limits

- Text only.
- No DSpark drafting and no Engram streaming: the model is placed by llama.cpp's own rules.
- DeepSeek depends on a llama.cpp pull request that was closed unmerged, plus the loader patch above.
- The numbers are one person's, on three Arc cards, with the two quants above.

The patch, the measurements and the write-up were prepared by the reporter with an AI coding agent on their own hardware;
they reviewed them. Questions about this setup belong in [#401](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/401).


# Maya on Apple Silicon (Metal)

Back to the [README](../README.md).

Experimental: GLM-5.3-Flash on a Mac with Apple Silicon, on its GPU through Metal, with the same dashboard and the same
OpenAI- and Anthropic-compatible API as on Linux and Windows (streamed answers, tool calls, thinking levels). It reads
pictures (`maya-vision`, the image encoder, runs on the GPU too), and it drafts
with the model's MTP block or the DFlash2 drafter (`--draft dflash2`) when that is faster on the Mac. One conversation at a time;
DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash and Qwen-3.8-Flash-Next are NVIDIA / AMD only for now. MiniMax-M3.1-Flash has its own
Metal engine (below): newer and rougher than GLM's, text only, and not wired into the setup yet.

**Status: experimental.** Mac support is a community contribution. Every change is built and unit-tested on GitHub's
Apple Silicon runners (the `macos` workflow: the engine, its sampler and the small synthetic-model tests), but the
Maya team has no Mac and has not run it with the real model. If you try it on a Mac, please tell us how it went in a
[GitHub issue](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues): the chip and its unified memory, the model, the speed
`./maya.sh --bench` prints, and anything that went wrong (`./maya.sh --report` collects the details).

```sh
./maya.sh --backend metal --check            # this Mac: chip, unified memory, Xcode's compiler, CMake
./maya.sh --backend metal --setup            # build maya-metal, download the model, write the config
./maya.sh                                    # start it: dashboard at http://127.0.0.1:8080
```

You need macOS on Apple Silicon (M1 or newer), the Xcode Command Line Tools (`xcode-select --install`), CMake 3.24+
(`brew install cmake`) and Python 3.10+. On a Mac `./maya.sh` picks the Metal backend by itself when nothing is set
up yet.

## How it runs

`maya-metal` (src/metal) is its own engine. It runs the whole forward pass as ggml graphs on ggml's Metal backend,
from the pinned llama.cpp that Maya already builds against, with the GGUF read directly: no pack. Its graph follows
llama.cpp's `glm5-next` model op for op (the mHC streams, the KDA linear-attention layers, the k-pool sparse indexer
and the nope MLA, the MoE), and the numbers are ggml's, the same kernels Maya's CUDA engine was validated against.

What is Maya's own is the memory plan. A Mac has one pool of unified memory, usually smaller than the model:

- **Dense weights, the KV cache and the recurrent state stay resident** (about 14 GB for Maya-S, plus the context).
- **The routed experts (95 % of the model) live in a slot pool.** If every expert fits (128 GB Macs and up for the
  smaller quants), they are all loaded at start and a token runs as one graph. Otherwise the pool takes what the
  GPU's working set leaves: the graph is cut after each MoE router, the engine reads that layer's top 8, reads the
  experts that are not resident from the SSD with parallel reads, and runs them from their slots. The least
  recently used experts make room. At start the pool is filled from this Mac's own expert profile, which the engine
  learns from every request (`expert-profile.bin` beside the model), or from the shipped one
  (`data/expert-profile-glm.bin`) the first time.
- The GPU never pages model data in from the file: that is far slower than the engine's parallel reads into the
  pool.

On a Mac with less unified memory than the model, decode speed depends on how many experts a token needs that are
not resident, and so on the SSD.

## MiniMax-M3.1-Flash

**Experimental**, and newer than the GLM engine. `maya-metal-mm3` (src/metal/mm3_metal.cpp, beside the GLM engine) runs MiniMax-M3.1-Flash from Maya's own GGUF
quants (the set's `-maya-indexers.bin` side file is read automatically). Its graph follows `tools/mm3/ref.py` op
for op: Gemma norms, per-head q/k norms, NeoX partial RoPE, MiniMax Sparse Attention on every layer (the indexer
scores read back to the host, which keeps the top blocks exactly as the reference selects them), swigluoai MLPs,
sigmoid routing with the correction bias. Sampling and the serve protocol are the GLM engine's, shared code and
all (`maya-metal-mm3 --serve`, driven by serve/server.py like the other engines).

The memory plan is the simple one the quants were made for: **every expert is resident**. Maya-XS (93 GB) fits a
128 GB Mac with room for the caches; Maya-S (122 GB) is past a 128 GB Mac's GPU working set, so XS is the Mac
quant. With nothing streamed there is no expert pool and no drafting yet (a request's `spec` keys are accepted;
plain decoding writes the same tokens), and pictures are not wired (the vision tower stays with the CUDA engine
for now), so MiniMax on a Mac is text only. `./maya.sh --backend metal --setup` does not offer MiniMax yet; run
it by hand:

```sh
cmake -S . -B build-metal -DMAYA_ENABLE_METAL=ON
cmake --build build-metal -j --target maya-metal-mm3
./build-metal/maya-metal-mm3 --gguf <the Maya-XS set's .gguf> --ids 1,2,3 --n 64
```

Its parity gate is `metal_mm3_test` (in the same `ctest` run): a synthetic 3-layer MiniMax (one dense lead
layer, two MoE layers, 17 index blocks so the selection must drop one) against a numpy port of the reference
forward in `tools/mm3_metal_fixture.py` - logits within 4.2e-5 relative L2 on the Metal GPU (gate 1e-3), a
sliced prompt equal to a one-shot one, greedy decoding token for token; `tools/test_mm3_serve.py`
(`metal_mm3_serve_test`) drives the serve protocol the same way. The contributor ran the real Maya-XS set on a 128 GB Mac Studio (M5 Max); bench
numbers from other Macs are as welcome here as GLM's. The port's notes: [PORT-MINIMAX-METAL.md](PORT-MINIMAX-METAL.md).

## Speed

Not measured by the Maya team yet (no Mac): your `./maya.sh --bench` numbers are welcome in an issue. What to expect:
with less unified memory than the model, part of the experts come from the SSD as a conversation needs them, and decode
is bound by those reads rather than by the GPU - so MTP drafting is off then (`MAYA_GLM_MTP_DECODE=1` turns it on). A
Mac that holds every expert (128 GB and more for the smaller quants) decodes from memory, where drafting pays.

The pool takes no more than the Mac has free when Maya starts, less `MAYA_GLM_RAM_HEADROOM_GB` (6 GB): unified memory
is shared with every other program, and a pool past it would be swapped (a slice that reads swapped experts stalls
for seconds). Close other large programs before starting Maya for the most experts in memory.

## Drafting

Decoding drafts a few tokens ahead and checks them in one pass of the model (a verify window), so each pass over the
weights can write several tokens. The drafts never change the answer: every row of the window is picked exactly as
plain decoding picks it (the same sampler, the same seed counter, the same penalties), and the tokens after the first
draft the model would not have written are dropped, the recurrent state rolled back to that row.
A window's rows are not the same bits as single tokens, though (within 4.9e-6 of them, below): ggml-metal picks its
matrix kernel by the column count, so where two tokens nearly tie, a window of another length can pick the other one.
The MTP draft length adapts to measured timing and is kept across requests, so the same greedy request can take
another path the second time (a prefix-cache hit after a cold prompt, say). `MAYA_GLM_MTP_FIXED=N` fixes the length;
`spec=1` (or `MAYA_GLM_MTP_DECODE=0`) decodes one token at a time.

- **MTP**: the model's own NextN block drafts with chained greedy steps; the draft length adapts to how many drafts are
  accepted and what a round costs on this Mac (as on CUDA: `MAYA_GLM_MTP_DRAFT`, `MAYA_GLM_MTP_FIXED`,
  `MAYA_GLM_MTP_ADAPT`). It is on when every expert fits in memory, where a token is GPU work and a window writes
  several for about the price of one. With experts streamed from the SSD a window needs more of them than one token
  does, so it is off there (`MAYA_GLM_MTP_DECODE=1` turns it on, `0` off).
- **DFlash2** (`--draft dflash2` at setup): a 0.7 GB block-diffusion drafter reads five of the model's layers and
  drafts up to 7 tokens in one pass, kept while the drafter is confident (`spec_min_p`, `spec_n_min`, `spec_cum`).
- A request's `spec=1` turns drafting off; `MAYA_GLM_NO_MTP=1` for the engine.

## Accuracy

The engine holds the CUDA engine's GLM parity gates (`src/core/glm_model_test.cpp`, `glm_model_big_test.cpp`) on the
Metal GPU (`ctest` in a Metal build; the figures below are from the contributor's Mac - GitHub's runners repeat the
small fixture's tests on every change):

| Test | Gate | maya-metal |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `metal_model_test` (6 layers, against `ref/glm.py`) | logits within 2e-3; streamed = batched within 1e-5; greedy chain equal | max 2.7e-6; 3.6e-7; equal |
| `metal_model_big_test` (45 layers, 512 tokens, f64 oracle) | KL p99 < 1e-4, max KL < 0.1, argmax >= 505/512, streamed vs batched < 5e-5 | KL p99 2.1e-8, max 2.3e-2, 511/512, 2.3e-6 |
| the same, the expert pool at its smallest and 64-token slices | the same gates | the same numbers |
| `metal_model_window` / `metal_model_big_window` (verify windows of 1-8 rows, every third draft wrong) | tokens = plain decoding's | equal; logits within 4.9e-6 |
| `metal_model_mtp` / `metal_model_big_mtp` (MTP drafting, fixed lengths 1 / 2 / 4 and adaptive) | tokens = plain decoding's | equal |
| `metal_mm3_test` (MiniMax-M3.1, 3 layers, 2,176 tokens, against a numpy port of `tools/mm3/ref.py`) | logits within 1e-3; sliced = one-shot; greedy chain equal | 4.2e-5; equal; equal |

Two choices keep it at f32 accuracy where Metal would otherwise round:

- ggml-metal's matrix-matrix kernel rounds its tiles to half precision. Weights stored in float (the router, the mHC
  projections) therefore run as f32 matrix-vector products, one per token, so a prompt and a decode step give the
  same bits. Quantized weights use ggml-metal's matrix kernels as llama.cpp does.
- The MLA latent cache is F32 by default (about 22 KB per token; `--kv-f16` halves it, as the CUDA engine stores it).
  On the GPU the DSA layers' matrix products round it to half anyway, so it is F16 there; `--kv-f32`
  (`MAYA_METAL_KV_F32=1`) keeps it F32, and the fused sparse-attention kernel then reads F32 rows.

## Options

`maya-metal --help` lists them. The ones that matter:

| Option | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| `--max-context N` | context length (set by the setup) |
| `--expert-gb X` | unified memory for the expert pool, in GB (default: the GPU's working set less the dense weights, the cache and 6 GB) |
| `--expert-profile F` | the ranked expert profile that fills the pool at start (set by the setup) |
| `--io-threads N` | parallel SSD reads for expert misses (default 16) |
| `--kv-f16` | the MLA latent cache in F16 |
| `--kv-f32` | keep the MLA latent cache F32 on the GPU too (twice the cache memory; for accuracy A/B runs) |
| `--cpu` | run on the CPU through ggml-cpu (reference runs) |

`maya-metal --gguf MODEL.gguf --ids 1,2,3 --n 64` decodes 64 tokens greedily from those token ids and prints the
speed, the expert hit rate, the bytes read and where the time went.

## Building it by hand

```sh
cmake -S . -B build-metal -DMAYA_ENABLE_METAL=ON
cmake --build build-metal -j
ctest --test-dir build-metal          # the parity tests above (the 45-layer fixture needs Python with numpy)
```


# Experimental MI50 (gfx906) source-build validation

## Status

This change adds experimental HIP source-build support for AMD Instinct MI50 (`gfx906`). It does not change Maya's installer. Build commands below assume a fresh source checkout and an existing ROCm installation that supplies `gfx906` rocBLAS/Tensile libraries. The necessary rocBLAS/Tensile support is supplied separately; this change does not rebuild or redistribute it.

The patch is based on Project Maya `10e69a0eb11541004abb24f9fa47147e9002326f` (v1.1.50). Upstream v1.1.50 already includes `gfx906` CMake target registration and `__builtin_amdgcn_sdot4` (via PR #505 `097adc1`) and `defined(__gfx906__)` Wave64 `ds_swizzle` + `quad_perm` attention reductions and `hip_fast_signs` (via PR #529 `9690e37`). Its pinned GGML source is `ggml-org/llama.cpp` commit `3cf03257f219afbe7334045ff7c6a06ac68c627d`; all 1,376 tracked regular files in the GGML subtree matched the pinned tree exactly.

## Validated model configurations

| Model | Family / quant | Status on this patch |
|---|---|---|
| Maya-M | GLM-5.3-Flash, IQ2_S | Four-MI50 bounded checks passed |
| Maya-L | GLM-5.3-Flash, IQ3_S | Four-MI50 bounded checks passed |
| Maya-S | DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash | Validated via ds4_gguf_test (see Section 7); server bench below covers GLM-5.3 |

These are fresh checks of this patch, separate from earlier custom-build reports. Both GLM configurations allocated 204,800-token contexts; functional inputs were approximately 8K tokens, not full-capacity prompts.

## Test system and build environment

| Component | Recorded system |
|---|---|
| GPUs | 4 × AMD Instinct MI50, 32 GiB HBM each (`gfx906`; some tools print “16GB”) |
| CPUs | 2 × AMD EPYC 7452 |
| Memory | 467 GiB RAM |
| Storage | U.2 NVMe |
| GPU links | PCIe 4.0 x16 on all four MI50s; no xGMI |
| Process affinity | CPU 0-31,64-95; NUMA memory node 0 |
| ROCm | 7.2.0 |
| HIP compiler | AMD Clang 22.0.0git |
| Host compiler | GCC 13.3.0 |
| CMake | 3.28.3 |

Upstream accepts `gfx906` at configure time via #505. This pull request adds direct peer-to-peer PCIe transfers (`MAYA_GLM_P2P`), enables opt-in tuned IQ2_S expert dispatch on gfx906 (`STRATA_GLM_GU22_TUNED`), enables `DS4_IQ_STRICT_FP` on `gfx906` in `src/ds4/ds4_iq.cu`, and restores the wave64 64-lane logical-warp regression probe in `tests/hip/intrinsics.cpp`. Every gfx906 code path is guarded behind strict architecture checks (`#if defined(STRATA_USE_HIP)` and `enable_gfx906_peer_cached` / `allow_gfx906 && gfx906` / `__gfx906__`), leaving non-gfx906 GPUs completely unchanged.

The source build used HIP for `gfx906`, disabled CUDA, enabled tests, prefill MMQ, and native experts, and used the pinned GGML checkout. The build produced Maya and the applicable HIP parity targets. Sections 2–6 report the GLM-5.3 server benchmark suite; `ds4_gguf_test` kernel and quant parity for `src/ds4/ds4_iq.cu` is reported in Section 7.

For a fresh checkout, link the supplied `build-hip` directory only if its target was built from that same checkout; do not reuse a build directory from another source tree. A generic configure/build recipe is:

```sh
cmake -S . -B build-gfx906 \
  -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release \
  -DCMAKE_C_COMPILER=/usr/bin/cc \
  -DCMAKE_CXX_COMPILER=/usr/bin/c++ \
  -DCMAKE_HIP_COMPILER=/opt/rocm/lib/llvm/bin/clang++ \
  -DCMAKE_HIP_ARCHITECTURES=gfx906 \
  -DCMAKE_HIP_PLATFORM=amd \
  -DBUILD_SHARED_LIBS=OFF \
  -DSTRATA_BUILD_TESTS=ON \
  -DSTRATA_ENABLE_CUDA=OFF \
  -DSTRATA_ENABLE_HIP=ON \
  -DSTRATA_PREFILL_MMQ=ON \
  -DSTRATA_GGML_DIR=/path/to/pinned/llama.cpp \
  -DSTRATA_NATIVE_EXPERTS=ON \
  -DSTRATA_PLE_FIXTURE_DIR=/path/to/checkout/bench/micro \
  -DSTRATA_PORTABLE=OFF \
  -DSTRATA_WERROR=OFF
cmake --build build-gfx906 --target maya --parallel 8
```

To reconstruct the matched control, compare against upstream `v1.1.50` (`10e69a0eb11541004abb24f9fa47147e9002326f`):

```sh
git worktree add --detach ../maya-mi50-control 10e69a0eb11541004abb24f9fa47147e9002326f
```

Build the control separately with the same recipe and use identical model/configuration inputs. Do not share build directories between arms.

With serving stopped and exclusive access to the four GPUs, build and run the selected checks from the checkout root. Configure your compatible ROCm library paths first, including `ROCBLAS_TENSILE_LIBPATH` if required:

```sh
set -e
cmake --build build-gfx906 --parallel 8 --target \
  maya hip_intrinsics glm_mv_bench hip_prefill_native_batch \
  hip_glm_prefill_attention hip_glm_handoff glm_hc_parity glm_kda_parity \
  glm_ffn_parity glm_dsa_parity glm_layer_parity glm_model_test \
  iq1_s_parity hip_handoff hip_expert_cache_staging hip_prefill_mmq_parity strata-device
for gpu in 0 1 2 3; do
  export HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$gpu
  for test in hip_intrinsics hip_prefill_native_batch hip_glm_prefill_attention \
              hip_glm_handoff iq1_s_parity hip_handoff hip_expert_cache_staging \
              hip_prefill_mmq_parity; do
    build-gfx906/$test
  done
  for test in glm_hc_parity glm_kda_parity glm_ffn_parity glm_dsa_parity \
              glm_layer_parity glm_model_test strata-device; do
    build-gfx906/$test --selftest
  done
  build-gfx906/glm_mv_bench --parity-only
done
```

This is a selected GLM/HIP suite, not a blanket CTest or all-model qualification. DeepSeek-V4.1 / DS4 is outside this validation.

## Test results

| Check | Result |
|---|---:|
| HIP compatibility mapping check | Pass |
| Server tests | 342 run, 11 skipped |
| Installer/tools tests | 384 run, 28 skipped |
| Pinned GGML manifest | 1,376 of 1,376 tracked files byte-identical |
| Extended applicable parity set, each of four MI50s | 16 checks passed per card; 64 total |
| Same applicable parity set on the Radeon AI PRO R9700 regression build | 16 checks passed |
| Service restoration after validation windows | Pass |

A fused-prefill check printed `SKIP` on the R9700 run because that target is compiled only for `gfx1100`/`gfx1151`; it is not included in either applicable-suite count. This check does not establish fused-prefill support on `gfx906`.

## Model packs and run settings

The existing native packs were prepared with upstream `iq_pack.py --compat-bf16`, using standard nearest-even BF16 rounding. Maya-M uses IQ2_S; Maya-L uses IQ3_S. The shipped `data/expert-profile-glm.bin` matches the upstream profile data by hash. The performance rows below are for GLM-5.3-Flash Maya models. DeepSeek-V4.1 Maya-S is a distinct model and has no result in this validation.

The recorded control and candidate configuration used the same meaningful engine settings:

```text
--glm-pack <Maya-M-or-Maya-L-pack>
--max-context 204800 --max-new 32768
--prefill 1024 --kv int8
--layer-split auto --split-device 1,2,3
--expert-profile <project>/data/expert-profile-glm.bin

STRATA_GLM_WARM_PROMPT=1
STRATA_GLM_COMPLETE_POOL=0
STRATA_GLM_PREFILL_MB=2048
STRATA_GLM_PREFILL_SUB=1024
STRATA_GLM_RESERVE_MB=1024
STRATA_GLM_SLOT_KEEP=0
STRATA_GLM_VRAM_EVICT=least
STRATA_GLM_SLOT_DIR=<writable-per-run-directory>

HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0,1,2,3
CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0,1,2,3
HSA_ENABLE_SDMA=0
ROCBLAS_TENSILE_LIBPATH=<compatible-gfx906-rocblas-library-directory>

service defaults: temperature 1.0, top_p 0.95, reasoning effort high
validation requests override: temperature 0, reasoning effort none, max_tokens 3072
```

Processes were pinned to Socket 0 (CPU 0-31,64-95; NUMA memory node 0) using Linux host OS `numactl --cpunodebind=0 --membind=0` / `taskset -c 0-31,64-95`. (Note: NUMA node and CPU affinity are host OS process launcher controls, not Maya engine variables). Libraries resolved through `/opt/rocm/lib` and `/opt/rocm-7.2.0/lib`. Path-specific values for model files, executable, slot directory, logs, and tokenizer are intentionally omitted. Those paths do not affect the documented settings and are machine-local.

Recorded SHA-256 identifiers (metadata hashes identify the existing packs; full expert shards were not rehashed):

```text

patch gfx906 executable: 19ded2f457293c23fd753217fe0afdc9abc4d137b329a90edcf3149ff1c87532
```

## Upstream benchmark protocol and results

Use Maya's standard benchmark while the server is stopped, then capture a report:

```sh
ln -s build-gfx906 build-hip  # fresh checkout only; do not replace an existing build
python maya.py --backend hip --config /path/to/mi50-config.json --bench
python maya.py --backend hip --config /path/to/mi50-config.json --report
```

The standard comparison uses greedy decoding, thinking off, three answers capped at 256 tokens, and a short warm-up; prefill is measured at 2,048 and 8,192 prompt tokens using the standard benchmark corpus (SHA-256: `66b02705575a889091f4accf20b8650568a015557c0d6ec9cfc1618ba445d3e0`). Full execution timing numbers are documented in the PR thread; all benchmark passes completed without engine errors or crashes.

The 204,800-token context is a configured capacity. The separate coding-task requests used about 8,191 input tokens; they did not test a 196K prompt.

## Coding-task queue

| Model | Captured output comparison | Quality result | Run status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Maya-M, IQ2_S | Byte-identical source output | Patch/control: 10/10 each | Completed |
| Maya-L, IQ3_S | Source output differs | Patch/control: 10/10 each | Completed |

The fixed task generated a self-contained Python HTTP body-limit helper; ten independent standard-library tests checked boundaries, malformed input and read behavior. The request bytes were identical between arms within each model. Exact-repeat requests and synthetic append-only tool-message continuations completed without protocol errors; no external tool was executed. The standard benchmark discards answer text, so it cannot establish token identity or output parity. Only the separate captured coding response is compared above; no benchmark answer-identity claim is made.

## DeepSeek-V4.1 scope note

Sections 2–6 report the end-to-end multi-GPU server benchmark for GLM-5.3 (Maya-M / Maya-L). DeepSeek-V4.1 Maya-S is validated at the kernel and quant level via `ds4_gguf_test` (reported below in Section 7).

## Kernel and Parity Suite Validation

### GLM-5.3-Flash Maya-L (IQ3_S)

Direct P2P PCIe transfers (`MAYA_GLM_P2P`) are included in this PR (`src/core/glm_window.cu`). The exhaustive host sign oracle passed all 128 packed 7-bit codes, all 256 raw 8-bit patterns and valid IQ grid bytes 1–62. Dedicated IQ3_S (type 21) and IQ2_XS (type 17) expert fixtures passed on each of four MI50s.

Recorded identifiers for comparison:

```text
candidate engine SHA-256: 30c17c5d4e7aeffddf07a374f0ac03ab4a65be1af8b9f68e5d17573a25e5451e
portable control engine SHA-256: 41dc37fc979de6b6dd464656086fae8543939440244d7b0ef1497a83a9f84772
candidate glm_fast.cu overlay SHA-256: fac440913f676644e3608f795e4965642f6e11c9e418b12e65a7504aa2e6b092
control glm_fast.cu overlay SHA-256: d7e83b947cefe897cd9b5c33db025f073aaea84e0ef17771d906616005b380ce
benchmark corpus manifest SHA-256: 70fc3af400dda4c270b1742153999480efe6c91ce8f8b724e0fd965557653be4
```

### DeepSeek-V4.1 Maya-S (`ds4_gguf_test`) & Multi-GPU Validation

1. **`ds4_gguf_test` (`gfx906` MI50 and `gfx1201` R9700)**: **100% PASS (`ALL OK (0 failed)`)** across all KV pack, sparse attention, WMMA, router, `mv`, `IQ1_S`, `IQ1_M`, `IQ2_XXS`, `IQ2_XS`, `IQ2_S`, `IQ3_XXS`, `IQ3_S`, `IQ4_XS`, `Q2_K`, `Q3_K`, `Q4_K`, `Q5_K`, `MXFP4`, and CPU-lane tests:
   - Upstream `ds4_iq.cu` sub-block scaling verifies mathematical parity.
   - Wrapping `sub_dot`, `gate_up_kernel`, `down_kernel`, and `lanes_sum` reductions in explicit `#pragma clang fp contract(off)` helpers with `asm volatile` register barriers (`fadd_no_fma`, `fsub_no_fma`, `fmul_no_fma`, `fma_no_contract`) preserves 100% bit-for-bit identity between token and window paths on both `gfx906` and `gfx1201`.
2. **GLM-5.3 HIP Microbenchmarks (`gfx906` MI50 & `gfx1201` R9700)**:
   - `hip_intrinsics`: **PASS** (100% parity on signed `sdot4`/overflow, 65,536 packed-byte pairs, wave32/64 shuffles, ballot, sleep).
   - `hip_expert_kernel_bench --parity-only`: **PASS** (`bit_identical=yes` across all 6 production quantization pairs including `gu=21/down=17` `IQ3_S/IQ2_XS` and `gu=22/down=22` with `hip_fast_signs` and `STRATA_GLM_GU22_TUNED=1`).
   - `glm_attn_bench`: **PASS** (100% bit-exact parity on all `dsa_score` and `mla_attend` shapes with hardware intra-lane butterfly reductions `ds_swizzle` + DPP `quad_perm` on `gfx906` and `v_permlanex16` + `row_xmask` on `gfx1201`).
   - `glm_mv_bench --parity-only`: **PASS** (`differ=0 max_abs=0.000e+00` across all `router_shexp`, `dense_gu`, `dense_down`, and `head` variants).

## Limits

- A mixed wave32/wave64 fat binary has not been validated.
- NVIDIA hardware validation remains with Maya's maintainers.

No production binary or configuration was promoted without passing all qualification gates; displaced services were restored with healthy endpoints and no retained benchmark lease.


# The models

Maya runs three models, each on an engine built for it. This page has each one's files, what they need and what is
special about it. Back to the [README](../README.md).

- [How the quants are named](#how-the-quants-are-named)
- [GLM-5.3-Flash](#glm-53-flash)
- [DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017](#deepseek-v41-flash-1017)
- [MiniMax-M3.1-Flash](#minimax-m31-flash)
- [Community quants of GLM-5.3-Flash](#community-quants-of-glm-53-flash)

## How the quants are named

Every Maya quant is named after its model first, then its tier - XS, S, M or L, by how many bits its experts keep - so
"GLM-5.3 · Maya-S" and "MiniMax M3.1 · Maya-S" never get mixed up. The setup asks which one to download; to pick one
yourself, give its setup id: `./setup.sh --model glm-maya-s24`. The names used before (`--model Maya-S24`,
`--ds4-quant maya-xs`, ...) keep working.

## GLM-5.3-Flash

**[GLM-5.3-Flash](https://huggingface.co/zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash)** (zai-org, MIT license; 321 B parameters, about 18 B
active per token, a context of up to 1 M tokens), on Maya's first engine (`maya`).

Project Maya's own quants of GLM-5.3-Flash, made from Z.ai's FP8 release with statistics from the FP8 model. Each keeps
the model's MTP block, which drafts tokens ahead.

| Model | Setup id | Size | Made for | Against the FP8 model |
| --- | --- | ---: | --- | --- |
| **GLM-5.3 · Maya-S** (default) | `glm-maya-s` | 96.5 GB | PCs with a smaller memory pool (RAM and VRAM together) | 97.9% of its zero-shot accuracy; the same next token 83% of the time |
| **GLM-5.3 · Maya-S24** | `glm-maya-s24` | 94.7 GB | cards of 24 GB or less: 4-bit attention leaves ~1.5 GB more of the card for experts, decode +14% | 97.7%; 83% |
| **GLM-5.3 · Maya-XS** | `glm-maya-xs` | 73.4 GB | PCs with 64 GB of RAM and a 24 GB card, at 128K context (int8 KV cache, thinking budget 8192) | - |
| **GLM-5.3 · Maya-M** | `glm-maya-m` | 116 GB | a bigger memory pool; more bits where they count, calibrated toward tool calls and front-end code | 97.9%; 86% |
| **GLM-5.3 · Maya-M-Derisked** | `glm-maya-m-derisked` | 116 GB | Maya-M with a weight edit by [Blackfrost_AI](https://x.com/Blackfrost_AI) that reduces blanket refusals ([its repo](https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/GLM-5.3-Flash-Maya-M-Derisked-IQ2_S-GGUF)); experimental | not measured yet |
| **GLM-5.3 · Maya-L** | `glm-maya-l` | 156.3 GB | the biggest memory pools | **99.2%**; 90% |

The setup recommends one for your PC. How they're made and measured: [MAYA-S](../bench/results/MAYA-S.md),
[MAYA-S24](../bench/results/MAYA-S24.md), [MAYA-M](../bench/results/MAYA-M.md), [MAYA-L](../bench/results/MAYA-L.md). On
[Hugging Face](https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/GLM-5.3-Flash-Maya-GGUF).

On AMD, GLM-5.3-Flash reads pictures too: setup builds its image encoder for the GPU.

### GLM-5.3-Flash's options

- **Several requests at once:** `./maya.sh --parallel 4` (Windows: `START-MAYA.bat --parallel 4`; up to 8) keeps it in
  the config, and the dashboard shows how many answers are being written. Each extra request keeps its own context in
  VRAM, so a few fewer experts fit there ([how](SETTINGS.md#several-requests-at-once)). They add up: on one GPU a
  token of each goes through the model together, so the dense weights are read once for all of them. In tokens per
  second for all of them together, against the same questions one after another: x1.41 for two and x1.35 for four on a
  V100 that streams most of Maya-S's experts from RAM, x1.67 for two and x2.02 for four on a Gorgon Halo (Radeon
  8065S, 160 GB for the GPU) that holds every expert in GPU memory. The more of the model VRAM holds, the more it
  gains.
- **Long contexts:** new setups keep each attention layer's cache in RAM with a window of it in VRAM
  (`--kv-streaming on`; `./maya.sh --setup` adds it to an existing config), so the VRAM holds more experts - at 512K
  on an RTX 3090, prompts 4-9x faster and decode +5%; 1M context fits one 3090 ([how](SETTINGS.md)).
- **Faster answers with a DFlash2 drafter:** `./maya.sh --setup --draft dflash2` (NVIDIA RTX 30 / A100 and newer,
  and one AMD Ryzen AI Max APU) downloads a small drafter that drafts up to 7 tokens a round and gives the same answers
  - Maya-L +44% on nine GPUs and +12% on two (against the MTP block), +19% on one RTX 3090, +9% on a Radeon 8060S
  with unsloth's UD-IQ2_XXS ([how](SETTINGS.md#drafting-with-dflash2)). How much
  it gains depends on the PC: on one GPU that reads much of the model from RAM it is often slower. So a
  [speed calibration](SETTINGS.md#the-speed-calibration) measures it on your PC and keeps it only where it is faster.
  The drafter's license is non-commercial (CC BY-NC-ND 4.0).

## DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017

The setup also offers **[DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017](https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017)**
(DeepSeek, MIT license; 748 B parameters) on an engine of its own (`maya-ds4`): `./setup.sh --setup` and pick it, or
`./setup.sh --model DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017`.

The server uses the pinned official prompt encoder, with numeric reasoning budgets and ID-matched parallel tool
results. See [the prompt format notes](deepseek-prompt-format.md) for API details and the standalone Jinja limits.

| Files | Size | Runs on |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| **[DeepSeek V4.1 · Maya-S](https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-Maya-GGUF)** (`deepseek-maya-s`), Project Maya's own quant (default): the experts in 2-3 bits, 92.6% the same next token as the original | 340 GB, with the Engram tables (Q6_K) | NVIDIA from Volta (V100) on, and AMD; Linux and Windows |
| **[DeepSeek V4.1 · Maya-XS](https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-Maya-GGUF)** (`deepseek-maya-xs`): the experts in ~1.6 bits, with its own 2-bit drafter - the whole model in a 128 GB PC's memory (the Engram tables stay on the SSD) | 277 GB, with the Engram tables (Q6_K) | the same |
| unsloth's [UD-IQ1_M GGUF](https://huggingface.co/unsloth/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-GGUF) (`--ds4-quant gguf-iq1_m`): for PCs with about 150 GB of VRAM + RAM | 301 GB, with Maya's n-gram tables | the same |
| unsloth's [UD-IQ3_S GGUF](https://huggingface.co/unsloth/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-GGUF) (`--ds4-quant gguf-iq3_s`): close to Q8's quality, for PCs with about 270 GB of VRAM + RAM; shares its n-gram tables with Maya's quants | 424 GB, with the n-gram tables | the same |
| unsloth's [UD-Q8_K_XL GGUF](https://huggingface.co/unsloth/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-GGUF) (`--ds4-quant gguf-q8`; DeepSeek's own formats): the closest to the original, for PCs with about 310 GB of VRAM + RAM | 467 GB, with Maya's n-gram tables | the same |
| LibertAIDAI's [EXL3 2.9 bpw](https://huggingface.co/LibertAIDAI/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-EXL3-2.9bpw) + DeepSeek's Engram tables | 414 GB | NVIDIA from Volta (V100) on; pictures from RTX 30 / A100 on; and AMD |
| LibertAIDAI's [EXL3 3.5 bpw](https://huggingface.co/LibertAIDAI/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-EXL3-3.5bpw) + the same Engram tables (`--ds4-quant exl3-3.5`: closer to the original, ~280 GB of VRAM + RAM) | 461 GB | the same |
| orcarouter's [Abliterated GGUFs](https://huggingface.co/orcarouter/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-abliterated-GGUF), the model with its refusals removed: Mix-128G (`--ds4-quant abliterated-128g`, ~114 GB of VRAM + RAM) or Mix-256G (`abliterated-256g`, ~236 GB); `abliterated` picks the one your PC holds. Their n-gram table is left out and Maya's Q6_K tables are read instead (once for both). A gated repo: accept its terms, then `HF_TOKEN` or `hf auth login` | 275 / 397 GB, with the 161 GB of n-gram tables shared with Maya's DeepSeek quants (downloaded once) | NVIDIA from Volta (V100) on (AMD not tried yet) |

- **Any amount of memory:** the most-used experts go to the GPUs, the others are read in place from the model's files -
