  from RAM where they fit, from the SSD otherwise. More VRAM and RAM is faster; keep the files on an NVMe SSD. The
  setup recommends a 256K context where your VRAM and RAM hold the model, 64K otherwise.
- **The DSpark drafter:** with the GGUFs the setup also fetches the model's own drafter (8 GB from Maya's repo:
  DeepSeek's FP4 experts, its dense weights in 8-bit) and measures whether drafting makes your PC faster; it keeps it
  only where it does.
- **Pictures:** with the GGUFs the setup fetches DeepSeek's vision tower (1 GB of its checkpoint). It waits in RAM
  and takes no VRAM between pictures: while a picture is read it borrows about 320 MB of the first GPU's memory, then
  gives it back to the model. `--no-vision` leaves it out.
- **One GPU or several:** the layers are split across the GPUs (pairs of equal GPUs also split each layer where the
  files' formats allow), its thinking levels (Off, Low, High, Max) in the chat, and its own numbers in the monitor.
- **Strix Halo / Gorgon Halo on Windows:** Windows counts the GPU's share of the memory against its page file, so the
  setup asks for a page file big enough to keep every expert on the GPU and says the size
  ([AMD_MAYA.md](AMD_MAYA.md)).
- **Other EXL3 quants from Hugging Face:** `tools/ds4/pack.py` packs them too. A head kept as BF16 (`head.weight`)
  is read as it is. Engram tables a quant stored in its own INT4 layout (`U8` + `embed.scale`, as in the quants derived
  from Diffbot's mixed 3 bpw) are not read: the tables are DeepSeek's own and the quant leaves them unchanged, so put
  DeepSeek's FP8 shards 47 / 48 of deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 in their place. Shards that write their
  tensors grouped by type instead of expert by expert stop the pack with that reason: rewrite them in expert order.

### DeepSeek's options

All of these are optional; the details and every setting are in [SETTINGS.md](SETTINGS.md).

- **Several requests at once:** `./maya.sh --parallel 4` (Windows: `START-MAYA.bat --parallel 4`; up to 8), as for
  GLM-5.3-Flash: each conversation keeps its own state, and a token of each goes through the model together. In tokens
  per second for all of them together: x1.57 for two and x1.26 for four on a V100 with most of Maya-XS's experts in
  RAM, against the same questions one after another with the DSpark drafter
  ([how](SETTINGS.md#deepseek-v41-flash-several-requests-at-once)).
- **Conversations kept:** the DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 engine holds one conversation: a prompt that continues it
  reuses its state, anything else starts over. With `MAYA_DS4_SLOT_KEEP=1`, a conversation another request takes the
  model from - a chat, or an agent's parent and workers - is set aside, and a request that continues it reads only its
  new tokens: in RAM, or across a restart from the folder. Off by default. How many are kept in RAM, the folder, its
  size cap and how long a slot lives are settings too
  ([conversations kept](SETTINGS.md#deepseek-conversations-kept-an-agents-parent-and-workers-on-one-model)).
- **Loading the experts:** the engine reads the pack's expert regions into VRAM and RAM at start. Once a region's
  bytes are copied, the load drops its pages from the file cache (`posix_fadvise`), so the same bytes are not held
  twice and a pack whose copies would not fit beside their cache loads at the disk's speed
  (`MAYA_DS4_LOAD_DROP_CACHE`, on; `0` keeps them: a restart then starts warm, and a pack that fits loads a few
  seconds faster without the calls). `MAYA_DS4_LOAD_THREADS` (12) and `MAYA_DS4_LOAD_INTERLEAVE` (off) set the load's
  threads and file order; [loading the experts](SETTINGS.md#deepseek-loading-the-experts) has the numbers for a GGUF and an EXL3
  pack, and the huge-page note for a load that stalls part-way.
- **Engram prompt read-ahead:** on one GPU, an optional read-ahead fills the ordinary row cache before each consuming
  layer. It groups sorted rows into larger zone reads, removes duplicate reads, and sizes look-ahead from the cache
  budget instead of letting a large prompt overwrite rows before their use. The model's row bytes and decode path are
  unchanged; it is off by default ([settings and caveats](SETTINGS.md#deepseek-engram-prompt-read-ahead)).
- **Large-RAM Linux hosts:** optional
  [locked Engram tables and strict CPU-expert RAM placement](SETTINGS.md#deepseek-explicit-ram-residency). Both are
  off by default; they need enough physical RAM.
- **Expert-copy startup on Linux (GGUF):** optional [direct reads](SETTINGS.md#deepseek-expert-copy-io) for the
  experts copied into RAM (off by default).
- **Prompt experts on AMD:** `MAYA_DS4_EXPERT_MMQ=1` uses the existing int8 MMQ path for routed IQ experts. It is off
  by default and adds q8 activation rounding ([routed prompt experts on AMD](SETTINGS.md#deepseek-routed-prompt-experts-on-amd)).

## MiniMax-M3.1-Flash

The setup also offers **[MiniMax-M3.1-Flash](https://huggingface.co/MiniMaxAI/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash)** (MiniMax; 428 B
parameters, 23 B active per token, up to 1 M tokens; the [MiniMax Community License](../LICENSE.minimax-m31): free for
non-commercial use, commercial use has conditions) on an engine of its own (`maya-mm3`, contributed by
[@needmorevram](https://github.com/needmorevram), #477): `./setup.sh --model MiniMax-M3.1-Flash`.

| Files | Size | Runs on |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| **[MiniMax M3.1 · Maya-XS](https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-GGUF)** (`minimax-maya-xs`), Project Maya's own quant: the experts in ~1.6 bits - the whole model in a 128 GB PC's memory | 93 GB | NVIDIA V100 / RTX 20 and newer, and AMD; Linux and Windows; macOS on Apple Silicon (the [Metal engine](METAL.md#minimax-m31-flash), experimental) |
| **[MiniMax M3.1 · Maya-S](https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-GGUF)** (`minimax-maya-s`): the experts in ~2.1 bits, closer to the original; 87.6% the same next token as the original (the average over its categories) | 122 GB | the same |
| Mia-AiLab's [EXL3 3.5 bpw](https://huggingface.co/Mia-AiLab/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-EXL3-EAQ-v1-3.5-bpw), with MiniMax's DSpark drafter and vision tower | 197 GB | the same |

With Maya's quants the setup fetches the vision tower and the DSpark drafter from the EXL3 repo (about 4 GB) for
pictures and drafting, on NVIDIA and AMD alike.

- **Set up for your PC:** the experts that do not fit in VRAM are computed on the CPU from RAM; the context (up to the
  model's 1M) is recommended by your memory; tensor parallel where the GPUs form groups; and the DSpark drafter only
  where it measures faster on your PC.
- **MiniMax's own settings:** the model card's sampling (temperature 1.0, top-p 0.95) and its thinking levels - Auto
  (the default: it decides whether to think), the five efforts Low to Max, and Off - with MiniMax's own prompt format.
- **Pictures:** on from the start, the model's vision tower running in the engine; `--no-vision` leaves it out.
- **Several requests at once:** `./maya.sh --parallel 4` (up to 8) keeps it in the config. Each conversation has its
  own KV cache in VRAM, so KV streaming and the DSpark drafter are off while it is above 1, and the setup recommends a
  context that leaves the caches room ([how](SETTINGS.md#several-requests-at-once)).
- **Tool calls, the OpenAI- and Anthropic-compatible API and the dashboard** work as for the other models, with its
  own numbers in the monitor.

Its engine settings: [SETTINGS.md](SETTINGS.md#minimax-m31-flash).

## Community quants of GLM-5.3-Flash

These also run on Maya:

| Model | Size | How | Notes |
| --- | ---: | --- | --- |
| [EXL3 3.05 bpw](https://huggingface.co/turboderp/GLM-5.3-Flash-exl3/tree/3.05bpw) by turboderp | 125.3 GB | in the setup: `--model EXL3-3.05bpw` | NVIDIA from V100, and AMD |
| [EXL3 4.05 bpw](https://huggingface.co/turboderp/GLM-5.3-Flash-exl3/tree/4.05bpw) by turboderp | 165.2 GB | in the setup: `--model EXL3-4.05bpw` | NVIDIA from V100, and AMD |


# MiniMax-M3.1-Flash on Apple Silicon (Metal) - port notes

Experimental. Back to [docs/METAL.md](METAL.md#minimax-m31-flash).

Started 2026-11-01 on Duckets Mac Studio (M5 Max, 128 GB unified) - the Maya team has no Mac;
this machine is the test hardware. Fork: Franzferdinan51/project-maya @ 465a981 (v1.1.48).

## Verified on real Mac hardware
- ./maya.sh --backend metal --check PASSES: M5 Max, ~109 GB GPU pool, clang 21, CMake 4.4, Python 3.15.
  (maya.sh needs Homebrew python first in PATH; system python3 is 3.9 and fails the check.)

## Why MiniMax does not run on Mac today
- src/metal is GLM-only: hand-written ggml graphs (glm_metal*.cpp) + expert pool + sampler + serve.
- MiniMax engine (src/mm3) is CUDA/EXL3-kernel only. The pinned llama.cpp fork has NO minimax arch.
- BUT Mayas own MiniMax quants are GGUF (peasantsmith/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-GGUF):
  dense in Q4_K/Q5_K/Q6_K/Q8_0, experts in IQ1_S/IQ2_XXS/IQ2_XS/IQ2_S/IQ3_XXS - all standard ggml
  types with ggml-metal kernels. No custom quant kernels needed. Activation is swigluoai.

## M3.1 architecture contract (src/mm3/mm3_geo.hpp - the kernels coverage checks)
- Every layer sparse attention: indexer (ix_heads = n_kv = 4, ix_dim 128) scores 128-token blocks,
  keeps topk (<=64) blocks + 1 local block; sparse_attention_freq all 1s in M3.1.
- Attention: n_head = 16 * n_kv, head_dim 128, partial rotary (n_rot <= hd), rope_theta, rms eps.
- KV cache (CUDA engine): BF16 [ctx][4][128] K and V + BF16 [ctx][128] index-key cache.
- MoE: <=256 experts, <=8/token, routed_scaling_factor; moe_layer_freq per layer; dense FFN
  (ff_dense) on non-MoE layers; shared experts (ff_shared).
- swigluoai: g=min(g,limit); u=clamp(u,-limit,limit); out = g/(1+exp(-1.702*g)) * (u+1). alpha=1.702.
- Oracles: src/mm3/mm3_cpu.cpp (CPU lane semantics), mm3_model.cu (full forward), ds4 IQ CPU rows.

## Plan
1. Build existing maya-metal (GLM) on this Mac - toolchain proof. [running]
2. mm3 metal engine beside glm_metal: GGUF loader (adapt glm_metal_load), graph builder for the
   sparse-indexer attention + MoE above, expert pool + sampler + serve reused.
3. Synthetic parity test in the style of metal_model_test (small fixture vs CPU reference).
4. Route MiniMax in maya.sh/setup/serve for --backend metal; update docs/METAL.md + docs/MODELS.md.
5. Real-model run + ./maya.sh --bench, file the results upstream (issue) and push to the fork.

## Blocker for step 5
- MiniMax Maya-XS GGUF = 93 GB. Sabrent: 52 GB free. Internal: 15 GB free. Needs ~41+ GB freed
  on Sabrent (or another disk) before the model can land.

## Milestone 2026-11-01: GLM engine verified on real Mac hardware
- cmake -DMAYA_ENABLE_METAL=ON build: BUILD-EXIT-0 (maya-metal + metal_model_test).
- ctest: 27/27 passed, 82 s total, CTEST-EXIT-0 (M5 Max). First full real-Mac pass of the suite.

## Progress — 2026-11-01 (Juno, on Duckets' Mac Studio)

**Milestone 1 DONE: the GLM Metal engine builds and passes its full parity suite on real Mac
hardware** — the first full real-Mac run the project has had (upstream has no Mac; CI only).
`ctest` in build-metal: 27/27 at that point.

**Milestone 2 DONE: the MiniMax-M3.1 Metal engine exists and passes parity.**
New files: `src/metal/mm3_metal.hpp`, `mm3_metal_impl.hpp`, `mm3_metal_load.cpp`, `mm3_metal.cpp`,
`mm3_main.cpp` (the `maya-metal-mm3` CLI), `src/metal/metal_mm3_test.cpp`,
`tools/mm3_metal_fixture.py`. CMake: mm3 sources join the `maya_metal` library; tests
`metal_mm3_gen` / `metal_mm3_test` / `metal_mm3_test_cpu` registered. Full suite now **30/30**.

Design as built (follows `tools/mm3/ref.py` op for op):
- Loader: minimax-m3 GGUF shards + the `-maya-indexers.bin` side file (dense layers' indexers),
  geometry from the `minimax-m3.*` keys exactly as `tools/mm3/pack_gguf.py`'s geometry() maps them.
  Norm weights are used as stored (llama.cpp's converter already folded the Gemma +1 in).
- Every tensor resident, routed experts included — the Maya quants are made to fit a 128 GB Mac
  whole, so there is no expert pool and **no graph cut at the router**: the router (sigmoid +
  correction bias + `ggml_argsort_top_k` + renormalised weights) runs in-graph and `mul_mat_id`
  consumes its top-k directly, over the stacked `ffn_*_exps.weight` tensors.
- Two graphs per layer: graph 1 computes q/k/v + indexer iq/ik (Gemma norms, NeoX partial RoPE),
  writes the BF16 KV and index-key caches, and reads the indexer scores `iq·ik` back to the host;
  the host picks kept blocks exactly as `ref.py`'s `msa_select` (block max, local blocks forced,
  top-k); graph 2 runs the masked sparse attention per KV group + the MLP (dense or MoE, all
  swigluoai) and writes the residual carry. The last layer's graph 2 also runs the head.
- Parity fixture: 3 layers (1 dense lead + 2 MoE), real head_dim/rotary/indexer widths, 2,176
  tokens = 17 index blocks so selection must actually drop a block; layer 0's indexers ride in
  the side file. Oracle = a numpy port of ref.py in the generator (k/v/iq/ik rounded to BF16 as
  the engines' caches do). Results (M5 Max): prompt logits rel L2 **4.1e-05 (CPU) / 4.2e-05
  (Metal)** vs the oracle, sliced == one-shot, greedy 8/8 tokens identical. Gates set at 1e-3.

Known v1 limits (deliberate, in order):
- Block selection is host-side from GPU scores: the scores readback is Tk×heads×T floats per
  layer. Correct and simple; an in-graph selection (ggml_top_k over block maxima) is the known
  next step when profiled on the real model.
- No `--serve` wiring yet: `maya-metal-mm3` is CLI-only (`--ids`); the serve protocol port is
  the next chunk, with the maya.sh/setup/docs wiring.
- The fixture is all-F32 (oracle exactness). The IQ1/IQ2 expert kernels the real quants use are
  ggml's standard mul_mat_id path (the same one every llama.cpp MoE GGUF takes), but a quantized
  fixture or the real model has not run yet — the real Maya-XS set is 93 GB and this Mac's
  Sabrent has ~52 GB free, so the real-model run waits on disk space (owner's call).

## Progress — 2026-11-01, later (milestone 3: serve + docs)

**The serve protocol is ported.** `src/metal/mm3_serve.cpp` implements serve.cpp's protocol for
Mm3Metal (GEN/STOP/PAUSE/WRAP/QUIT, PP/T/STAT/DONE lines, the shared sampler.cpp chain — same keys,
same Philox draws, seeded requests repeat). Deltas, all the model's rather than the protocol's: no
drafting (spec keys accepted; plain decoding writes the same tokens), GENI refused (vision tower not
wired), stop tokens from the GGUF's `tokenizer.ggml.eos_token_id`, and PAUSE/snapshots are nearly
free — Mm3Metal's caches are positional, so `snapshot_save/restore` are position save/rewind (added
to the engine for this). `tools/test_mm3_serve.py` (`metal_mm3_serve_test`) passes: a paused
request's follow-up reuses the kept prefix and writes the uninterrupted request's tokens, seeded
sampling repeats exactly, spec=1 changes nothing. Full suite: **31/31 passed** on this Mac (serial
ctest, 331 s — the disk-streaming GLM tests were slow on the Sabrent that day; no failures).

**Docs**: docs/METAL.md gained the MiniMax section (design, the resident-experts memory plan, why
Maya-XS is the Mac quant — Maya-S's 122 GB is past a 128 GB Mac's ~115 GB GPU working set), the
intro no longer calls MiniMax NVIDIA/AMD-only, the accuracy table has the mm3 row, and
docs/MODELS.md's Maya-XS row lists macOS/Apple Silicon.

**Still not done (deliberately)**: maya.py's setup/model-select integration — `./maya.sh --backend
metal --setup` does not offer MiniMax yet, and serve/server.py is not pointed at maya-metal-mm3 by
the setup. The engine speaks the server's protocol, so the remaining work is launcher plumbing in
maya.py (model routing, the GGUF download + pack step, recommended context), best done against
maya.py's own test suite (tools/test_maya.py, tools/test_maya_metal.py) rather than by feel.

## Progress — 2026-11-01, evening (milestone 4: the real model runs)

**Maya-XS runs on the Mac.** The full 93 GB set (peasantsmith/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-GGUF, Maya-XS:
two IQ1_S shards, 47.8 + 45.1 GB, plus the 12.5 MB indexers side file) was downloaded to
models/Maya-XS after freeing space on the Sabrent (121 GB free before the download, ~34 GB after).
All runs below are maya-metal-mm3 on the M5 Max with 8,192-token context state (1.16 GB) and
92.99 GB of weights resident.

- Load: 103–117 s (disk-bound).
- Chat prompt (the model's own template, 191 tokens): prefill 153 tok/s; the model answered
  "The capital of France is Paris." and emitted EOS. Decode 16.2 tok/s.
- Document prompt (3,227 tokens = 26 index blocks, so top-16 selection genuinely prunes): prefill
  242 tok/s; asked for the Maya-XS size "according to the document", the model reasoned over the
  document and quoted its line back ("Maya-XS (93 GB) fits into a 128 GB Mac with …") before the
  48-token budget cut its think block. Decode at ~3.2 k context: 6.7 tok/s — the sparse attention
  over ≤2,048 kept keys plus the per-layer host selection is the v1 cost, as designed.
- A raw completion prompt with no BOS and no chat template derailed into a repetition loop on this
  1.6-bit instruct quant — a prompt-format artifact, recorded so nobody re-diagnoses it: use the
  model's template (its tokenizer BOS is 200019, EOS 200020).

**One real bug found and fixed by the real set:** the loader derived the indexers side file's name
from the shard stem, but the real sets name it after the set, not the quant-tagged shard
(…-Maya-XS-maya-indexers.bin beside …-Maya-XS-IQ1_S-00001-of-00002.gguf), so the dense layers'
indexers were never found and the first real load failed on blk.0. The loader now discovers the
side file by scanning the shards' directory for the longest -maya-indexers.bin/.gguf name that
prefixes a shard name. Fixture parity unchanged (rel L2 4.24e-05); the four mm3 tests pass 4/4.

**Still not done (deliberately)**: maya.py launcher integration, as in milestone 3. Speed work
(fusing the per-layer host selection, batching decode graphs) is future work; v1 is
correctness-first, as the GLM engine was.


# Project Maya quants of GLM-5.3-Flash - the plan

Two quants of our own, made from the official weights with error-feedback rounding and scale refinement sized to what we can run:

| | Maya-S (first) | Maya-L (later) |
|---|---|---|
| for | PCs with a smaller memory pool across RAM and VRAM (it runs from 32 GB of RAM, the rest streamed from the SSD) | quality first: larger memory pools |
| size | ~80-85 GB (experts ~74-78 GB) | ~135-145 GB (~3.5 bits a weight) |
| quality target | as close to the FP8 model as ~2 bits allow; no loops | KL <= 0.05 against the FP8 model |
| compare against | the FP8 model (Z.ai's release) | the FP8 model (Z.ai's release) |

## What we start from

- **Source**: `zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash`: 62 safetensors shards, **328.4 GB**, block-wise FP8 (e4m3 weights, an fp32
  scale per 128x128 block). This is Z.ai's own deployment precision, so nothing is lost against BF16. The BF16 repo
  (640 GB) isn't needed.
- **Model**: 45 layers. The first 3 are dense (FFN 12288); the other 42 are MoE with 288 routed experts each (top-8,
  4096 x 2048, gate/up/down) plus one shared expert. Attention is 34 KDA (linear) layers and 11 DSA/MLA layers.
  There are mHC 4-stream residuals and one NextN (MTP) layer. Routed experts are 304.4B of the 321B parameters; one
  expert is 25.2M parameters.
- **The engine's development file** (before Maya's own quants): a 1.6-bit GGUF with experts of ~84 GB.

## Machines

- **EVO-X5 (arrives 2026-10-07)**: Ryzen AI Max+ 495, 16 cores, RDNA 3.5 40-CU GPU, 192 GB unified memory (up to
  160 GB for the GPU), 2-4 TB Gen4 NVMe. It does the heavy passes: one MoE layer in BF16 is 14.5 GB, so a layer, its
  Hessians and the calibration activations all fit at once. BF16 is native here (the V100s don't have it). Needs
  PyTorch for ROCm (gfx1151-class); check that first.
- **Mercury / Uranus**: the engine side.
  - Score every candidate quant in our engine (`MAYA_GLM_SCORE`: NLL, perplexity, top-1, and KL against a
    reference dump).
  - Add kernels for the new types.
  - Measure speed.

## Pipeline

### 0. Fetch (needs the owner's OK for the download)

- The 62 shards plus config and tokenizer, onto the EVO-X5's NVMe, resumable.
- Each file is checked against Hugging Face's LFS sha256.
- Time depends on the internet line: ~45 min at 1 Gbit/s, ~7.5 h at 100 Mbit/s.

### 1. Calibration and evaluation text

About 1M calibration tokens plus 100k held-out evaluation tokens, all in GLM's chat template, with thinking traces
where the model would think:

- **Chat**:
  - multi-turn conversations
  - instructions and writing
  - English, Portuguese (BR), Spanish, Chinese and a few others
- **Code**:
  - Python, JS/TS, C/C++, Rust, Go, SQL, shell
  - code reasoning traces
- **Math and science reasoning** (long `<think>` traces)
- **Tool calls / JSON / agent turns** (GLM's `<|observation|>` turns)
- **Long documents**: several 16k-32k-token samples, because the KDA state and the DSA selection behave differently there
- **The owner's own usage**, if they want it included: the dashboard conversations, or just the routing profile
  (`expert_usage.txt`)

Public datasets will be listed with exact files and sizes before anything is downloaded. Self-generated data
(answers from our current engine to ~500 written prompts) fills gaps in the chat format.

### 2. Reference and statistics pass

PyTorch on the EVO-X5, layer by layer, FP8 dequantized to BF16. It reads each layer once and keeps every token's
hidden state between layers (~1M x 4 streams x 4096 x 2 B = 32 GB). The reference forward uses `transformers`'
own `glm5_next` code (5.16+), checked against our engine on a few prompts first.

Per layer it records:

- **Activation statistics per expert**:
  - the diagonal, which is the imatrix
  - the full X^T X Hessians for gate/up (4096^2) and down (2048^2) inputs, accumulated in fp32 and kept on NVMe
    (~1 TB for all experts, so each layer's are used and dropped before the next)
- **Routing statistics per expert**:
  - frequency
  - REAP saliency (router weight x ||expert output||, averaged over the tokens routed to it)
  - the same per domain
- **The reference**: for the evaluation tokens, the final logits as the top-64 log-probs plus logsumexp at every
  position. This is the KL yardstick, in the format `MAYA_GLM_SCORE_REF` reads (extended to top-k).

Estimate: ~1 h compute (18B active parameters x 1M tokens; Hessian accumulation) plus reading 328 GB once.

### 3. Candidates

Every tensor is quantized at each candidate GGUF type with **error-feedback rounding**:

- **Method**: GPTQ over the full Hessian, block by block. Inside each block, llama.cpp's own quantizer chooses
  scales and grid points, weighted by the inverse-Hessian diagonal. The block's error then propagates to the
  remaining columns. This works for scalar K-quants and for the IQ lattice types, since a block is a block.
- **Candidates**:
  - routed experts: IQ1_M, IQ2_XXS, IQ2_XS, IQ2_S, IQ3_XXS, IQ3_S, IQ4_XS
  - everything else: Q4_K, Q5_K, Q6_K, Q8_0
- **Recorded**: the Hessian-weighted output error per (tensor, type) and per expert group.
- **Cost control**: the error table needs a sample of experts per layer (~32 of 288). Only the final assembly
  quantizes everything.
- **Gumbel-softmax refinement**: the paper's Gumbel-softmax training of the grid choices and scales, published for
  scalar K-quants. It's a later add-on for any K-quant tensors the allocation picks, kept only if it measurably
  lowers KL.

### 4. Allocation (RCO-style)

- **Goal**: choose a type per tensor under the byte budget, minimizing predicted end-to-end damage (each tensor's
  error times its layer's sensitivity, with sensitivity from small perturbation runs). Then score the top
  2-3 allocations in our engine against the FP8 reference and keep the best. RCO proper uses gradients of the true
  KL; this approximates it with measured errors plus measured KL checks.
- **Maya-specific: per-expert bits.** Our engine loads its own pack, so experts don't all need the same type.
  - Hot experts (high routing frequency and saliency) get more bits; the long tail gets fewer.
  - The GGUF version keeps one type per tensor so it still loads in llama.cpp.
  - Usage on real text is very skewed (that is why Mercury's VRAM tier hits 97%), so this should buy more quality
    per GB than anything else.
- **Fixed floors against loops and drift**. These parts are only ~6 GB:
  - attention, MLA, KDA and shared experts at Q6_K-Q8_0
  - the 3 dense layers at Q8_0
  - output head Q6_K or better, embeddings Q6_K
  - down never below gate/up for the same expert

### 5. Pruning study (Maya-S only)

Pruning frees bytes, but it is not free:

- OpenMOSE's REAP prune of this exact model (60 of 288 experts per layer removed) measured KL 0.45 against the
  original, and perplexity +2.8%.
- The public 3.5 -> 3.0-bit step costs about 0.07 KL.

So: budget-matched runs at 0%, ~10% and ~20% pruning, each with the bits the freed bytes buy, judged by KL, task
checks and the loop test. My expectation is that light or no pruning wins and per-expert bits do the work.

### 6. Assembly

- **GGUF**: standard types, one type per tensor, loads in llama.cpp. Metadata comes from today's GGUF.
- **Maya pack**: what our engine loads, including per-expert types when used.
- **Vision**: the mmproj stays BF16 (1.2 GB) for the day the engine gets vision.

### 7. Evaluation (each candidate, the same text)

- **KL against the FP8 reference**, plus perplexity and top-1 agreement:
  - on the held-out mix
  - separately for chat, code, math and Portuguese
  - long context (16-32k)
- **Loop test**: 50 long generations at the default sampling (T=1.0, top-p 0.95) and 20 greedy ones. Measures
  repeated n-grams, runs that never end, and broken thinking-block structure.
- **Task accuracy against the FP8 model** (zero-shot ARC-Easy/Challenge, HellaSwag, WinoGrande, PIQA the way
  lm-evaluation-harness scores them: tools/maya_quant/zs_*.py), and spot checks:
  - GSM8K (100)
  - HumanEval subset
  - IFEval subset
  - MMLU-Pro (300)
  - a Portuguese chat set
- **Speed** on Uranus (64 + 32) and Mercury.

## Engine work (Mercury/Uranus)

- **Done 2026-10-06: the decode kernels** for IQ2_XS 17, IQ2_S 22, IQ3_S 21 and IQ1_M 29, beside IQ2_XXS 16,
  IQ3_XXS 18, IQ1_S 19, IQ4_XS 23, Q2_K 10 and Q3_K 11.
  - Checked on synthetic experts against a double-precision reference: every type lands at the same ~1.5% (expert
    kernels) and ~0.9% (dense GEMV), which is the q8_1 activation's share.
  - The prompt path's MMQ now also builds Q2_K-Q6_K.
  - The CPU lane goes through ggml, which covers every type.
- **IQ1_M has no MMQ in llama.cpp**, so a layer of it couldn't go through the batched prompt path. It stays out of
  the candidates unless that path gets a dequantize-and-GEMM fallback.
- Light kernels (the prompt path's sparsely routed experts) still cover only 16/18/19. Others go through MMQ, which is
  correct but slower for a handful of rows.
- Per-expert types: the route groups its 8 experts by type and launches per group.
- The KL reference as top-k log-probs, so a reference from the EVO-X5 can be scored on the V100s.

## Order of work

1. EVO-X5 setup: ROCm PyTorch; check the reference forward against our engine.
2. Download the source (with the OK).
3. Calibration text (exact dataset list first).
4. Pass 2 (statistics + reference).
5. Error table.
6. Allocation and pruning study.
7. Maya-S assembly + evaluation.
8. Maya-L.

The engine kernels go in parallel on the V100s.

## The owner's requirements for the headline quant (2026-10-06)

- **As close to FP8 as possible at the smallest size, no loops, no broken output.** Post-training is in scope:
  - Error-correcting rounding first.
  - Then, where the measurements still show damage, block/expert-wise output-matching refinement of the quantised
    scales and grid choices, and a short distillation of the small floating-point parts against the
    FP8 teacher's outputs.
  - Every candidate passes the loop test before it is called a release.
- **Made on Uranus** (the EVO-X5 slipped a day):
  - The FP8 source is on its PCIe 4 NVMe, and the 64 GB RAM holds the calibration activations.
  - The V100 does the statistics pass; its CPUs, plus Mercury's, quantise.
  - Mercury tests the two-GPU case.
- **Vision:** the release carries the vision tower.
  - The FP8 checkpoint's 347 vision tensors are converted into an mmproj GGUF (BF16; Q8_0 measured as an option).
  - The engine's GLM vision path is a release item.
- **Calibration and evaluation weighted toward what the community benchmarks:** single-file HTML/CSS/JS pages,
  three.js scenes and canvas/WebGL animations (plus shaders), beside chat, code, maths, tool calls, Portuguese and long
  documents.
  - Their experts get measured and bits accordingly.
  - The evaluation adds three.js / HTML generation prompts: does the page parse and run (headless browser), no loops.
- **QA: a context-fill test.**
  - Fill the context to its configured maximum (32k, then 64k / 128k) with real text and keep generating.
  - Check that the answer stays coherent, nothing crashes, and memory stays inside its budget.
  - Later on the roadmap: speeds at several context sizes, published in the repo.

## Later (the owner sets the start)

- **EXL3 quants** (ExLlamaV3's trellis/QTIP-style format): load and run them in the engine. Added to the roadmap
  2026-10-06. Not started; the owner decides when.


# Responses API

Maya accepts `POST /v1/responses`, with JSON or SSE output, using the same model,
authentication, CORS policy, context limit, and generation engine as Chat Completions.
This includes GLM models served by `maya` and DeepSeek-V4.1 models served by
`maya-ds4`, with their configured chat templates, tool syntax and vision paths.

```sh
curl http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/responses \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"input":"Hello","store":false,"stream":true,"max_output_tokens":256}'
```

With the OpenAI Python SDK:

```python
from openai import OpenAI

client = OpenAI(base_url="http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1", api_key="your-key-or-any-text")
response = client.responses.create(
    model="your-model", input="Hello", store=False,
)
print(response.output_text)
```

## Supported behavior

- String `input`, or a full history containing user, assistant, system and developer
  messages. `instructions` supplies system instructions for this request.
- `input_text`, `output_text` in history, and `input_image` with an `image_url`
  (including data URLs). Images use the configured vision encoder; only the `auto`
  detail mode is supported. Function results may contain text and image parts.
- Function tools with `name`, `description`, `parameters`, and optional `strict`.
  `tool_choice` accepts `auto`, `none`, `required`, or `{"type":"function","name":"..."}`.
  Calls to undeclared or disallowed functions fail the response. Required/named
  choices must produce a complete call. `parallel_tool_calls:false` stops generation
  at the first complete call, with the tokens actually consumed reported in usage.
- Truncated function calls have `status:"incomplete"` and do not emit a completed
  function-arguments event. An end-of-turn inside an unfinished call fails the
  response. `strict:true` enables schema validation before any generated content or
  executable arguments are published; omitted/false strict does not enforce schemas.
- `function_call` output and `function_call_output` history, matched by `call_id`.
  A group of function results can arrive in a different order from its calls.
  All calls need their results in the input before generation can continue.
- `reasoning.effort`, `temperature`, `top_p`, `max_output_tokens`, and `metadata`.
  Reasoning is returned as a `reasoning` item with `reasoning_text` content, and
  can be replayed in history. It is plain model reasoning, not a generated summary.
- SSE response/item/content lifecycles, text and function-argument deltas, and final
  usage. Hitting the output limit yields `response.incomplete` with
  `incomplete_details.reason="max_output_tokens"`; an engine failure yields
  `response.failed`. Disconnecting stops foreground generation; background generation
  continues independently of a reader.
- `prompt_cache_key` is a validated optional cache hint (up to 256 characters),
  echoed in the response. Native prompt-prefix reuse determines actual cache hits;
  the hint does not partition or force the engine cache. String-valued
  `client_metadata` is accepted as diagnostic context and is not stored or echoed.
- The `include:["reasoning.encrypted_content"]` projection is accepted only with
  explicit `reasoning.effort:"none"`, when there are no reasoning items to encrypt.
  Encryption and encrypted reasoning input remain unsupported.
- `include:["message.output_text.logprobs"]` and `top_logprobs` (0-20; either one
  asks) return token log-probabilities in the message's `output_text` part and its
  `response.output_text.delta` / `.done` events, on the DeepSeek engine only (another
  engine refuses the request with a 400). See docs/SETTINGS.md, "Token
  log-probabilities".

## Conversation State

Responses are **not stored by default**, including when `store` is omitted. With
`store:false`, send the full history. For example, append `response.output` and the
matching tool results to the original input before requesting the next turn:

```python
history = [{"role": "user", "content": "Look up alpha"}]
response = client.responses.create(model="your-model", input=history, tools=tools, store=False)
history += [item.to_dict() for item in response.output]
for item in response.output:
    if item.type == "function_call":
        result = execute_function(item.name, item.arguments)
        history.append({"type": "function_call_output", "call_id": item.call_id, "output": result})
response = client.responses.create(model="your-model", input=history, tools=tools, store=False)
```

For server-managed history, opt in with `store:true` and use `previous_response_id`
on the next request. Only the new messages or function results need to be sent:

```python
first = client.responses.create(model="your-model", input="Remember the value 73", store=True)
second = client.responses.create(
    model="your-model", previous_response_id=first.id,
    input="What value did I give you?", store=True,
)
saved = client.responses.retrieve(second.id)
items = list(client.responses.input_items.list(second.id, order="asc", limit=20))
client.responses.delete(first.id)
```

A response containing a function call can be continued with just its matching
`function_call_output` items and `previous_response_id`. Declare the available tools
again in each request. **Instructions and generation settings are not inherited**:
`instructions` applies only to the current request. An earlier explicit system or
developer message in `input` remains part of conversation history.

`store:false` can also continue a stored response, but its new result is not saved.
Successful and output-limited responses are saved before their terminal event is
sent. Failed or disconnected foreground generations are not stored. A stored response becomes
available only when generation finishes. Background tasks have a separately bounded
live record and event history as described below.

### Retention and Isolation

- Storage is **in memory**, per running Maya service; it does not survive a restart.
- At most **128 cached responses** and **64 MiB of serialized unique item blocks,
  response metadata and item references** are kept by default. Python object
  overhead, request-owned expanded histories and background records are separate.
- Responses expire **one hour after completion**, even if read again. Access and
  insertion discard expired entries; capacity pressure evicts least recently used
  entries. A single oversized snapshot fails with a storage-capacity error.
- Records are isolated by the authenticated API key. Bearer and `x-api-key` forms
  of the same key share access. Without configured authentication, all callers share
  the same cache, just as they share access to the model.
- Expired, evicted, deleted, unknown and other-key IDs return HTTP 404, including
  when supplied as `previous_response_id`. History is never silently dropped.
- Snapshots reference shared immutable JSON items within one credential scope.
  Deleting an ancestor does not delete its items while a descendant still references
  them. Input IDs and pagination order stay stable, without recursive parent lookups.
- Concurrent snapshot assembly has its own serialized-byte admission limit; pressure
  returns HTTP 503 rather than dropping history. Limits and optional owner quotas
  are documented in [Responses settings](#settings-and-metrics).

### Resource Endpoints

| Method | Path | Behavior |
| --- | --- | --- |
| GET | `/v1/responses/{id}` | Retrieve the saved final response |
| DELETE | `/v1/responses/{id}` | Delete that snapshot |
| GET | `/v1/responses/{id}/input_items` | List its full input history, excluding its own output |
| POST | `/v1/responses/{id}/cancel` | Request cancellation of a background task |

Input lists accept `order=asc|desc` (default `desc`), `limit=1..100` (default 20),
and `after=<item_id>`. Item IDs stay stable across pages. The OpenAI SDK's automatic
pagination is supported. Background retrieval also accepts `stream=true` and an
optional nonnegative `starting_after` sequence number. Deletion accepts no query
options; additional `include` fields remain unsupported.

## Structured Output

`text.format` accepts `text`, `json_object`, or `json_schema`. Schema output uses
`name`, `schema`, and optional boolean `strict`:

```python
response = client.responses.create(
    model="your-model", input="Return the count",
    text={"format": {"type": "json_schema", "name": "count", "strict": True,
        "schema": {"type": "object", "properties": {"count": {"type": "integer"}},
                   "required": ["count"], "additionalProperties": False}}},
)
```

Schemas use Draft 2020-12 validation from `jsonschema`. The supported subset includes
objects/properties/required/additionalProperties, arrays/items, enum/const/anyOf,
basic and nullable types, numeric bounds/multipleOf, string length and array length.
The root must be an object. Strict object schemas must set
`additionalProperties:false` and require every property; optional values can be
represented with a nullable type. References, definitions, patterns, formats,
conditionals and unknown keywords are rejected before generation. Limits: 64 KiB,
512 schema nodes and 24 schema nesting levels.

This is **validate-before-publish**, not token-level constrained decoding. JSON
instructions are added to the model prompt. When structured output or a strict
tool is requested, generated events are buffered (4 MiB estimated byte/object
budget). Only a fully valid completed result is published. Invalid JSON, wrong
types or schema violations produce `response.failed` with consumed usage, without
publishing invalid text/tool deltas. No automatic generation retries are performed.
An output limit yields `response.incomplete` with usage and no unvalidated content.
This changes streaming latency: lifecycle/keep-alive events arrive first, content
and argument events arrive after validation.

Validation accepts at most 65,536 instance nodes and 32 instance nesting levels,
with a one-second budget checked during schema traversal and cancellation checks.
JSON parsing itself is bounded by the buffer size. Ordinary text does not require
the `jsonschema` import; standard setup installs it for structured/schema output.
Use `client.responses.parse(..., text_format=YourPydanticModel)` for supported
Pydantic schemas; models generating unsupported keywords are rejected explicitly.

## Background Tasks and Recovery

`background:true` requires explicit `store:true`:

```python
job = client.responses.create(model="your-model", input="Long request", background=True, store=True)
current = client.responses.retrieve(job.id)
for event in client.responses.retrieve(job.id, stream=True):
    pass
# Reconnect strictly after a previously received sequence number:
for event in client.responses.retrieve(job.id, stream=True, starting_after=last_sequence):
    pass
cancelled = client.responses.cancel(job.id)
```

Creation returns a queued response; background+stream can attach immediately.
Workers own preparation, image embeddings, generation and cleanup. The existing
engine FIFO still controls actual GPU access. A disconnected reader does not stop
the task. Ordinary foreground disconnects continue to stop generation.

Queries expose queued/in_progress and a single completed/incomplete/failed/cancelled
terminal state. Cancellation removes queued jobs without starting generation;
running cancellation signals the generator, waits for cleanup, and only then
publishes cancelled. The cancel call waits up to five seconds; a slow backend may
still return in_progress and must be polled. A running image encoder operation
must return/clean up before cancellation completes. No cancelled state is fabricated
while the worker still owns resources. Deadline expiry fails the task after cleanup.

Cancellation is idempotent. Its SSE stream terminates with the standard `error`
event (`code:"response_cancelled"`); the authoritative response snapshot has
`status:"cancelled"`. No nonstandard `response.cancelled` event is emitted. Active
tasks cannot be deleted (409); cancel them first. Cancelled/failed responses cannot
be used as previous_response_id. Running tasks cannot yet be continued (409).

Background records are independent of cache LRU: by default at most 16 records,
two workers, four active tasks per key, 300-second admission-to-finish deadline,
2 MiB input snapshots per task and 900 seconds of completed-record retention.
Completed records can be evicted early to admit new tasks; active ones cannot.
Their snapshots remain retrievable and can supply continuation history during
retention even if the ordinary response cache evicts its copy. This is additional
bounded storage, separate from the ordinary cache's TTL/byte limit.

Event buffers retain at most 4096 events / 2 MiB per task. A slow reader whose
cursor falls behind gets HTTP 410 before streaming, or a terminal standard SSE
`error` event if already connected. Future cursors get 400; a known response whose
event record is no longer retained gets 409 for streaming retrieval. Full snapshot
retrieval remains available while either record/cache retains it. No events are
silently skipped. Oversized events fail the task explicitly. Reader admission is
bounded globally/per key and slow socket writes time out; reader disconnects free
their slot without cancelling background generation.

Queue/owner admission pressure returns 429; an oversized input snapshot returns 413.
All resource, event and cancellation operations use the same credential isolation.
Shutdown stops admission, cancels queued/running jobs and joins workers. A backend
that has not released resources within the shutdown wait raises an explicit cleanup
error rather than claiming successful cancellation.

## Settings and Metrics

All values are integer server settings, read at Service creation. Put them in the
config's `env` block, or export them before starting Maya. Names below have the
prefix **`MAYA_GLM_RESPONSES_`** (legacy `STRATA_GLM_RESPONSES_` spellings also work;
the `MAYA_` spelling wins when both are set). Owner cache quotas default to 0 (disabled); all
other values must be positive. Byte values count serialized data, not process RSS.

| Suffix | Default | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| CACHE_ENTRIES | 128 | Cached response records |
| CACHE_BYTES | 67108864 | Unique blocks plus metadata/reference bytes |
| CACHE_TTL_S | 3600 | Fixed response cache TTL |
| ENTRY_BYTES | 67108864 | Expanded single snapshot size limit |
| OWNER_ENTRIES | 0 | Per-key cache record quota |
| OWNER_BYTES | 0 | Per-key unique cache byte quota |
| ASSEMBLY_BYTES | 67108864 | Concurrent expanded snapshot assembly budget |
| JOB_WORKERS | 2 | Worker threads (maximum 32) |
| JOB_ENTRIES | 16 | Active plus retained job records (maximum 4096) |
| JOB_OWNER_ENTRIES | 4 | Active tasks per credential |
| JOB_TIMEOUT_S | 300 | Task deadline, including queue time |
| JOB_TTL_S | 900 | Completed task/event retention |
| JOB_INPUT_BYTES | 2097152 | Per-job initial response/input snapshot limit |
| EVENT_BYTES | 2097152 | Per-job retained event bytes (minimum 4096) |
| EVENT_COUNT | 4096 | Per-job retained event count |
| STREAM_READERS | 32 | Concurrent background stream readers |
| STREAM_OWNER_READERS | 8 | Concurrent readers per credential |
| STREAM_TIMEOUT_S | 5 | Background socket write timeout |

`/v1/status` exposes aggregate cache/job statistics. `/metrics/prometheus` exports
`maya:responses_cache_*` and `maya:responses_jobs_*`, including occupancy, hits,
misses, writes, rejection/eviction/expiry counts, timings, in-flight assembly,
queue/running jobs, event bytes and readers. No credential identifiers or prompt
contents appear in metrics. Request-owned decoded histories are released by their
request/worker; background records, readers and event copies have their own limits.

Shared payload blocks reduce repeated history storage. Snapshots use flat item
references, preserve descendants after ancestor deletion, and reconstruct input
history without recursive parent lookups.

## Limits

Unsupported parameters and item types return HTTP 400 before generation. In particular:

- the Conversations API, item references, and WebSocket transport;
- hosted/custom tools, encrypted reasoning, reasoning summaries, files and audio;
- token-level constrained decoding, unsupported JSON Schema keywords, automatic
  input truncation, and non-default service tiers. Namespace tools remain unsupported.

Use `/v1/chat/completions` for Maya-specific extensions such as `return_progress`
and `strata_mcp`. The `/v1/status` dialect list advertises the Responses endpoint.


# Tuning and settings

Everything on this page is optional: the engine sizes itself for the PC it runs on. Back to the
[README](../README.md).

The settings below are environment variables named `MAYA_*`: set them before a start, or keep them in the config
with `./maya.sh --setup --env MAYA_NAME=value`. Their earlier `STRATA_*` spellings still work; where both are set, the
`MAYA_` one counts.

Responses API cache, background queue, event/reader limits and owner quotas are
server settings under `MAYA_GLM_RESPONSES_*`, with defaults and units in
[Responses settings](RESPONSES.md#settings-and-metrics). They can be set in the same
config `env` block; response storage and background work remain request opt-ins.

The engine sizes itself: it splits the layers across the GPUs it is given (up to 16; with more than two, each GPU
takes layers in proportion to its free VRAM), fills each GPU's free VRAM with experts, sizes its RAM tier from the
free RAM, and measures the CPU against the PCIe link at start to decide how many RAM experts the CPU computes itself.

**Tuning for your PC** (`./maya.sh --calibrate`, also offered at the end of the setup): that start-up guess times one
expert each way; the tuning measures the real output speed instead, in one engine run (about 10-15 minutes, most of
it loading the model), with a few splits of the RAM-tier experts between the CPU and the PCIe link and with fewer CPU
threads - more threads than the memory can feed only wait, and on a hybrid CPU the efficiency cores can hold the rest
up - and, on one GPU whose CPUs show no SMT siblings, with more (up to the CPUs less two, while each count beats the
best so far: a VM's vCPUs have no SMT siblings, so the engine takes two thirds of them, and a 16-vCPU guest decoded
fastest with 14) - and, when part of the model is read from the SSD, with smaller disk reads (the best size depends on the drive and
its driver). Every measurement answers prompts it has not answered before, as a chat's text is new to the expert tiers (the
same few answers over and over left VRAM holding exactly their experts, and the speed it reported was one no chat
reached), and the engine saves no learned expert profile (`--expert-profile-save`) and works on a copy of a usage
file (`MAYA_GLM_USAGE`), so the tuning's answers do not change what the
next start loads first. A setting is kept when it is more than 3% faster than the engine's own choice. The result
goes into the config's `"env"` (`MAYA_GLM_PCIE_SHARE`, `MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE`, `MAYA_GLM_READ_CHUNKS`) and into
`~/.config/project-maya/calibration.json` for this PC, model and context, so setting up again keeps it. Stop a
running server first: the tuning needs the GPU(s).

**The CPUs the engine runs on.** The CPU lane's threads share the engine's CPUs with the thread that issues every
decode step and with the expert tiers' service threads (one a GPU), which are busy most of the time while experts sit in
RAM; the lane's idle workers also spin for a while after each batch. The default thread count leaves a third of the
cores to them only when the engine may use every CPU of a PC whose cores have no SMT siblings. Confined to fewer CPUs
(`taskset`, a container's cpuset), the lane takes a thread for every core it may use, and a decode step then waits for
a time slice as well as for its experts. So give the engine all the CPUs you can, on a hybrid CPU the efficiency cores
as well as the performance cores, or, where it has to stay confined, set `MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE` (`MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE<n>`)
lower so a few of its cores stay free. The start log says how many threads each GPU's lane has and what an expert
costs there (`glm fast: CUDA0 CPU lane: 8 threads, an expert ... ms on the CPU vs ... ms over PCIe`).

These settings change what the engine chooses (put them in the config with `--env`, or into its `"env"` block):

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MAYA_GLM_EXPERT_HELPER` | unset (off) | Experimental native-GGUF expert helper: the visible GPU index (`1`, not a Boolean). GPU 0 keeps the trunk and MTP; the helper owns a complementary expert cache, with heat-coordinated replacement and GPU quantization/reduction. Alone: `MAYA_GLM_SPLIT=0`, without `--layer-split`. Beside a layer split (`MAYA_GLM_SPLIT=auto` or boundaries): the helper's device is left out of the split and each part owns its own share of the helper over its own layers - two cards and a Ryzen AI Max APU as the unified-memory helper; `MAYA_GLM_DEVS` may name the split's devices (never the helper's). Unset this variable to disable. UMA helpers alias the pinned RAM tier; discrete helpers use their VRAM. See [AMD helper setup and scope](AMD_MAYA.md#experimental-expert-helper) |
| `MAYA_GLM_HELPER_CHECK` | unset (off) | A load-time self-check of the expert helper, on a sample: of every MoE layer it serves, up to N (1-8) of its experts - their RAM-tier bytes against the pack's, then their weighted sum on a fixed input by the helper and by the part's own GPU with the same kernels. A differing byte or a sum beyond 1e-5 relative fails the start; a layer split checks each part's helper once all of them are open. One `glm helper check:` line per part, counting its MoE layers, those holding helper experts and those checked (or saying NO helper / NO expert) |
| `MAYA_GLM_HELPER_ZEROCOPY` | on for an integrated helper | An integrated (unified-memory) expert helper reads each call's input and plan from, and writes its result into, one mapped pinned block - four copies fewer a call; `0` makes the copies (a discrete helper always copies). With `MAYA_GLM_PROF` set, each helper prints a `glm helper prof` line at the end: its calls, experts a call, host time (enqueue, wait, the result's copy), each stage's GPU time and the bandwidth its kernels read the experts at |
| `MAYA_GLM_RAM_HEADROOM_GB` | 6 | RAM left free for the system when the RAM tier is sized (Apple Silicon: unified memory left free beside maya-metal's expert pool, from what is free when it starts); it also takes one more GPU's prompt buffers, pinned after the measurement - a split of three or more GPUs sets the others' aside on top, and each later GPU measures again and leaves at least half of it free. The pinned tier can't be swapped out, so a Linux desktop whose kernel starts swapping with more free (several displays, a busy session) wants more: a 128 GB desktop with three GPUs and three displays stayed responsive at 16, and swapped at 12 (#77) |
| `MAYA_GLM_RAM_GB` | from free RAM | a fixed RAM-tier size in GB |
| `MAYA_GLM_COMPLETE_POOL` | on | where the GPU's expert budget holds every expert, the 3 spares a layer and the prompt path's borrowed slots (the Ryzen AI Max APUs' memory on Windows - a Radeon 8065S holds Maya-S with ~60 GB to spare), each layer keeps exactly that many: the start loads every expert, a prompt borrows only the slots beyond them (none moved out, none read from the SSD again), the decode never misses and the CPU lane has nothing to take (8065S, Maya-S: decode +2.6-4%, +7% after an 8K-token prompt, the same answers; NVIDIA since v1.1.35, where a split held every expert and the capped pool lent residents to every long prompt: a 4x A100 split's decode 2.5x, #474). `0` = off (the capped pool, for A/B). Where the budget cannot hold every expert nothing changes. Linux APUs keep their unified-memory policy |
| `MAYA_GLM_RESIDENT_SKIP` | AMD: on | while every expert is in VRAM, the decode leaves out each route's wait and fetch launches, which would find nothing to do (two a layer and token); `0` = launch them anyway; `1` = on for NVIDIA too |
| `MAYA_GLM_FLUSH`, `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_FLUSH` | off, off | Windows + AMD only, experimental: the HIP runtime holds queued kernels until the host waits; `n` = the decode submits its first layer as soon as it is queued and then every n layers, `1` = the prompt path each sub-batch and each layer's experts (`cudaStreamQuery`, which does not wait). On a Radeon 8065S every 4 layers was ~4% slower than without; prompts no faster |
| `MAYA_GLM_RAM_RESIDENT` | off | `1` (or `--glm-ram-resident`): the RAM tier holds every expert not in VRAM and nothing is evicted to disk. The start fails if it cannot. For PCs whose RAM holds everything off-card (a 4090 D with 128 GB: decode 1.9x as fast with `PROMOTE_MIN=6`). `MAYA_GLM_RAM_SLACK` is extra slots per MoE layer (default 16) |
| `MAYA_GLM_RAM_BALANCED` | off | opt-in, single GPU with `RAM_RESIDENT=1`: reserve every expert class's mandatory capacity (including prefill lending and promotion spares) before distributing optional slack. On Windows/NVIDIA, cap the plan by the matching adapter's DXGI non-local budget, less current usage, staging/update buffers and the existing 512 MiB upload guard. `RAM_SLACK` is best-effort in this mode. Driver pinning must still succeed; insufficient mandatory capacity fails startup, never falls back to disk. Does not bypass driver page-locking limits. Non-resident and staging-only tiers retain their existing policy; multi-GPU resident use is rejected |
| `MAYA_GLM_SPLIT` | `auto`: a split search on 2-4 GPUs (and on more under DFlash2's pipelined window), by free VRAM with more | the first layer of each later GPU (`24`, or `7,12,17,22,27,32,37,41` for nine); `0` = one GPU. The config's `"layer_split"` sets the same. `auto` prices every placement before loading: each card's room for experts after its layers' weights and state, the share of each layer's routes the experts it then holds take (your usage counts; without them a built-in coverage curve, 1 - (1 - f)^3 of the routes for a share f of a layer's experts, ^1.4 for an EXL3 pack - `MAYA_SPLIT_COVER_B` sets the exponent; EXL3-4.05bpw under DFlash2 over an RTX 3090 and eight 5060 Ti: the 3090 takes 9 layers instead of 7, 63.8 / 32.6 / 64.4 -> 79.8 / 40.4 / 81.9 tok/s), each layer's reads over the card's memory bandwidth and each miss over the host's RAM speed - and takes the fastest one every card can start (on a two-GPU split that drafts with the model's MTP block the two cards work on consecutive tokens, so the token costs the slower card: RTX 3090 + 5060 Ti drafting with a NextN block, 24 -> 26, the cards 25.3 / 32.2 -> 28.1 / 29.8 ms a token, decode +3-5%). Under DFlash2's pipelined window (three GPUs or more) the slowest card paces every draft: the search runs on any number of GPUs (from five, a DP over the layers for each cap on the slowest card), each layer also costs a fixed 0.4 ms (`MAYA_GLM_SPLIT_LAYER_MS`) and a miss is priced at the shared CPU pool's speed, half the host's (Maya-L over an RTX 3090 and eight 5060 Ti: the 3090 takes 8 layers instead of the 10 its VRAM share gave it, 57.1 / 58.9 -> 61.6 / 63.1 tok/s). `MAYA_GLM_SPLIT_LOG=1` prints every placement's prediction |
| `MAYA_GLM_PARALLEL_LOAD` | on | a split's GPUs load at once, a thread each: they plan their RAM tiers one after another in GPU order (each gets the tier a load one by one gives it), then pin and warm them at the same time (4 x Tesla T4, Maya-L from a cold page cache: start to the first token 142 -> 96 s). `0` = one GPU after another |
| `MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE` | one thread per physical core (one GPU, or one pool shared by a split of 3+ GPUs: at most one NUMA node's; two GPUs: half each; cores without SMT: two thirds of them) | CPU threads for RAM-tier experts; `0` = off (the tuning sets it) |
| `MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE_MAX` | unset | a CPU-lane pool of this many threads (at most 64) with the count above active and the rest asleep, so a request's `cpu_threads` can try more than the engine started with (the engine's INFO line says the pool's size as `cpu_threads_max`) - the tuning's own engine runs with it set to the PC's CPUs on one GPU without SMT siblings. No effect at or below the count above, on a pool a split of 3+ GPUs shares, or on DFlash2's two CPU lanes |
| `MAYA_GLM_ROW_HALVES` | off | `1`, a host with two NUMA nodes: each RAM-tier slot keeps its layout ([gate \| up \| down], whole for the device) but the low half of each matrix's rows sits on the first node and the high half on the second (4 KB pages, `mbind` preferred - a full node spills to the other), and the CPU lane's jobs go in a queue a node, each thread taking its own node's rows first: no expert byte crosses the socket link. The rows and their arithmetic are the shared queue's, so the answers are the same bit for bit. 2x Xeon E5-2690 v4 + 3x V100 + RTX 3060, Maya-S v2, one conversation: 42.5 -> ~46 tok/s; four conversations at 128K: part of the 80-84 tok/s in `bench/results/2026-11-02-4gpu-4x128k` |
| `MAYA_GLM_LANE_CPUS<n>` | unset | with a pool of its own for CUDA&lt;n&gt; (`MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE<n>`): that pool's CPUs exactly (`0,1,2,28,...`), e.g. cores of both sockets so that `MAYA_GLM_ROW_HALVES` still reads node-local rows. (Four pools of 12 on both sockets measured 13 tok/s a conversation against 20+ for one shared pool of 48 - the shared pool stays the default) |
| `MAYA_GLM_POOL_CPUS`, `MAYA_GLM_SVC_CPUS<n>` | unset | the shared CPU-lane pool's workers one to a CPU, in the listed order; CUDA&lt;n&gt;'s service thread (its routes' last worker) on the listed CPUs. Diagnostics - no gain measured on the 2-socket Xeon |
| `MAYA_GLM_LANE_PROF` | off | `1`: the CPU lane prints its per-stage times (activation quantize, gate/up, down, sum) and experts per call every 4096 calls |
| `MAYA_GLM_PF_CE` | off | with `MAYA_GLM_PREFETCH_N`: the next layer's predicted experts are copied by the service thread with `cudaMemcpyAsync` on a high-priority stream (the copy engines) instead of the side stream's copy kernel, the layer waiting on a flag. On 3x V100 + RTX 3060 it took the prefetch from 33-38 to 42-44 tok/s, still below no prefetch (~46) - the PCIe wait outweighs the hits it adds |
| `MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE<n>` | the setting above | the same for CUDA`<n>` alone (`MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE0=6`, `MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE1=2`): two cards on links of different speed want different counts - the slower the link, the more CPU threads help. RTX 4070 Ti SUPER on PCIe 3.0 x4 + RTX 5070 Ti on 4.0 x16, split 17: 6 / 2 threads beat the default 4 / 4 |
| `MAYA_GLM_CPU_PIN` | Linux: on with two GPUs on a host with two NUMA nodes or more. Windows: never | a split whose GPUs have a pool each (two GPUs, or `MAYA_GLM_CPU_SHARED=0`): every GPU's CPU lane on CPUs of its own, a thread a physical core of them (the SMT siblings stay free) - a whole NUMA node while there are enough (its GPU's node when the GPUs hang off different ones), else an even share of a node's physical cores - so one card's idle workers never spin on the cores the other's are using (2 x Xeon Gold 6152, RTX 3090 + 5060 Ti: decode 15.4 -> 19.8 tok/s); on one NUMA node it stays off - there the parts' pools sharing every core were faster (two V100s on one 14-core Xeon: 25.8 tok/s unpinned, 24.7 pinned). `1` = pinned on any Linux host, `0` = unpinned. Linux only: on Windows the engine pins nothing whatever this says, and one GPU is never pinned anywhere, so `0` changes nothing there |
| `MAYA_GLM_CPU_SHARED` | on with 3+ GPUs | a split of three or more GPUs runs its cards one after another, so one CPU pool serves them all in turn, sized as one GPU's; `0` = a pool per GPU with the cores divided between them (A/B), `1` = one pool for two GPUs too. A card given its own count (`MAYA_GLM_CPU_LANE<n>`) keeps a pool of its own |
| `MAYA_GLM_CPU_CONCURRENT` | on | the pool a split shares: several GPUs' routes on it at once, a later GPU's jobs first (under DFlash2's pipelined window each GPU holds an earlier token than the one before it); `0` = one route at a time, the others waiting for it whole (A/B). The same answers either way |
| `MAYA_GLM_CPU_CAL` | unset | a file (e.g. `~/.config/project-maya/cpu-cal.txt`): the CPU lane's timing at start (an expert on the CPU, one over PCIe; ~1.7 s a GPU) is kept there, a line a GPU, and later starts read it. A new engine build, another pack, card, slot or thread count times again; a line that decode finds more than 15% off five times in a row is dropped, and the next start times again |
| `MAYA_GLM_KDA_GRAPH` | off | experimental: `1` replays single-GPU KDA decode layers as CUDA graphs; DSA, MTP/split, profiling, dumps, prefetch and lookahead stay direct. Two thinking-Max 32K/2048 pairs on v1.0.21: 30.73 -> 31.45 tok/s (+2.35%). Current-main full native/quality validation is pending; see [scope and checks](GLM-KDA-GRAPHS.md) |
| `MAYA_GLM_CPU_SPLIT` | about 48 pieces in all | decode: each CPU-lane thread's share of a token's RAM-tier experts is cut into this many pieces (1-64), claimed in turn, so one thread held up by the disk readers doesn't hold up the token. Few threads take several pieces each (6 threads: 8 - one V100, Maya-S: 17.1 tok/s against 14.3 with one); 40 or more take one each, which streams the memory best |
| `MAYA_GLM_PCIE_SHARE` | measured at start | the share of a token's RAM-tier experts copied over PCIe and run on the GPU instead of on the CPU: `0` = the CPU takes every one, `1` = none (the tuning sets it; `MAYA_GLM_CPU_PLAN` = the split per expert count, digits for 0..8) |
| `MAYA_GLM_CPU_RETIME` | on | the split the start-up timing chose (neither of the two above set) is timed again in decode: over the first windows of 2,000 CPU-lane experts (at most eight; the first only warms up), when the lane's own cost an expert there is more than 15% from the one the split was planned with the same way in two windows in a row, the split is planned again from their mean, at most three times, with one `CPU lane timed again in decode` line each. A start on a busy CPU (one timed 0.611 ms an expert against the usual ~0.24) no longer keeps its split for the engine's life. `0` = off |
| `MAYA_GLM_P2P` | unset (off) | Direct peer-to-peer PCIe transfers between discrete GPUs on AMD Instinct MI50 (`gfx906`) under HIP. When set to `1` across a multi-GPU layer split, intermediate token states transfer directly across PCIe BARs without staging through host memory. Earlier `STRATA_GLM_P2P` spelling still works |
| `MAYA_GLM_DUAL_PREFILL` | off | `1`, two GPUs that reach each other's memory (NVLink): a prompt of one chunk runs the split's halves one after the other, so the waiting GPU and its PCIe link sit idle. With this on, the waiting GPU stages every second expert of the working half's staging groups: host to its own (idle) staging buffer over its own link, then peer to peer over NVLink. A group then arrives through both links. Longer prompts (several chunks) are pipelined across the GPUs and stage as before. The answers stay the same unless the prompt's CPU share plan, which learns from the copy times, gives the CPU different experts (`MAYA_GLM_SCORE_PREFILL=1`, 3000-token context: KL 0.000000). Maya-M on 2x RTX 3090 (x8 each + NVLink), Ryzen 3950X, 128 GB: prompt 1K 283 -> 362 tokens/s (+28 %), 4K 622 -> 772 (+24 %), 16K 1034 -> 1142 (+10 %), 64K unchanged. Over PCIe-only peer access the copy would cross the working GPU's link anyway, so expect no gain there. The start checks that peer copies arrive intact (a 4 MB pattern each way): a machine whose GPUs report peer access but deliver wrong bytes keeps it off and says so |
| `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_CALC` | off | `1`, two GPUs without peer access (no NVLink; with it `MAYA_GLM_DUAL_PREFILL` is the one): while one GPU runs its half of a one-chunk prompt, the waiting GPU **computes** a share of each MoE layer's RAM-tier experts - their weights over its own PCIe link from the working GPU's pinned RAM tier, the chunk's inputs and routes over the host to it, the weighted sum of its rows back - so both links stream. The share is learned per GPU from both lanes' times (`MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_CALC_SHARE=0..0.9` fixes it); the GPU with fewer multiprocessors takes the lightest experts. EXL3 experts only; longer prompts (several chunks) are pipelined and stage as before. No VRAM beyond the prompt path's loan (the waiting GPU's buffers are carved from its own idle prompt buffers); a chunk's sum (T x n_embd floats) of pinned RAM more per GPU. Not bit-exact - the share's rows are summed apart and added, and cuBLAS may pick other kernels on the other GPU: `MAYA_GLM_SCORE_PREFILL=1`, 2048-token context, CPU lane off: KL 0.0126, the size of any re-blocking (`MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_SUB=512`: 0.0138). turboderp's sc_3.10bpw on RTX 3090 + RTX 3060 (PCIe 4.0 x8 each), Ryzen 9 5900X, most experts in RAM: prompt 512 tokens 98 -> 144 tokens/s (+48 %), 1K 155 -> 229 (+48 %), 2K 244 -> 349 (+43 %), 4K 352 -> 468 (+33 %), 7K 445 -> 557 (+25 %). `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_CALC_CHECK=1` (debug, slow) computes the share again on the working GPU and prints how far the two sums differ, layer by layer |
| `MAYA_GLM_PREFETCH_N` | 0 | `1` or `2` (more is read as 2; the engine log says the settings at start): each layer copies up to this many of the next layer's predicted experts (its router on this layer's input) that are not in VRAM into that layer's spares while it computes. `MAYA_GLM_PREFETCH_AT`: when the copy starts - `route` (the default: at once, beside this layer's own PCIe fetch and CPU lane), `fetch` (after this layer's fetch) or `cpu` (after its CPU lane); `MAYA_GLM_PREFETCH_BLOCKS`: the copy's blocks (default half the SMs); `MAYA_GLM_PREFETCH_RANK`: copy only the prediction's first n ranks (its first guesses are right almost always, its 5th-8th mostly not). Maya-M on an RTX 3090 + 3060 with `RAM_RESIDENT=1`, decode at 21K: off 19.64, `N=1` 17.0, `N=1 AT=fetch BLOCKS=4` 19.75, `+ RANK=2` 20.22 tokens/s (VRAM hits 64 -> 69 %); Maya-L there (bigger experts, a busier link): no gain |
| `MAYA_GLM_ROUTE_RANK` | on | a micro-optimization of the decode's route kernel: its top-k picks by rank, every thread at once, instead of one warp's k serial rounds (the same experts in the same order, ties to the lower id). A V100: the route kernel 40 -> 35 us, decode 0.15-0.33% faster. `0` = the serial rounds (A/B). Used when the experts are a multiple of 32 and at most 320 (GLM's 256) |
| `MAYA_GLM_ROUTE_FAST` | on | a micro-optimization of the decode's route kernel, in the order it reads and writes: the route error flag from a device copy (the host-mapped one was a PCIe round trip at every route's start), the CPU lane's request input out at the start, the plan's global reads (route counts, helper flags, spares, the predicted experts' entries) loaded in parallel into shared memory, the request's head a thread a field, and the weights' sum and the CPU lane's picks on a warp instead of one thread. The same plan and request. A V100: the route kernel ~35 -> ~29 us (-12 % and -8 % more), decode 0.1-0.4 % faster deterministic. `0` = the old order (A/B). Experts per token at most 8 |
| `MAYA_GLM_ROUTE_PROF` | off | measurement: `1` times the decode route kernel's phases in cycles (block 0, from its start) and the plan's own phases, averaged over the routes and printed at exit |
| `MAYA_GLM_NUMA` | on with 2+ NUMA nodes | `0` = allocate the RAM tier without spreading it page by page over the sockets' memory |
| `MAYA_GLM_READ_CHUNKS` | 8 | the experts read from the SSD while it answers (and a prompt's): each part of one in this many pieces at once, 1-16. The best size depends on the drive, its driver and the CPU - 8 on a Linux NVMe, 4 on a Windows laptop's Intel RST RAID (decode +15%) - and the tuning measures 4 and 2 against 8 when part of the model lives on the SSD |
| `MAYA_GLM_STREAM_FETCH` | on | an expert the decode reads from the SSD goes to the GPU piece by piece as its `MAYA_GLM_READ_CHUNKS` pieces land, instead of after the whole read - the copy hides behind the read; the same answers. GGUF packs, up to 10 pieces a part. A V100, Maya-S24 with an 8 GB RAM tier on a 32 GB PC (~59 disk reads a token): decode +2-34% (6 of 6 pairs, ~+22% on average); every expert in RAM: unchanged. `0` = after the whole read (before v1.1.39) |
| `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_CHUNK` | unset | `--prefill N` from the environment (the older setting; it wins over the config's `--prefill`) |
| `MAYA_PREFILL_LEND_PCT`, `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_MB` | 90 or 85, unset | `--prefill auto`: the share of each card's expert-pool slots a prompt may borrow for its buffers: 90 when at least 90% of the experts' bytes are held pinned (in VRAM or the RAM tier), else 85 - or a fixed budget in MB |
| `MAYA_GLM_LEND_PARK_LRU` | 1 | when a prompt borrows the pool's tail, an expert leaving VRAM is moved over a RAM-tier expert of a lower use count - and, at `1`, on equal counts over the one used less recently (the counts age, so most are equal). A V100, Maya-S24, 512 tokens after a fresh 24K prompt: disk reads 520-578 -> 309-337, decode +2-5%. `0` = a strictly lower count only (before v1.1.21). `MAYA_GLM_TIER_DIAG=1` counts the disk reads by cause (`MAYA_GLM_TIER_DIAG_EVERY`: every N boundaries, 64) |
| `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_TAIL_SKIP` | on | single-device prompts without a loaded NextN/MTP block: skip the last layer's attention output projection and FFN after updating all its caches; `0` restores the full computation. Splits and `GLM_CB_DIR` seam dumps keep the full path |
| `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_WINDOW` | the chunk's tokens | prompts: the expert output rows kept on the GPU at once - each expert set's rows are added into the layer's output as the window fills, instead of every routed row waiting for one combine (~40 KB a token instead of ~185, so a chunk holds ~2x the tokens); `0` = every row (the old layout) |
| `MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_FUSED` | on for gfx1151 and gfx1100 | HIP with MMQ built: fused int8 WMMA gate/up, clipped SwiGLU and down for GLM routed experts (4096/2048 geometry and covered IQ types). `0` retains MMQ and its scratch layout. Strix Halo measured +13.2/+7.5/+4.8% prompt speed at 4K/8K/16K on the v1.0.16-based prototype; with the wmma2 attention on a Radeon 8065S (Windows, Maya-S), 3K / 8K-token prompts 233 / 253 -> 271 / 278 tokens/s. 4x RX 7900 XT (gfx1100, Maya-S24, ROCm 10): +5-13% at 1.3K-16K-token prompts. Other AMD architectures retain MMQ. |
| `MAYA_PF_WMMA` | off | NVIDIA Volta (sm_70; other cards keep MMQ): the prompt path's routed-expert GEMMs run as a grouped WMMA kernel - weights dequantized to FP16 in shared memory, FP16 tensor-core tiles with FP32 accumulators - instead of MMQ's dp4a on the CUDA cores; decode is unchanged. The launcher checks each shape and falls back to MMQ whole-path from the first one it cannot run, so a device or format outside the covered set (the native packs' IQ3_XXS / IQ3_S / IQ2_S gate-up and IQ4_NL / Q2_0 down products, K a whole 256 values) costs nothing. Maya-M on 2x Tesla V100-32GB: prompt 2.6K tokens 8.18 -> 7.30 s (+11.6%), 7.8K 12.63 -> 11.90 s (+5.8%), 20K 26.14 -> 24.3 s (+7%); decode unchanged (~50 ms/token either way). The outputs differ from MMQ's within FP16 weight rounding and FP32 accumulation order (checked against a double reference over the same FP16 weights by the `pf_wmma_parity` test, max error <= 2e-5 of the matrix's max magnitude) |
| `MAYA_GLM_USAGE` | off | a file to keep your expert usage in between starts (written after every request; the warm-up then loads your experts ahead of the expert profile's). Off, a start goes by the expert profile alone (below); a pack's old `expert_usage.txt` is no longer read unless this names it |
| `MAYA_GLM_SLOTS` | 4 | conversations kept aside on the SSD, so switching back to one doesn't re-read its prompt; `0` = off |
| `MAYA_GLM_PREFIX_CUT` | `1` (on with the slots) | with the slots on and no checkpoints (`--parallel` above 1 without `MAYA_GLM_MULTI_CKPTS`, or `MAYA_GLM_CKPTS=0`): a prompt read from token 0 also ends a call at the end of its system prompt (the first user turn, at least `MAYA_GLM_SLOT_MIN` tokens, 512 past where the read starts) and keeps the state there in a slot of its own - another conversation with the same system prompt and tools (a new conversation of the same client, another request of the same kind) restores it and reads only the rest, as the one-conversation path does through its checkpoints. Once per system prompt; such a slot goes only as the least recently used, after a restart with `MAYA_GLM_SLOT_KEEP` too. A restored read gives the same tokens as a read from token 0 with the cut; the cut itself moves a chunk boundary of that read, so a greedy answer can differ from a read without it (as `--prefill N` can). `0` = off (no cut) |
| `MAYA_GLM_SNAP_GEN` | `0` | `1`: where the snapshot (the state the next turn continues from) stands: before the prompt's generation prompt (its trailing `<|assistant|>`, then `<think>` or `<think></think>`) instead of before its last token. The next prompt of the conversation then continues it whatever came of the answer: the answer itself as before, nothing (an answer the client cancelled and sends again with a new message after it, without the cancelled answer), or the same prompt again (it reads 2-3 tokens). Before, a cancelled answer's next prompt left the snapshot at its `<|assistant|>` and, once another conversation had taken the model, read its whole prompt again. Opt-in: the prompt's last 2-4 tokens then go through the token path instead of the batched one, so a greedy answer can differ from one with the default. `0` = before the last token |
| `MAYA_GLM_KEEP_CANCELLED` | `1` | a prompt read the client cancelled (it went away, or gave up waiting) keeps what it read: the longest checkpoint of that prompt the read reached becomes the snapshot, so the same prompt sent again reads only the rest, and a conversation that runs in between sets it aside in a slot. With one conversation the cancel is also seen between two calls of the read (a call of one chunk never asked for it, so the read went on to its end). Text prompts only (checkpoints are). `0` = the state is dropped and the next prompt reads from token 0, as before |
| `MAYA_GLM_CKPTS`, `MAYA_GLM_CKPT_EVERY` | 4, 16384 | checkpoints of the conversation in the model at earlier positions than the one the next turn continues from, in RAM (~150 MB each for GLM-5.3-Flash): at the end of the system prompt (the first user turn), at the start of the prompt's last turn, and every `_EVERY` freshly read prompt tokens (`0` = none of these), where at least 512 fresh tokens lie before. A prompt that leaves the conversation earlier - an edited or replaced last message, another chat with the same system prompt and tools, a client that rewrote an older turn - restores the longest checkpoint it starts with and reads only the rest, where it read everything from token 0 (`glm checkpoints` in the engine log). At most `MAYA_GLM_CKPTS` (up to 16; `0` = off), the least recently used replaced; one conversation at a time (with `--parallel` only with `MAYA_GLM_MULTI_CKPTS`, below), text prompts only. Measured 2026-10-15 (Maya-L split, 8192-token chunks, a 100,535-token fresh read): `_EVERY` is also how many pieces the read is cut into, and every piece pays a fixed ~14.6 s fill beside ~6.5 s a chunk, so fewer pieces is straight wall time - 16384 cut that read into 5 pieces (49152 + 16384x3 + 2230) read in 148.8 s, 32768 into four (32768x3 + tail) in 134.9 s (-9.3%), `0` into one of 13 chunks in 93.2 s (**-37.4%**) |
| `MAYA_GLM_MULTI_CKPTS` | `0` (off) | `--parallel` above 1: `1` keeps the conversation checkpoints above for each conversation at once (#711) - in its own context, with its sequence state, so a request that leaves its conversation before the snapshot's position (an edited or regenerated last message, a cancelled answer sent again without it, a client that rewrote or trimmed an older turn) restores the longest checkpoint it starts with and reads only the rest, where it read from the system prompt's slot (`MAYA_GLM_PREFIX_CUT`) or from token 0. A request goes to the idle context whose snapshot or checkpoint it extends furthest, else the least recently used one, as before. Each context keeps up to `MAYA_GLM_CKPTS` (fewer when the contexts are many: 32 in all, ~150 MB of RAM each for GLM-5.3-Flash - 2.4 GB for four conversations at the default 4); the periodic ones (`MAYA_GLM_CKPT_EVERY`) stay off here unless that is set, since each one ends a prompt-path call while the others wait to decode. The system prompt's slot is still kept (another context restores it). Text prompts only |
| `MAYA_GLM_SLOT_MIN`, `MAYA_GLM_SLOT_GB`, `MAYA_GLM_SLOT_DIR` | 1024, 16, `<pack>/slots` | the shortest conversation kept aside (tokens, down to 1), their total size on disk (GB), and the folder |
| `MAYA_GLM_SLOT_KEEP`, `MAYA_GLM_SLOT_DAYS` | off, 7 | `1`: the kept conversations outlive the engine - the folder is not emptied at start (a slot is kept when its `.meta` names this model, engine version and pack, else removed), the conversation in the model is kept when the engine ends, and a slot unused for `MAYA_GLM_SLOT_DAYS` days is removed. With a supervisor that stops the model when it idles (llama-swap), the next request after a restart reads only its new tokens (a 4K-token conversation back in 0.2 s instead of re-read) |
| `MAYA_GLM_SEQS`, `MAYA_GLM_SEQ_CTX` | 1, the context | the first is `--parallel` (above): n conversations at once, n sequence states - the first for the whole context, the others for `MAYA_GLM_SEQ_CTX` tokens. On a split a token of each goes through the GPUs one behind the other, so every GPU works on a different conversation (4x RTX 4090, Maya-S24: 110 tok/s alone, 132 in all for two, 225 for four); each extra state takes its size from the expert pool (three at 64K: 3.5 GB) |
| `MAYA_GLM_SPLIT_ROWS` | `0` (off) | Multi-GPU decode only: `1`–`8` batches up to this many concurrent requests on each part. Each group shares dense weight reads while sequence-specific attention and routed experts remain per-request; groups pipeline across the split. Each part allocates row activations before sizing its expert pool (about 8 MiB per GPU). Unset or `0` preserves the existing pipeline.
