Maya loads so the model runs as MiniMax defines it - keep it with the other files. Pictures and the DSpark drafter
come from the EXL3 repo's own files (its vision tower and its draft, about 4 GB, downloaded into
`MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-companions` beside the model, or taken from an EXL3 download already there), on NVIDIA and on
AMD alike. The options above apply, but for `--tp` (the GGUFs run the layer split) and `--cache-gb`;
`tools/mm3/pack_gguf.py --model <folder>` builds the pack by hand (`--text-only`: without the companions).

**The prefill calibration.** How a PC reads prompts fastest depends on its GPU, so it is measured there: the setup
(step 9), `./maya.sh --calibrate` and the first start of a MiniMax config set up before it run the engine once over a
~4K-token text (`maya-mm3 --calibrate-prefill`, tools/calibrate_mm3_prefill.py) with a few settings - the chunk of
tokens a step takes and, with Maya's GGUF quants on AMD, the matrix products' library - and keep the fastest. A larger
chunk's buffers take VRAM from the experts: where every expert still fits it is simply taken when faster; where most
experts stay in RAM (each chunk then copies them over PCIe, so a larger one reads prompts much faster) a second start
with the default chunk measures the decode too, and the larger chunk is kept only when it reads prompts at least 25%
faster and decodes at most 3% slower. The engine stores it beside the model (`prefill-tune.txt`, a line
for this PC: its GPUs, their architecture, the GPU runtime and the driver) and takes it at every start; a new GPU or
driver gets the defaults until it is measured again. The config records the result (`installer.prefill_calibration`).
It takes one start of the model and a few minutes; the answers are the model's either way.

**Projecting a direction out (`--ablate`; off by default, changes the answers).** As DeepSeek's
[`--ablate`](#deepseek-projecting-a-direction-out---ablate): `--ablate FILE` removes one direction r from every residual write - each layer's attention output and its FFN output
(the dense MLP's in a dense layer, else the routed experts' and the shared expert's together) become
y - s r (r . y) before they are added to the residual stream. The use it is written for is abliteration (r a refusal
direction); the pack is not changed. The direction comes from a `llama-cvector-generator` file (`direction.N`, pick N
with `--ablate-layer`); an abliteration adapter is DeepSeek's only and is refused here. It runs on the layer split:
`--tp` with `--ablate` loads the layer split instead (a NOTE). Without `--ablate` nothing runs; `--ablate-layer` or
`--ablate-scale` without it is an error. A refusal direction can need `--ablate-scale` above 1 on this model: 1.5
for the one fitted on Maya-XS. With thinking on (24 harmful prompts, 4096 tokens) it refused none at 1.5, but 4
ended at the token cap with no answer, against 22 refusals and 2 with no answer for the stock model.

| Option | Default | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| `--ablate FILE` | off | a control vector (GGUF) whose direction is projected out (changes the answers; without it nothing is loaded or run) |
| `--ablate-layer N` (with `--ablate`) | the file's only `direction.N` | which `direction.N` of the control vector (1-4096) |
| `--ablate-scale F` (with `--ablate`) | 1 | s, 1e-6 to 1e6; 1 removes the component along r, above 1 over-projects |

Setup and debugging variables:

| Variable | Default | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| `MAYA_MM3_HOST_FREE` | `1` | decode: a layer whose routed experts are all in VRAM plans them on the GPU, so a token or a verify window runs without waiting for the host at each layer; `0`: the host plans every layer (the same tokens) |
| `MAYA_MM3_PIPE` | `1` | `--parallel` on a layer split: the GPUs as a pipeline (each holds a different group of the running conversations, the same tokens); `0`: all of them through the GPUs together, one GPU at a time |
| `MAYA_MM3_GRAPH` | `1` | decode on one GPU with every MoE layer host-free: a token's and a verify window's forward replayed as one graph (the same tokens); `0`: launched kernel by kernel |
| `MAYA_MM3_WIN_SUBMIT` | `1` | Windows + AMD: each layer's queued work started as it is enqueued (the driver otherwise waits for the host); `0`: off |
| `MAYA_MM3_ATTN_WIDE`, `MAYA_MM3_MSA_WIDE` | `1` | the sparse attention's decode kernel with more threads, the indexer's prompt scores in register tiles - each the same results as before; `0`: the earlier kernels |
| `MAYA_MM3_LT_STORE` | the pack's `lt-picks.txt` | where hipBLASLt's measured kernels are kept |
| `MAYA_MM3_LANE_FREE` | `2` | without `--cpu-threads`, on a CPU without SMT siblings free (a VM's vCPUs, a CPU without SMT): the cores the CPU lane leaves to the engine's other threads and the OS; `0`: every core, as before |
| `MAYA_MM3_PROF` | off | debugging: `1` each forward's GPU time by part (events), `2` the same with the host waiting at each part (AMD), and the DSpark proposals' parts |
| `MAYA_MM3_MIN_CC` | - | testing only: `70` lets the setup take GPUs before RTX 30 / A100 (a V100), with a warning |
| `MAYA_MM3_IQ_Q8K` | `1` | GGUF quants: the routed experts' inputs rounded as the CPU lane rounds them (a scale every 256 values), so an expert gives the same sums on the GPU and the CPU; `0`: a scale every 32 |
| `MM3_TIMING`, `MM3_CONF`, `MM3_LANE_PROF`, `MM3_MISS_STATS`, `MM3_TP_PROF`, `MM3_TP_TRACE`, `MM3_NSYS` | off | debugging: the engine prints timings, the drafts' confidences, the CPU lane's and tensor parallel's profiles |
| `MM3_PROMOTE` | - | debugging: the expert cache's promotion rule with `--tp` (`threshold,pending,decay`) |
| `MM3_STRESS`, `MM3_STRESS_T` | off | debugging: a drafting stress run |
| `MM3_DUMP_KEPT`, `MM3_DUMP_HS` | off | debugging: write the sparse attention's kept blocks / hidden states to files |
| `MM3_TOPK` | the model's | debugging only: routes each token to this many experts - the answers are no longer the model's |

## DeepSeek: routed prompt experts on AMD

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MAYA_DS4_EXPERT_MMQ` | unset (off) | `1`: use the existing GLM q8_1/int8 MMQ path for supported DeepSeek IQ expert prompt sets of at least 32 rows; other sets keep the existing path. HIP only. |
| `MAYA_DS4_PREFILL_FUSED` | on for gfx1151 (Strix / Gorgon Halo) | the prompt sets through GLM's fused int8 experts (`MAYA_GLM_PREFILL_FUSED`'s kernels at DeepSeek-V4.1's 5120 / 2304 shape, with IQ1_M gate / up and down and IQ2_XXS / IQ2_XS downs added): the weights decoded to int8 in the WMMA tiles' shared memory, the activations and the SwiGLU's H rounded to int8 per 32 values, the products on the int8 matrix cores - where it covers both formats of a layer's experts (IQ1_M, IQ2_XXS, IQ2_XS, IQ2_S, IQ3_XXS, IQ3_S, IQ4_XS gate / ups), else the existing path. No VRAM of its own: the int8 rows sit in the set's down output and H in its FP16 rows until the down writes them. Radeon 8060S, the GSQ-RCO IQ2_XXS GGUF (IQ1_M / IQ2_XXS / IQ2_XS experts): `--bench` prompts 234 -> 257 tok/s (2K), 430 -> 468 (8K), decode unchanged; perplexity over 7,699 tokens of these docs 8.4907 -> 8.4937. It changes answers slightly (the int8 activations). `0` = off; `1` = on for gfx1100 and RDNA4 too (not measured). Takes the sets it covers before `MAYA_DS4_EXPERT_MMQ` |

The switch quantizes both the prompt activations and the FP16 SwiGLU intermediate to q8_1.
This approximate prompt path can change answers; it is off by default. It uses a separate workspace for each
host thread, device and stream, and keeps the existing path during graph capture.

## DeepSeek: the indexer's scores on the matrix cores

The sparse attention's indexer scores every earlier position of the context for each new row (prompt and answer).
On RDNA 3 / 3.5 (`gfx11`: RX 7000, Radeon 780M, Strix / Gorgon Halo's 8060S / 8065S) these scores run on the WMMAs by
default: the same exact products, summed in another order than the portable kernel, so the last bits differ and a
long greedy answer can part from the other kernel's after a while; the quality is the same (a Gorgon Halo, DeepSeek EXL3
2.25: KL to the full-precision model 0.1649 against 0.1661, the same top token 91.08% against 91.07%) and long
prompts read faster (+3% at 8K, +6% at 32K, +10% at 64K; answers the same speed). RDNA 4 (`gfx12`) and other AMD
cards keep the portable kernel. On NVIDIA it stays opt-in.

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `DS4_INDEX_TC` | on for AMD `gfx11`; off on NVIDIA | `0`: the portable kernel on AMD too (the log says so). `1` on NVIDIA (compute capability 8.0+): the tensor cores' kernel (int8 MMAs) |

## Qwen-3.8-Flash-Next (CYBER-FROST)

`./maya.sh --setup --model cyber-frost` sets up Qwen-3.8-Flash-Next (CYBER-FROST): peasantsmith's GGUF of Blackfrost-AI's
CYBER-FROST-3.8 (a fine-tune of
Qwen3.8-Flash-Next: 512 experts, 10 a token, GatedDeltaNet and sparse-attention layers, an n-gram table, an MTP block;
the Qwen Community License 1.0, `LICENSE.qwen-community-1.0`). One 112.5 GB file, downloaded from the commit
`tools/qwen/download.json` pins and checked against its sha256. It runs on the `maya` engine's own Qwen path, from the
file in place: the setup writes `maya-cyber-frost.json` (`maya-cyber-frost-hip.json` on AMD), the pack (the index of
the file's tensors, its small float weights and the tokenizer with the model's chat template) and the MTP drafter, built
from the file's NextN block (`tools/mtp_from_gguf.py`, in `<model folder>/pack/mtp`). Pictures: the fine-tune kept the base model's vision
tower byte for byte, so the setup downloads the base model's mmproj (0.9 GB, pinned and sha256-checked, into
`<model folder>/vision`) and writes the config's `"vision"` entry and the engine's `--vision`; the image encoder
(`build-vision/bin/maya-vision`) starts only when a picture arrives, on the first GPU: in VRAM that is free, and where
that is short the engine gives back as much of its expert cache as the encoder needs (all of it on a small card, and
then the draft layer's experts too) while the picture is read, then maps it again and refills the same experts (a
second or so per request with new pictures; text answers and the decode speed stay as they were; NVIDIA, the HIP build
lends only free VRAM). Only when the GPU cannot spare that much does the encoder run on the
CPU, and the engine's log says why. `--no-vision`, or Images off on the Models page, reads text only.

**Where the experts live.** The most-used experts go to VRAM (`--expert-cache auto`: the free VRAM less the reserve).
When this PC's RAM cannot hold all the others, the engine reads them straight from the GGUF: the next-most-used are
copied into RAM, as many as the available RAM allows less 12% of it (at least 6 GB) for the system and the page cache,
and the rest are read from the file when they are routed (from the page cache, or the SSD). The 52 GB n-gram table is
always read from the SSD row by row and never enters RAM. With about 84 GB of VRAM and RAM together every expert stays
in memory.

**Defaults from the model card**: temperature 1.0, top-p 0.95, top-k 20 (the GGUF's `general.sampling`). Its template
always thinks; its reasoning effort is the thinking level:

| `reasoning_effort` | The prompt |
|---|---|
| `xhigh` (default; the server's `max`) | the template's default: a system note asks for careful reasoning |
| `medium` (the server's `high`) | no note |
| `low` | a note asks for brief thinking |
| `none` | the think block closed at once (`<think>\n\n</think>`, as its Qwen base writes thinking off) |

The prompt is the GGUF's own template, verbatim (`<pack>/tokenizer/chat_template.jinja`): it always adds a fixed
system prompt of the model's makers ahead of any system message a request sends, and thinking is always on in
it. Tool calls are Qwen's XML form. A
thinking budget (`"thinking_budget"` in the config, 32768 by default; 8192 for GLM-5.3-Flash Maya-XS; Settings > Chat defaults > Thinking budget changes it without a restart) closes the think block when it is reached.

**The context.** 32K to 256K (the model's reach). Only the 12 attention layers keep a KV cache, about 14 KB a token in
INT8 (26 KB in FP16), in VRAM; the setup recommends the largest context whose cache takes at most ~12% of the VRAM.

**Drafting.** The MTP drafter proposes up to three tokens a window; the model checks them, so the answer is the same.
The setup measures whether it is faster on this PC (it is, by far, where most experts run on the GPU and on code; less
where most run on the CPU) and keeps it only then; `./maya.sh --speed-calibration` measures again.

Engine options, in the config's `"args"`:

| Option | Default | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| `--max-context` | the setup's choice | the context, in tokens |
| `--expert-cache` | `auto` | experts in VRAM: `auto` takes the free VRAM less the reserve; a number caps it |
| `--vram-reserve-mib` | 700 | VRAM left free for the driver, the desktop and other programs |
| `--expert-profile` | `<pack>/expert-profile.bin` | the ranking of every (layer, expert) pair: which start in VRAM, then RAM |
| `--experts-from-gguf` | `auto` | `auto`: the experts from the GGUF (RAM tier, then the file) when the RAM cannot hold them all, else all in RAM; `on` / `off` force it |
| `--mtp` | `<pack>/mtp` | the MTP drafter's folder - always loaded (the engine's server needs it) |
| `--mtp-max-t` | the setup's measurement | `1`: the drafter's drafts off (windows of one token, prompt-lookup drafts only) - where drafting does not measure faster; unset: drafts up to `--spec` |
| `--spec` | 4 | the decode window's tokens (the drafts plus one) |
| `--prefill` | `auto` | the prompt's chunk: `auto` takes the largest the expert cache can lend |
| `--kv` | `int8` (the setup) | the KV cache's format: `int8` (the model card's own llama.cpp line runs q8_0 K / V; the attention takes as long as in FP16, with half the VRAM), `fp16` (exact; the engine's default without the flag), `k8v4` (INT8 K, rotated 4-bit V) or `q4_0` |

| Variable | Default | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| `MAYA_EXPERT_RAM_GB` | unset (what the RAM allows) | caps the experts' RAM tier, in GB (the dashboard's Models page: Experts in RAM) |
| `MAYA_EXPERT_STAGERS` | Linux: the reads at once the model's drive serves fastest (2, 4, 8 or 16, measured in ~1 s at the first start on a drive and kept in `~/.config/project-maya/drive-reads.txt`; `MAYA_EXPERT_DRIVE_PROBE=0` skips the measurement); otherwise a quarter of the CPU threads, 2 to 8 | the threads that read a layer's experts from the file while the CPU prepares the layer (their reads overlap); `0`: read one by one when the CPU needs them |

## HIP EXL3 single-row matrix jobs

HIP automatically uses a smaller EXL3 matrix-vector kernel when every job in a batch has one activation row.
It keeps workspace for one row instead of eight. A batch containing any multi-row job keeps the generic kernel.
No setting is needed. Set `DS4_EXL3_MV1=0` in the engine configuration's `env` and restart to force the generic
path; unset it or use `1` to restore automatic single-row selection. Use this exact spelling for the kernel switch.

The input-split planner stays unchanged, preserving the order of partial sums. This does not expand weights, reserve
another expert cache, or change model quantization. It affects native EXL3 matrix jobs; GGUF jobs use their own path.

Validation command after building `exl3_test` on an idle GPU:

```sh
ctest --test-dir build-hip -R '^(exl3_test|exl3_mv1_test|exl3_mv_generic_test)$' --output-on-failure
```

The default, explicit-enable and opt-out tests run the existing CPU-reference and exact multi-row checks, plus mixed one-/three-row batches with
strided inputs and guarded outputs. It includes integer EXL3 bitrates with all three codebooks and the supported
half-integer bitrates. A controlled DS4 native-driver screen on three R9700s, based on v1.1.27, measured 3.4-5.0% higher generation
throughput with draft proposals disabled, and about 0.7% with MTP. Expert placement was frozen and adjacent on/off
requests shared one loaded model; the drafter stayed loaded in both modes. A separate fresh-load comparison was
inconclusive because the controls drifted. These are limited model measurements, not production API acceptance or
a general speed guarantee. Full-vocabulary diagnostic dumps matched across settings and repeats on the tested
states; other GPU architectures and broader workloads remain unvalidated.

## AMD: setting up with a ROCm older than 7

The HIP setup takes ROCm 7 or newer. A distribution's own ROCm 6.x packages (Debian's 6.4 under `/usr`, hipBLAS 2.x) are
untested and refused unless this is set when the setup or `--check` runs (in the shell, not the config's `"env"`); see
[AMD_MAYA.md](AMD_MAYA.md). With ROCm 7 the setting does nothing.

| Variable | Default | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| `MAYA_HIP_ALLOW_ROCM6` | unset (ROCm 6 refused) | `1`: the setup goes on with a ROCm 6.x, with a warning (untested: the build or the engine may fail); the build then takes hipBLAS 2's `HIPBLAS_V2` API and leaves out `hipblasSetWorkspace`, which hipBLAS 2 does not have |


# Setting up Maya

The setup in detail: what its first run does, starting again and updating, its options, and what to do if the build
fails. Back to the
[README](../README.md).

## What the first run does

It first asks which model to set up:

- **GLM-5.3-Flash** - Project Maya's GGUF quants (or turboderp's EXL3 quants), from one GPU up; the steps below;
- **DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017** - [LibertAIDAI's EXL3 quant at 2.9 bits](https://huggingface.co/LibertAIDAI/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-EXL3-2.9bpw)
  (211 GB) or [at 3.5 bits](https://huggingface.co/LibertAIDAI/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-EXL3-3.5bpw) (258 GB, about
  280 GB of VRAM and RAM; `--ds4-quant exl3-3.5`, its own folder; a second quant hard-links the Engram tables from the
  first instead of downloading them) and its two Engram tables from [DeepSeek's repo](https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017)
  (203 GB), on its own engine (`build/maya-ds4`): Linux, NVIDIA V100 or newer (pictures: RTX 30 / A100 or newer), and AMD. It is fast with about 230 GB
  of VRAM and RAM together; with less, the setup warns and the engine reads the experts that do not fit from the
  model's files on the SSD (`--mmap-experts auto`), much slower - one RTX 3090 with 64 GB of RAM: prompts about 50
  tokens/s, answers 4-5 tokens/s (faster with `--dspark none`). The same steps with these differences: the
  contexts are 32K-1M (256K recommended where the PC's VRAM + RAM hold the model's files, else 64K; 512K and 1M
  take VRAM from the expert cache, and a prompt that long reads
  slowly - the indexer's work grows with the square of its length); step 5 shows both
  sources and checks every file against its pinned size (and, once downloaded, its sha256: `tools/ds4/download.json`, `download-3.5bpw.json`);
  `--model-dir DIR` uses files you already have; the pack is `DIR/maya-ds4/` (`tools/ds4/pack.py`, about a minute);
  pictures need nothing more - the model's own vision tower runs in the engine; the config is `maya-ds4.json`, with
  tensor parallel across pairs of equal GPUs (`--tp 2`). The engine balances its own layer split; `./maya.sh --calibrate`
  (not offered by the setup) measures each `--tp` that groups your GPUs differently and fewer CPU threads, an engine
  start each (about an hour on 9 GPUs), and keeps the fastest answers whose long prompts still read at 60% of the
  config's own `--tp`'s speed; a setting the engine does not start with is left out (`tools/calibrate_ds4_tp.py`; the
  result goes into `maya-ds4.json` and a setup again keeps it).

  Or from [unsloth's GGUFs](https://huggingface.co/unsloth/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-GGUF) (`--ds4-quant`; the setup asks):
  **UD-Q8_K_XL** (508 GB, the default: dense weights BF16, routed experts MXFP4, the Engram tables FP8 - DeepSeek's own
  formats); its **UD-Q4_K_XL** (501 GB: dense weights Q8_0, less VRAM) is no longer in the setup's list - `--ds4-quant
  gguf-q4` and an install of it work as before. The same engine reads them in place (the pack is
  `tools/ds4/pack_gguf.py`'s), from NVIDIA Volta (V100) on: the most-used experts in VRAM, the others read from the
  GGUF through the OS page cache - from RAM where they fit, from the SSD otherwise - so it runs whatever the memory
  (more VRAM and RAM is faster; keep the files on an NVMe SSD; `--mmap-experts off` copies as many of them into RAM as
  fit instead, on huge pages split across the CPU sockets' memory - measured no faster than the page cache). Prompts
  go through in chunks of 8192 tokens, also where tensor parallel falls back to one GPU per layer group (the log says
  `tensor parallel 2 -> 1`, as with the Maya quants) - before v1.1.17 those ran every prompt in windows of 16 tokens,
  an order of magnitude slower on long prompts (a community measurement, 7x RTX 3090, Maya-S read in place: 32K tokens
  99 tokens/s in windows, 1010 in chunks). The GGUFs have no DSpark drafter and no vision tower.
- **MiniMax-M3.1-Flash** (`--model MiniMax-M3.1-Flash`) - [Mia-AiLab's EXL3 quant at 3.5 bits](https://huggingface.co/Mia-AiLab/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-EXL3-EAQ-v1-3.5-bpw)
  (197 GB with MiniMax's DSpark drafter; every file pinned and checked, `tools/mm3/download.json`) on its own engine
  (`build/maya-mm3`): NVIDIA from V100 on, Linux and Windows, and AMD (as GLM's EXL3 quants); Maya's GGUF quants
  (`--model minimax-maya-xs` / `minimax-maya-s`: [SETTINGS.md](SETTINGS.md#minimax-m31-flash)) run on both too.
  About 250 GB of VRAM
  and RAM hold all of it; with less the setup warns. The same steps with these differences: the contexts are 32K-1M,
  the recommended one the largest (up to 256K) whose KV cache fits beside the model in this PC's VRAM + RAM, its RAM
  said, with warnings past 256K and when the cache would take the experts' RAM; the pack is `DIR/maya-mm3/`
  (`tools/mm3/pack.py`, Maya's own copy of MiniMax's chat template inside it); pictures are on - the model's vision
  tower runs in the engine (`--no-vision`: off); the config is `maya-mm3.json`, with tensor parallel in groups of 4 or 2
  where the GPUs make whole groups, KV streaming on, MiniMax's sampling (temperature 1.0, top-p 0.95) and thinking level
  Auto; then the DSpark drafter is measured on this PC (`--drafting` chooses without measuring), as DeepSeek's. Its
  settings: [SETTINGS.md](SETTINGS.md#minimax-m31-flash).

  Or orcarouter's [abliterated model](https://huggingface.co/orcarouter/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-abliterated-GGUF)
  (DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 with its refusal direction taken out of the weights), in two versions:
  **Mix-128G** (`--ds4-quant abliterated-128g`; experts ~1.6 bits, ~114 GB of VRAM + RAM) and **Mix-256G**
  (`--ds4-quant abliterated-256g`; experts 2.5-4.25 bits by layer - IQ2_S / IQ3_XXS / IQ3_S / MXFP4 - ~236 GB of
  VRAM + RAM), in `<models>/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-abliterated-GGUF/<version>`; `--ds4-quant abliterated` picks the
  one your PC holds. Their n-gram table (shard 2, not abliterated) is not downloaded: both read Maya's Q6_K tables
  (161 GB, in `Engram-Q6_K` beside them - linked from a Maya DeepSeek quant on the same drive, else downloaded once).
  Its repo is gated: accept its terms on Hugging Face, then set `HF_TOKEN` or run `hf auth login` - the download
  sends that token (curl reads it from a file only you can read). It is served as `deepseek-v4.1-flash-abliterated`,
  with DeepSeek's DSpark drafter and vision tower as for unsloth's GGUFs (the drafter only proposes tokens - the model
  checks each one).

GLM-5.3-Flash's first run takes 20-40 minutes plus the download:

1. checks the PC (GPUs, driver, CUDA toolkit, compiler, RAM, CPU);
2. asks which GPUs to use (all of them by default, up to 16), how much context (32K recommended), which model to
   download: GLM-5.3 · Maya-S (recommended; Maya-S24 on cards of 24 GB or less), Maya-M, Maya-M-Derisked or Maya-L, and how it
   drafts: the model's MTP block (the default) or a [DFlash2 drafter](SETTINGS.md#drafting-with-dflash2) (NVIDIA RTX
   30 / A100 and newer, and one AMD Ryzen AI Max APU);
3. installs its Python packages into `.venv` and gets llama.cpp's source at a pinned commit (it lists both and asks);
4. compiles the engine for your GPU(s) (10-30 minutes, once);
5. **the model**: it shows the source, the size (Maya-S: 96.5 GB) and the exact `curl` commands, and downloads only
   when you answer `y`; every file is checked against its published sha256. You can run the commands yourself
   instead, or use files you already have: `./maya.sh --gguf-dir DIR` - other quants than these four are
   experimental: they run, but Maya is not measured with them. With DFlash2 it also downloads the drafter (0.7 GB,
   checked against its sha256) next to the models;
6. builds the *pack* - the engine's index of the model files, about 1 GB, written into the model folder. A Maya
   model's files that were already there are checked against their sha256 first (once each); the pack records the
   files, and a start refuses it if they change afterwards (`--repack` builds it again);
7. **pictures**: compiles the image encoder (10-20 minutes, once) and fetches its files (1.1 GB, shown and asked
   first); `--no-vision` skips it;
8. writes `maya-<model>.json` and `run-maya-<model>.sh`; with DFlash2 it runs the
   [speed calibration](SETTINGS.md#the-speed-calibration) (5-20 minutes: DFlash2 stays only if it is faster on your
   PC); then the settings [tuned for this PC](SETTINGS.md) earlier, or it offers the tuning (about 10-15 minutes;
   `./maya.sh --calibrate` any time) - and starts the dashboard.

> **The start takes a few minutes**: the engine pins most of the free RAM (all but about 6 GB) for its expert tier
> and warms its caches. Other programs get little RAM while Maya runs.

**Linux: systemd-oomd.** Stock Ubuntu 22.04 and newer run `systemd-oomd`, which ends a desktop session's whole
terminal (Maya, and the setup's own tuning with it) when the session's memory pressure stays over 50% for 20 seconds.
Loading a large RAM tier - about 200 GB of DeepSeek experts - reclaims the page cache for minutes and can cross that,
with RAM still free and nothing in `dmesg`; only the journal says `systemd-oomd killed ... process(es)` (issue #497).
`--check` and the setup warn when it runs. Stop it for the run with `sudo systemctl stop systemd-oomd` (it starts again
at the next boot), or exempt your user sessions for good with a drop-in, then log out and in:

```bash
sudo mkdir -p /etc/systemd/system/user@.service.d
printf '[Service]\nManagedOOMMemoryPressure=auto\n' | sudo tee /etc/systemd/system/user@.service.d/90-maya-oomd.conf
sudo systemctl daemon-reload
```

## RDNA4 EXL3 prefill

On an R9700 / RX 9070-family GPU, grouped EXL3 prompt processing can use the
RDNA4 matrix kernels, with 16-row tiles (opt-in for now: `EXL3_WMMA_GFX12=1`). [Guided setup](EXL3_RDNA4.md#guided-setup) shows how to save the
settings through `--env` or an existing configuration, verify the build and
tests, and switch back for comparison. This applies to EXL3 expert weights,
not GGUF, and floating-point results can differ from the portable path.

## Next time, and updating

**Next time**, just run `./setup.sh` again (or the model's `./run-maya-<model>.sh`): it starts right away, nothing is
downloaded twice, on the same screen - its dashboard shows the Monitor's numbers (the speed, where the experts live, the
GPUs, the requests), Tab its log. Ctrl+C stops it - and so does any other way of ending `maya.py`: `kill`, a
service manager, closing the terminal or the window, even a crash: the server and the engine end with it (the
engine keeps its conversation where there is time for that).

**Updating:** the dashboard's **About** says when a new version is out, and its **Update** button does it: Maya
downloads the new code (git), compiles only the engine files that changed and loads the same model again - the model
is not downloaded again, and your settings stay. By hand: `git pull`, then `./setup.sh`.
(The check asks GitHub for the latest release, at most every six hours; `MAYA_UPDATE_CHECK=0` turns it off.)

## Running Maya as a systemd service

Set the model up and start it by hand once first, then let a unit start it at boot. The unit runs the config the setup
wrote (`maya-<model>.json`, in the Maya folder):

```ini
[Unit]
Description=Maya
After=network-online.target
Wants=network-online.target

[Service]
User=you
WorkingDirectory=/path/to/maya
ExecStart=/path/to/maya/maya.sh --config /path/to/maya/maya-<model>.json
Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=30
TimeoutStartSec=3600
TimeoutStopSec=600

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
```

- **Time to start and stop.** A big model at a long context takes a long time to load (the engine pins its expert tier
  in RAM and warms its caches), and the more context you give it (1M tokens, say), the longer. systemd ends a unit that
  is not up inside `TimeoutStartSec` and starts it again, which then loads again, for ever. Set it well above the
  slowest start you have seen by hand (`TimeoutStartSec=infinity` turns it off). `TimeoutStopSec` is the time Maya has
  to save its conversations and free the GPUs after the stop signal; give a big model several minutes.
- **Run the calibration once, by hand, first.** A start measures nothing when nobody is there to wait (a service, a
  pipe, `--yes`): it prints one line naming what it skipped and starts with the engine's defaults. Run the
  calibrations yourself in a terminal while the service is stopped (`./maya.sh --config maya-<model>.json
  --speed-calibration --no-start`, and for MiniMax `--calibrate --no-start` too). They load the model a few times and
  keep the answer in the config, so the service starts straight from it afterwards. A terminal start of the same config
  still measures what is missing, as before.
- **Logs.** The server's output (the same lines a terminal shows) goes to the journal: `journalctl -u maya -f`. The
  engine writes its own log to the file named by `"log"` in the config, `maya-<model>.log` beside it by default.
- The model needs the whole machine: stop the service before a calibration, a setup or `--check`, and do not start two
  units on the same GPUs.

## Options

| Option | |
| --- | --- |
| `--setup` | set up again (other GPUs, context, model folder) |
| `--check` | only check the PC |
| `--gguf-dir DIR` | use GLM-5.3-Flash GGUF files you already have: their folder, or the `.gguf` file (the first one of a split model) when the folder holds several models. The folder must be writable: the pack goes inside it. A GGUF Maya does not list (a pruned model such as RCO-152E included: the shipped expert profile is for 288 experts a layer and is left out of any other count) gets its own sampling (`general.sampling.*`) and chat template from the file, else GLM's |
| `--models-dir DIR` | where downloaded models go, each in a folder named after it - `DIR/Maya-L/` holds that model's GGUF files (default `../Maya-data/models`); put it on the NVMe |
| `--import-model FOLDER` | use model files you downloaded some other way (a download manager, a copy from another PC, a Hugging Face cache): finds Maya's published files in FOLDER and its subfolders by size and sha256 - their names do not matter - and moves them into `--models-dir`'s layout (same drive) or copies them (another drive, a cache link: `--copy`; the originals stay). Prints what was found, what is missing and what did not match; files that did not match are never touched, and a file already in place is never replaced. Moved files are recorded as verified |
| `--copy` | with `--import-model`: copy the files that cannot be moved |
| `--download-model` | download the model without asking (the commands and size are still printed) |
| `--model NAME` | which model and files, without asking. Maya's quants by their setup id - the model first, then the tier (XS, S, M, L by the experts' bits): `glm-maya-s`, `glm-maya-s24`, `glm-maya-m`, `glm-maya-m-derisked`, `glm-maya-l`, `deepseek-maya-xs`, `deepseek-maya-s`, `deepseek-maya-m`, `minimax-maya-xs`, `minimax-maya-s` (or the full name, e.g. `MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-XS`). Or a model, its files asked: `DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017`, `MiniMax-M3.1-Flash`. The names used before still work: `Maya-S-v2-IQ2_XXS` (GLM-5.3 · Maya-S), `Maya-S24`, `Maya-M`, `Maya-M-Derisked`, `Maya-L`, and `EXL3-3.05bpw` / `EXL3-4.05bpw` / `EXL3-sc_4.10bpw` - turboderp's EXL3 quant of GLM-5.3-Flash at 3.05 or 4.05 bits per weight, or the self-calibrated one at 4.10 with 8-bit attention and shared experts ([3.05bpw](https://huggingface.co/turboderp/GLM-5.3-Flash-exl3/tree/3.05bpw), 125.3 GB; [4.05bpw](https://huggingface.co/turboderp/GLM-5.3-Flash-exl3/tree/4.05bpw), 165.2 GB; [sc_4.10bpw](https://huggingface.co/turboderp/GLM-5.3-Flash-exl3/tree/sc_4.10bpw), 167.8 GB; all with the MTP block) that the engine reads in place from its safetensors: NVIDIA from V100, and AMD; none measured against the FP8 model (`EXL3-sc_4.10bpw` is not in the setup's list: named by hand, or set up before, it works as before) |
| `--model-dir DIR` | use DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017's files you already have: the folder with a GGUF's shards, or with the EXL3 quant's files and the Engram shards `model-00047-of-00048.safetensors` / `model-00048-of-00048.safetensors` of DeepSeek's repo (it must be writable: the pack goes inside it). Or an EXL3 folder of GLM-5.3-Flash or MiniMax-M3.1-Flash at any bit rate that is not one of the listed downloads (turboderp's other branches, Mia-AiLab's other bit rates): the setup reads the folder's own `config.json` and `model.safetensors.index.json`, checks each file whole by its header, never offers the listed downloads for it, writes a config named after the folder (`maya-<folder>.json`) and uses the folder's own sampling (`generation_config.json`) and chat template (`chat_template.jinja`) - GLM's / MiniMax's where the folder has none. MiniMax: `--model MiniMax-M3.1-Flash --model-dir DIR` is the same |
| `--ds4-quant Q` | DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017's files: `maya-s`, `maya-xs` (Maya's quants, the same as `--model deepseek-maya-s` / `deepseek-maya-xs`; Maya-S the default), `gguf-q8` (unsloth's UD-Q8_K_XL GGUF), `gguf-q4` (its UD-Q4_K_XL: dense weights Q8_0; not in the setup's list, named by hand), `exl3` (LibertAIDAI's EXL3 quant at 2.9 bits), `exl3-3.5` (its 3.5-bit quant: closer to the original, about 280 GB of VRAM and RAM), `abliterated-128g` / `abliterated-256g` (orcarouter's abliterated model as its Mix-128G / Mix-256G GGUF, with Maya's Q6_K n-gram tables; `abliterated`: the one this PC holds; a gated repo: `HF_TOKEN` or `hf auth login`); the EXL3 quants need NVIDIA V100 or newer (pictures: RTX 30 / A100 or newer) and are offered only there, and on AMD (as GLM's EXL3 quants there; [docs/EXL3_HIP.md](EXL3_HIP.md)) |
| `--mm3-quant Q` | MiniMax-M3.1-Flash's files: `exl3` (Mia-AiLab's EXL3 quant at 3.5 bits, NVIDIA), `maya-xs` or `maya-s` (Maya's GGUF quants, the same as `--model minimax-maya-xs` / `minimax-maya-s`; `gguf-xs` / `gguf-s` before) |
| `--no-vision` | text only: no image encoder (DeepSeek, MiniMax: their vision tower off; Qwen-3.8-Flash-Next: no mmproj download) |
| `--draft mtp` / `--draft dflash2` | how the model drafts tokens ahead: its MTP block (the default), or a DFlash2 drafter - downloaded (0.7 GB) into `DIR/GLM-5.3-Flash-DFlash2/` and passed as `--dflash` (Maya-L: +44% on nine GPUs, +12% on two, +19% on one GPU; NVIDIA RTX 30 / A100 and newer, and one AMD Ryzen AI Max APU (Strix / Gorgon Halo) - older cards and other AMD GPUs keep the MTP block unless you pass this; the drafter's license is CC BY-NC-ND 4.0, non-commercial use only). The [speed calibration](SETTINGS.md#the-speed-calibration) then keeps it only if it is faster on your PC |
| `--gpu N` / `--gpus 0,1,2,3` | one GPU, or several (up to 16) that split the model's layers |
| `--context N` | context length in tokens: 8192, 32768 (default), 65536, 131072; DeepSeek: 32768, 65536, 131072, 262144, 524288, 1048576 (the default 262144 where VRAM + RAM hold the model, else 65536); MiniMax: the same, the default by this PC's memory |
| `--vram-reserve-mib N` | VRAM in MiB the expert pool leaves free on each GPU (default 700), kept in the config - more where the desktop or other programs on the GPU need room ([the expert pool's size](SETTINGS.md#the-expert-pools-size)) |
| `--port N`, `--host 0.0.0.0 --api-key KEY` | another port; reachable from your network (always set a key; several: `KEY1,KEY2`) |
| `--env KEY=VALUE` | an engine setting kept in the config (see [Tuning](SETTINGS.md)) |
| `--host-compiler g++-12` | when your g++ is newer than your CUDA accepts ("unsupported GNU version") |
| `--rebuild`, `--repack` | compile the engine / build the pack again |
| `--calibrate` | tune the engine for this PC (see [Tuning](SETTINGS.md)): GLM's CPU lane, DeepSeek's `--tp` and CPU threads, MiniMax's [prefill calibration](SETTINGS.md#minimax-m31-flash) (the chunk and the matrix products' library a prompt is read with); then start; with `--no-start` only tune |
| `--speed-calibration` | measure again whether the drafter (DFlash2, or DeepSeek's or MiniMax's DSpark) decodes faster on this PC ([how](SETTINGS.md#the-speed-calibration); 5-30 minutes, with Maya stopped) and keep the faster, then start; with `--no-start` only measure |
| `--drafting measure\|on\|off` | the drafter without the question: `measure` = the speed calibration (the default with `--yes`), `on` = draft with it, `off` = no drafting - both skip the measuring |
| `--no-speed-calibration` | the same as `--drafting on` |
| `--yes` | the recommended answers (the model download still needs `--download-model`) |
| `--plain` | the setup in plain text with typed answers, and Maya in the terminal, not on their own screen (a pipe gets this too, and `--yes` a plain setup); the setup screen's whole output is in `maya-setup.log` |

## Web pages on another address

A browser stops a web page served from another address (a local chat client on `http://127.0.0.1:8090`, say) from
calling Maya's API unless Maya allows that page. List the pages in the config, `maya-<model>.json`:

```json
"cors_origins": ["http://127.0.0.1:8090"]
```

Exact addresses (scheme, host and port, no path); `["*"]` lets every page in - set an `api_key` with it, or any page
you open could use your model. It opens `/v1/*` and nothing else: the settings and the tools stay for Maya's own page.
Without it nothing changes. A new `--setup` writes the config again: add the line back after it.

## Giving the GPU back when idle

To share the PC with other GPU programs (another model server, games), let Maya unload the model after a while without
requests. In the config, `maya-<model>.json`:

```json
"idle_unload_s": 900,
"env": {"STRATA_GLM_SLOT_KEEP": "1"}
```

(`--idle-unload 900` on the server's command line does the same.) After that many seconds with no request running or
waiting, the engine ends: its GPU memory and its pinned RAM tier are given back (an RTX 3090 with Maya-S24: 22.9 GB of
VRAM and 79 GB of RAM). The dashboard and `/health` say `unloaded`, and `/v1/models` still lists the model with the
status `unloaded`. The next request loads the model again - about a minute before its first token - and the experts
in VRAM warm up again over the next answers. With `STRATA_GLM_SLOT_KEEP=1` the conversation in the model is written to
the disk on the way out and read back, so the next turn of the same chat reads only its new tokens (1,630 of 1,690
prompt tokens reused after an unload). `0` (the default): never. A new `--setup` writes the config again: add the
lines back after it.

## If the build fails

- **"nvcc ... cannot build for these GPUs":** Volta needs CUDA 12.x, Blackwell 12.8 or newer. The setup takes the
  newest installed toolkit that fits.
- **"unsupported GNU version":** `./maya.sh --setup --host-compiler g++-12` (install `g++-12` first).
- **`cuda_profiler_api.h` or `nvtx3/nvToolsExt.h` is missing:** Maya's CUDA build needs the profiler API and NVTX
  headers as well as `nvcc` and the runtime. Install those development components for your toolkit. With a split
  CUDA 13 toolkit, NVIDIA's official header wheels can also stay in a project-local folder:

  ```sh
  python3 -m pip install --target .cuda-headers \
    nvidia-cuda-profiler-api==13.3.27 nvidia-nvtx==13.3.29
  CPATH="$PWD/.cuda-headers/nvidia/cu13/include${CPATH:+:$CPATH}" ./maya.sh --setup
  ```

  Select wheel versions that match your toolkit. This include layout was checked with CUDA 13.3,
  `nvidia-cuda-profiler-api==13.3.27` and `nvidia-nvtx==13.3.29`; other CUDA major versions can use a different
  layout. Use the same `CPATH` when rebuilding after an update. No shared toolkit files need changing.


# Maya on Windows

Back to the [README](../README.md).

The same installer sets Maya up natively on Windows 10/11 (64-bit), and the engine and the image encoder
compile there with Visual Studio 2022 and CUDA 12.8 (CUDA 13 works for RTX 20 and newer). Maya is developed
and measured on Linux; on Windows 11 a user runs Maya-L on an RTX 4090, built with CUDA 13.4 (the speed table above,
[#63](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/63)). Tell us how it runs on yours.

1. Install once:
   - The NVIDIA driver and [CUDA Toolkit 12.8](https://developer.nvidia.com/cuda-12-8-0-download-archive).
     CUDA 13 also works for RTX 20 and newer; V100 needs CUDA 12.x. The CUDA version shown by `nvidia-smi` is the
     driver's capability, not proof that the toolkit (`nvcc`) is installed.
   - [Visual Studio **2022** Build Tools](https://aka.ms/vs/17/release/vs_BuildTools.exe), with
     **Desktop development with C++**, **MSVC v143 - VS 2022 C++ x64/x86 build tools** and a **Windows 10 or 11 SDK**.
     The full Visual Studio IDE is optional. **VS 2026 Build Tools work too with CUDA 13.2 or newer** - which
     Visual Studio a CUDA release supports is in its installation guide ([13.2 onward list VS 2026's MSVC 195x](
     https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/cuda-installation-guide-microsoft-windows/index.html), 13.1 and every 12.x stop
     at VS 2022); with a CUDA 12.x toolkit, 2026 alone does not satisfy the installer's check;
     2022 can be installed alongside it.
   - **64-bit Python 3.10+**, for example `winget install -e --id Python.Python.3.12 --scope user`.
   - [Git for Windows](https://git-scm.com/download/win) to clone and update the repository, or download and extract
     its source zip instead.
2. Open a **new PowerShell or Command Prompt** after installing the prerequisites. Clone the repository (or open
   the extracted folder), then check the PC:

   ```powershell
   git clone https://github.com/mw00/project-maya.git
   cd project-maya
   .\START-MAYA.bat --check
   ```

   `--check` reports the GPUs, toolkit, compiler, RAM and page file without downloading the model or building the
   engine. Maya finds Visual Studio's compiler environment itself; a Developer Command Prompt is not needed.
3. Start the setup, putting the model on an NVMe SSD with enough [free space](../README.md#what-you-need) for the chosen model.
   Change the drive and folder to suit your PC:

   ```powershell
   .\START-MAYA.bat --setup --models-dir "D:\Maya-models"
   ```

   Choose the model and context when asked. The launcher creates its own `.venv`; setup installs its Python
   packages, CMake and Ninja there, builds the engine, and asks before downloading the model. Later, run
   `.\START-MAYA.bat` (or double-click it) to start the installed model, then open `http://127.0.0.1:8080`.
   All `./maya.sh` options also work here; PowerShell needs the leading `.\`. Add `--plain` for plain-text setup.

- **Check the page-file size, even with "System managed" enabled.** Windows' GPU allocations and Maya's pinned
  RAM tier consume commit capacity (RAM + page file). A small page file can keep the RAM tier below the free RAM
  available, leaving more experts on the SSD. `--check` recommends roughly the selected GPUs' total VRAM plus
  9 GB of page-file capacity (about 57 GB for a 48 GB card). If it warns, set a custom size at least as large as it
  recommends, with enough free disk space: System > About > Advanced system settings > Performance > Settings >
  Advanced > Virtual memory. Restart Windows after changing it. "System managed" can remain too small on a
  large-RAM PC ([#20](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/20)). On an AMD APU (Strix Halo, Gorgon Halo)
  the page file needs to be at least about the GPU's share of the memory: see
  [AMD_MAYA.md](AMD_MAYA.md#windows) ("The page file on a Windows APU").
- **A GPU filled to the brim pages instead of failing.** Windows' driver moves GPU memory to system RAM when the card
  runs out, and decode then falls to a few tokens per second. The expert pool checks the free VRAM again once it is
  written (`--expert-cache auto`, the default), and the engine log says at start how much is free on each GPU
  (`LOW` below 256 MiB). A drafter (DFlash2) takes a few hundred MB more on its first request. If it warns, or
  answers slow down after the first one, add `"--vram-reserve-mib", "1500"` (say) to the config's `"args"`, or set up
  again with `--vram-reserve-mib 1500` ([the expert pool's size](SETTINGS.md#the-expert-pools-size)).
- With several CUDA toolkits installed, setup chooses the newest compatible one. To select one explicitly, add
  `--nvcc "C:\Program Files\NVIDIA GPU Computing Toolkit\CUDA\v12.8\bin\nvcc.exe"` to the setup command.
- After setup, stop any running Maya server and run `.\START-MAYA.bat --calibrate --no-start` to tune the CPU/PCIe
  split and thread count for your PC. Then start Maya normally. See [Tuning](SETTINGS.md) for the optional RAM-resident
  tier and other settings, and [Something went wrong?](../README.md#something-went-wrong) for `--bench` / `--report`.
- Not WSL2: WSL2's GPU driver pins only about 1 GB of RAM, and Maya pins tens of GB. Run
  `START-MAYA.bat` in Windows itself.
- A Tesla V100 runs it too, built with CUDA 12.4 ([#57](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/57)). Run
  `--calibrate` once: it tunes Maya for your PC, and a setup for another context keeps its settings.
- AMD on Windows, Strix Halo / Gorgon Halo included: `START-MAYA.bat --backend hip --gpu 0
  --setup` builds the HIP engine with AMD's HIP SDK, or offers AMD's ROCm SDK wheels in `.venv` when there is none
  ([docs/AMD_MAYA.md](AMD_MAYA.md#windows)).
  `tools\hip\build_maya_windows.bat` builds it by hand (an RX 7900 XTX,
  [#54](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/pull/54)).


# DeepSeek-V4.1 prompt format

The server uses DeepSeek's MIT-licensed `encoding/encoding.py` at revision
`1bae0e1088993b9b82de08f59de5be5a34e42170` of
[deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017](https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017/tree/1bae0e1088993b9b82de08f59de5be5a34e42170/encoding).
The source is vendored as `serve/ds41_encoding.py`, without functional changes;
its copyright and permission notice are in `LICENSE.deepseek-v41`. Other model families keep their Jinja renderer.

The DeepSeek adapter preserves later system/developer messages, reminders, tasks,
message-local tool declarations, tool call/result IDs and text-block boundaries.
Parallel results render in call order. Integer reasoning budgets 1–100 are accepted;
`low`/`high`/`max` mean 50/75/100, and the existing `medium`/`xhigh` aliases mean
75/100. These are prompt conditioning, not completion token limits. Anthropic
`thinking.budget_tokens` still maps to Maya's existing low/high/max levels.

DSML parameter `string` flags determine argument types. Leading answer newlines are
preserved. OpenAI tool responses use the reference's bare `function.name` and optional
call-level `namespace`; internal routing and Anthropic tool names stay qualified
(`namespace::name`). DSML parameters are buffered until their closing tag before
argument JSON is streamed, so the first argument fragment may arrive later.

`tools/ds4/chat_template.jinja` remains a standalone compatibility export for ordinary
chat and tools. It is **not** the server's renderer and does not implement the full
reference protocol: reminders/tasks, message-local tool schemas, later system roles,
ID-based result sorting and namespaces differ. Matching that preprocessing in a
sandboxed Jinja template would duplicate the reference encoder's merge/sort/image and
thinking policies. External consumers needing byte-for-byte conformance should use
the pinned Python encoder and its input format instead. Existing model packs are not
rewritten by this change.

Offline conformance checks (no weights or GPU):

```sh
python -m unittest serve.test_ds41_conformance -v
```

The five official fixture pairs are in `serve/fixtures/deepseek_v41`, with provenance.
The tests also exercise adapter and streaming parser behavior. Image placeholder
conformance does not by itself validate vision inference; malformed/truncated model
output is handled on a best-effort basis rather than claimed as reference-equivalent.


# DeepSeek serving draft timing

DeepSeek `STAT` lines expose existing DSpark measurements alongside `drafts` (the accepted/offered ratio):

- `draft_rounds`: drafter calls counted per requesting sequence. A batched call serving several sequences counts each sequence, rather than one scheduler step.
- `draft_ms`: cumulative host-measured drafter time, in milliseconds.
- `verify_ms`: cumulative host-measured verification forward time, in milliseconds.

With serial serving, values cover the current request's decode, excluding its prefill. With `--parallel`, they cover the engine's current busy interval across requests; they are not attributed to individual requests. All three reset with their reporting interval and are Prometheus **gauges**, not lifetime counters. The server preserves them in its engine statistics and exports `maya:engine_draft_rounds`, `maya:engine_draft_ms`, and `maya:engine_verify_ms` from `/metrics/prometheus`.

These reuse existing timers without adding device synchronization. They measure the regions already timed by the engine: they are not GPU-only timings, and `draft_ms + verify_ms` is not a complete decode wall-time breakdown. For example, verification's accept/reject sampling is outside its forward timer. Use output tokens and decode wall time for throughput, and these measurements to help interpret drafter cost versus verification work.

The fields do not change proposal selection, sampling, or model arithmetic. Sampled proposals remain controlled by the existing `DS4_DSPARK_SAMPLE=1` setting.


# DeepSeek prefill transfers: only the experts a chunk picks

CUDA: on for chunks of up to `DS4_SKIP_UNPICKED_ROWS` rows (1536) - an agent's turn, a short prompt. HIP: on for every
chunk where the CPU lane's experts are read in place from the model's files (`--mmap-experts on`, or `auto` choosing
it), off otherwise, as before. `DS4_SKIP_UNPICKED=1` takes every chunk, `0` none, on either; `DS4_SKIP_UNPICKED=alt`
(tests, one GPU) turns it off and on by turns, a run each. This affects the normal chunked prefill streamer, not
`DS4_FLOW_TP` or decode. `DS4_FLOW_STATS=1` reports copied/skipped expert counts.

Why by the files on HIP: read in place, a chunk's streams are the disk's reads, and those set its pace, not the wait for
each layer's routing. A Radeon 8065S (Gorgon Halo, Windows), EXL3 2.9 bpw, 11,802 of 15,744 experts in VRAM and the rest
read from the SSD, chunks of 8192: a chunk streamed all 51 GB of those experts while its rows picked about 1,100 of them;
now 5-7 GB. Fresh prompts, median of 2: 2K tokens 53.8 -> 210.0 tok/s, 8K 171.7 -> 330.1. The skip changes no
arithmetic (the parity below); on that PC the streamed path's last-row logits already differ from one pass of a prompt
to the next without it, and with it they differ by the same amount (`DS4_SKIP_UNPICKED=alt`, three passes of one
6,000-token prompt in one process: off/off KL 0.027, off/on 0.023 and 0.032, the same top token).

Why by size on CUDA: a chunk that skips waits for each layer's routing before it copies that layer's experts, where
otherwise the copies run ahead across layers. A short chunk picks few of the CPU lane's experts, so its streams are that
much shorter; a long one picks most, and the waits cost more than the few skipped save. An RTX 3090 + eight RTX 5060 Ti
(PCIe 3.0 x8 each), EXL3 3.5 bpw, two thirds of the experts on the CPU lane, an agent's turns at 118-134K tokens of
context (one chunk each):

| New tokens | Every expert streamed | Only the picked ones |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 530 | 4.6-4.9 s | 3.6-3.8 s |
| 1,040 | 5.0-5.2 s | 4.4-4.9 s |
| 2,050 | 6.0-6.3 s | 6.7 s |
| 117,670 (29 chunks of 4,096) | 1,515 tok/s | 1,282 tok/s |

Parity on that setup: a 2,048-token prompt in one chunk (`--no-expert-cache --window 2048 --dump-logits`), every row's
logits (2,048 x 129,280) the same bit for bit with it and without it.

Wait for each layer/chunk routing result and skip zero-row experts without compacting virtual slots or changing batches, kernels or accumulation order. Transfers still overlap computation within each layer. With explicit RAM copies, stage only the TP member's gate/up rows into existing blob offsets; keep full down matrices for strided DMA. File-backed reads retain their existing layout. Deferring cross-layer prefetch may hurt workloads with many active experts.

## Measured on the earlier base

Base: `abc09c74ba692d808062dfa92d2b7c081831ac03`. Ryzen 9950X, 256 GB RAM, two Radeon AI PRO R9700 32 GB cards (PCIe 5 x8 upstream each), one 16 GB RX 9070-family card behind PCIe 4 x4, ROCm 7.14.1. DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 UD-Q8_K_XL, context 262144, TP2 plus singleton, `DS4_SPLIT=36,4`, `--reserve-mib 2048 --mmap-experts off --dspark none`. Both arms use identical hardware/split. `DS4_FLOW_TP` and `DS4_PROF` off.

Fresh synthetic API prompts, one output token, temperature zero, reasoning none, every response `cache_n=0`:

| Prompt tokens | Baseline seconds | Enabled seconds | Speedup |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1043 A | 42.293 | 17.988 | 2.35x |
| 1043 B | 40.671 | 16.698 | 2.44x |
| 2067 C | 42.167 | 19.558 | 2.16x |

Individual measurements, not confidence intervals or a general performance claim. Routing diversity and cache warmness matter. Other quants, NUMA hardware and CUDA have not been measured.

## Correctness

Original and enabled binaries on that base consumed identical 512 input IDs and separate copies of a frozen initial expert profile, identical weights and settings. CLI: `--ids <comma-separated IDs> --window 512 --n 0 --dump-logits <output.f32>`. All 512 x 129280 float32 logits were bit-identical, maximum absolute difference zero. An independent original repeat was also bit-identical. Copy a frozen profile for each arm; do not reuse the profile modified by the preceding run.

The current-main port still needs a full-model parity/performance rerun before merge. The earlier numbers do not establish parity or performance on the newer upstream engine.

CPU regression coverage: random byte payloads, TP widths 1/2/4, RAM parts 1/2, gate/up and full-down slices, bounds and destination guards. Run:

```sh
g++ -std=c++20 -O2 -DNDEBUG -Wall -Wextra -fsanitize=address,undefined tests/ds4_ram_slice_test.cpp -o /tmp/ds4_ram_slice_test
/tmp/ds4_ram_slice_test
```

Also registered as `ds4_ram_slice_test` with `STRATA_BUILD_TESTS=ON`. Byte-slicing checks do not replace full-model parity.
